### Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposed cross-reference and annexes such as project title, general description, term sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the funding proposal. The information contained within the funding proposal must be reflected in all relevant documents.

The total length of a completed funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 50 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service level agreement timeframe.

The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals shall be made available to the public simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. A.20. Executing Entity information

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The Holistic rangelands approach seeks to address the multiple variable patterns leading to extreme weather events such as floods and droughts; resulting in humanitarian emergencies, food insecurity and infrastructure damage. Therefore, the proposed programme will propose “Building Climate Resilience for Livelihoods and Ecosystem Services (BCRE)” aimed at building resilience against climate change and variability in the Horn of Africa (HoA) region, specifically targeting Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and South Sudan. The BCRE programme is expected to contribute towards the following: i) reduce the vulnerability and increase the resilience of agro pastoral and pastoral communities to climate-related hazards; ii) improve access to information on climate change and variability in the HoA region by providing each beneficiary community with a package of resilience solutions, including infrastructure for weather monitoring and early warning systems (EWS); iii) access to improved agricultural technologies and best practices; iv) access to renewable energy; v) capacity building for cooperatives and associations; vi) access to credit facilities; vii) access to land management; viii) access to innovation; ix) capacity building for cooperatives, and x) infrastructure to generate climate information. GCF funding will complement the required resilience package by providing additional resources to address the identified gaps in the HoA region. The programme is designed and prepared to cope with climate and other environmental hazards. With an estimated budget of USD 310 million, the programme will be implemented over a period of five years (2018-2023) taking into account climate change considerations into agricultural practices. Key constraints to climate adaptation that the programme seeks to address include: i) limited access to information on climate variability and change; ii) infrastructure gaps and limited access to climate information. The outputs of the programme are expected to contribute towards the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): i) SDG 13: Climate action; ii) SDG 2: Zero hunger; iii) SDG 5: Gender equality; iv) SDG 6: Clean water and sanitation; v) SDG 7: Affordable and clean energy; vi) SDG 8: Decent work and economic growth; vii) SDG 10: Reduced inequalities; viii) SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production; ix) SDG 13: Climate action; x) SDG 15: Life on land. The programme will be implemented by host countries Ministries of Agriculture, with regional coordination through the Intergovernmental Authority on Development (IGAD) and its subsidiary bodies.

Co-financer’s contribution means the financial resources required, whether Public Finance or Private Finance, in addition to the GCF contribution, that are necessary for the resources requested by the Accredited Entity to implement the project or programme described in the funding proposal.

# B.1 CLIMATE CONTEXT (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## 1. The Horn of Africa (HoA): One of the most food insecure regions in the world.

The eight countries in the HoA - including Djibouti, Ethiopia, Eritrea, Kenya, Somalia, Sudan, South Sudan and Uganda (Figure B.1) - are home to about 250 million people, of whom 160 million (or about 64 per cent) live in areas where food is extremely scarce.

## 2. In addition, about 80% of the region is classified as arid and semi-arid land (ASAL), with about 40% of the total land area being desert or degraded land. The region suffers from poor climatic conditions coupled with severe environmental degradation, which are exacerbated by adverse socio-economic conditions and human activities.

Since the 1970s, several countries in the HoA have experienced recurring droughts and famine at least once every decade (a frequency that has increased over time). For example, Ethiopia has experienced a famine at least once every decade since the mid-1970s. In Somalia, for example, the Famine Early Warning Systems Network (FEWS NET) predicts that the loss of agricultural livelihoods and food security will increase significantly in 2023 due to expected below-average rainfall, coupled with expected desert locust crop and pasture losses.

The impact on food security is further exacerbated by disruptions to domestic market supply chains and household food and income insecurity. The Food Security and Nutrition Working Group (FSNWG) indicates that between 18-21 million people in Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and Sudan are expected to experience crisis IPC 3 or a worsening levels of acute food insecurity from May to June due to the need of emergency food assistance to meet their basic needs. This is an increase of about 40% compared with the number of people in the three countries by February 2023, due to consecutive poor rains and locust infestation.

Similarly, in Djibouti, an estimated 60 million refugees and returnees in the country are at Crisis IPC 3 or worse and in urgent need of food assistance. The situation is not much different in Ethiopia, where an estimated 8.1 million people are currently in Stressed or Crisis IPC 2 and IPC 3 respectively. This has been exacerbated by consecutive poor rains, locust infestation and the ongoing negative impact of past poor harvests.

In South Sudan, an estimated 6 million people will be at Crisis IPC 3 levels of acute food insecurity at the peak of the lean season from May to June, requiring urgent food

## Climate Vulnerabilities, Impacts, and Adaptation Options

### Climate Change Indicators

5. The historical seasonal cycle of precipitation and temperature (mean minimum and maximum) for stations in the region is shown in Figure B1.1 below. For South Sudan (Wau), Kenya (Dagoretti Station), Somalia (Mogadishu), Djibouti (Djibouti), Uganda (Kabale) and Eritrea (Asmara) (for the period 1981-2019) is presented in Figure B1.1 below. As observed at figure B1.1, stations across the Horn exhibit different precipitation patterns. Some stations such as Wau and Mogadishu exhibit a bimodal precipitation season, while others show trimodal seasons. This corresponds to single temperature peaks during the dry season (May-June) and two temperature peaks in the wet season (September-October). Temperatures vary from about 7°C (min) to 26.3°C (max) at the station. At El Nahud in the southern part of Sudan, temperatures are higher than those recorded at other stations with a minimum monthly rainfall of about 193 mm in May (398c) and October (376c). The observed maximum mean monthly rainfall at this station is also lower compared to other stations. At Wau, the temperature peaks during the wet season are similar in Sudan. Although the temperature at Wau peaks in the same month as El Nahud, the magnitude is lower, and the mean monthly rainfall is higher (about 193 mm/month). Both Dagoretti in Kenya and


---
**Page 13**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

Mogadishu in Somalia experience bimodal rainy seasons with different first peaks in April and May respectively, but the second peak is observed in November. Both stations experience two temperature maxima (about equal magnitude) during March to July, although the amplitude of the mean monthly rainfall is lower (i.e., less than 35 mm/month). This can be explained by the influence of a precipitation area over the western equatorial Indian Ocean, which affects Somalia and northeastern Ethiopia. The same applies for stations in southwestern Uganda, with bimodal rainfall seasons in SAM (March-April) and SON (September-November), while stations in northern Kenya have two maxima during the months of March to May.

## Observed trends in precipitation, minimum and maximum temperature for the period 1976-2005 are shown in Figure B.1.3a. As observed, trends in precipitation range from -9 to 9 mm/month/decade over the entire region. Negative trends were observed in northeastern Ethiopia, southwestern Uganda, northwestern Uganda, northern Ethiopia, all of Djibouti and parts of Somalia. These trends are statistically significant only in the north-eastern area of Ethiopia. On the other hand, negative trends are

**GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 6**

C

observed in most parts of Kenya, especially in the central and southern parts of the country. Declining trends are also observed in the southern parts of Ethiopia and the northern part of Eritrea. Some parts of Somalia and northeastern Kenya have experienced a general warming trend. However, these negative trends are not statistically significant. With regard to temperature, a general nighttime warming trend is observed over the entire HoF, except for a few areas over the northeastern parts of Ethiopia and southern parts of Sudan. The observed precipitation deficit in some parts of the region and the large-scale increase in evaporation rates have led to increased potential evaporation, which in turn can lead to increased water demand and thus intensified drought impacts.

Figure B.13: Observed trends in precipitation, minimum and maximum temperature for the period 1976-2005. Stippling denotes areas where trends are statistically significant at 0.05 level.

**7. Analysis of area-averaged rainfall and temperature over the region based on CRU data for the period 1900-2020 is also shown in Figure B.13. The results show that the region has experienced high interannual variability in both precipitation and temperature, with a general warming trend from the 1970s to the late 2000s characterised by a decreasing rainfall trend, while the last decade (2011-2020) shows an increasing rainfall trend. The region has experienced a consistent regional warming trend over the last forty years (1981 to 2020), and this is one of the key indicators that climate change is affecting the region. Declining trends in precipitation, increases in rainfall and increases in temperature are threatening crop productivity, with farmers experiencing declining yields and some areas becoming less suitable for the production of major crops.

### Climate projections for the period 2021-2050

8. Climate projections for the period 2021–2050 show a general increase in annual mean temperature and precipitation over most of Africa, with some exceptions (Figure B1.4). Under the medium-low emissions scenario, the annual mean near-surface temperature is projected to increase by more than 3°C relative to the baseline period (1976-2005), while under RCP8.5, the difference between the projected period (2021–2050) and the baseline period (1976-2005) is estimated at about 4.5°C. The increase in temperature is expected to be more pronounced over most of the region. Warming under RCP8.5 is projected to be high, reaching up to 2.4°C, as shown in Figure B1.4 (bottom). This warming will have significant impacts on agriculture and water resources during the MAM season is important for most of the region, especially in sectors sensitive to rainfall variability. Therefore, increased temperature and precipitation variability during this season can negatively affect food security and key production sectors due to water scarcity and heat stress.

### Figure B1.4: Projected change in mean temperatures over the Horn of Africa for the period 2021-2050 under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 emission scenarios

The change is computed relative to the baseline (1976–2005). Stippling denotes areas where differences are statistically significant at p ≤ 0.05 level.


### Projected changes in the spatial distribution of precipitation under the RCP4.5 scenario indicate that widespread wet conditions are likely over the southern part of the region. On the other hand, precipitation deficits are projected under RCP8.4 over the whole of Sudan, Eritrea, central and northern


---
**Page 16**
---

parts of southern Sudan, northern and western Ethiopia and southern Uganda. Other parts, including all of Somalia, Djibouti, south-eastern Ethiopia, northern Uganda and Kenya, are projected to receive more precipitation in future periods (RCP8.5). However, the increase is only significant for rainfall in southern Kenya and a small part of Uganda. Under the RCP8.5 scenario, precipitation deficits are expected to increase in parts of the region. The spatial extent of the deficit extends further into the south-eastern Ethiopian highlands than under RCP4.5. It is projected that regions with less precipitation will experience an increase in the frequency of heavy rainfall events and more intense flooding. In contrast, regions with more precipitation are expected to experience a decrease in the frequency of heavy rainfall events and fewer droughts in the Hoa region. Flood events pose major risks to the populations living in these areas and often result in loss of life, displacement from homes, damage or destruction of property, damage to crops and arable land, as well as loss of productive assets such as livestock due to death or disease. In addition to projected changes in spatial distribution, time series analyses of annual precipitation and temperature for the two scenarios (RCP4.5 and RCP8.5) have been presented to quantify uncertainties in future precipitation and temperature projections over the region. Under both scenarios, the results project a likelihood of small and consistent temperature increases in future periods. Under this scenario, the projected increase is 1–2°C for the period between 2036 and 2065. However, under RCP8.5, higher temperature changes of up to 4-6°C are projected over the region. The temperature increase under this scenario is faster than under RCP4.5, especially between 2030 and 2100. For precipitation, both scenarios project a decrease in annual precipitation for most parts of the region during the initial period. This variability is projected to be higher under RCP8.5 than under RCP4.5, with recurrent wet and dry periods.

11. Other useful indicators for assessing climate change, impact and associated risks include the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) Expert Team on Climate Services such as Standardised Precipitation Index (SPI), Standardised Precipitation Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI), Warm Season Drought Index (WSDI), Cold Season Drought Index (CSDI), Maximum Temperature Anomaly (MTA), Maximum Consecutive Wet Days (CWD), Normalised Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI) and Mean Temperature Anomaly (MTA). The SPI is a widely used index for assessing drought conditions. For the period 2021-2050 (using scenario modelling) in the future and the reference period (1978-2005) under RCP4.5, moderate to severe droughts are expected over most of Sudan, Eritrea, northern South Sudan and Ethiopia and southern Uganda. Under RCP6.0, similar conditions are projected for the same regions but with a slight increase in western and eastern Kenya and south-eastern Ethiopia. Under RCP8.5, moderate drought is expected to intensify over most of these regions while severe droughts (SPI < -1) are projected to occur over eastern Sudan, northern South Sudan and Ethiopia. The SPI shows the calculated SPI for the region. Under RCP8.5, moderate drought is expected to intensify over most of these regions while severe droughts (SPI < -1) are projected to occur over eastern Sudan, northern South Sudan and Ethiopia. with more pronounced effects in north-eastern Ethiopia; the White Nile region of Sudan; the coastal, northern and Rift Valley regions of Kenya; central, western and northern Uganda; and southern Tanzania. In these areas, CWD is projected to increase by up to 50% or more in the southern parts of the region under RCP4.5, although the change is uncertain. The projected increase in CWD could affect crop growth and development, which in turn could affect food security in the affected regions. These effects are likely to be most pronounced for pastoralists who will need to adapt to drier days in the future. In Appendix 1.3, CW (defined as a day with a rainfall threshold equal to or greater than 0.2 mm) is projected to increase by up to 50% under RCP4.5 in parts of southern Sudan; western South Sudan; western Ethiopia; southern Eritrea; Uganda; and northernmost and eastern Tanzania. In these areas, the frequency of CW days will increase, with some regions expected to experience increases in CWD. Again, the projected increase in CWD in certain parts of the region could affect crop growth and development, which in turn could affect food security.

### 13. Appendices 1.5 and 1.6 present the Normalised Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI) and a proxy for Main Drought (MAD). NDVI measures the presence or absence of live green vegetation and is very important for livestock management, especially in terms of pasture availability in terms of abundance and searchability. MAD is an index that combines information from several sources to provide a proxy measure of meteorological and agricultural drought conditions on a seasonal time scale. This new index has been developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) of the United Nations (UN), using a standardised method of layer combination described in detail in the Atlas, to produce a Combined Drought Index (CDI). The CDI is based on the NDVI and MAD data layers. On the maps, most of the pastoral areas, usually located in the eastern part of the region, tend to have lower vegetation cover than other parts of the region. This is due to the semi-arid nature of these areas affecting different locations at different times and to different degrees. In particular, the end of the 2008/2009 La Niña event in early 2010 was a period when drought conditions were particularly severe, affecting livestock management, especially in terms of pasture availability in terms of abundance and searchability.

### The CDI maps show that drought is an inscappable feature of the GHA region, particularly in the semi-arid areas where an estimated 75 per cent of the labour force is engaged in pastoral activities. Drought can be acute or chronic, as illustrated in Appendices 1.7 and 1.8. For example, the severe drought of 2010/11 in Kenya and Ethiopia led to a significant drop in milk production, with over 350,000 cattle dying and 250,000 deaths in Somalia. Similarly, statistics from the EM DAT global database show that since 1984, there have been 67 drought-related disasters in the GHA region, affecting more than 1 million people.

The highest number (maximum) of people affected by any one episode was in Kenya, with over 20 million people affected. The second-highest number was in Ethiopia, where over 15 million were affected. In Somalia, there have been three major droughts since 1984, each leading to a significant drop in their market prices, with milk production falling significantly for most affected households. Wildlife in these areas has also been severely affected. The food security situation in the region is further complicated by the fact that many pastoralists are already living on the edge of subsistence.

### 13.5 Drought affected parts of the northern sector countries such as Ethiopia, Sudan and Djibouti. In Ethiopia, drought led to a significant drop in milk production, with over 20 million people affected. More than 9.5 million livestock - on which pastoralist families depend for food and livelihoods - have already died or been lost due to the drought. Over 4 million in Ethiopia, 2.5 million in Kenya and over 3 million in Somalia, with many more at risk.

## Agriculture and Livelihoods (i.e., changes in soil health/ fertility, heat stress on crop and livestock production, scarcity of pasture and water for crop production and livestock rearing due to delayed onset, increased incidence and persistence of pests and diseases affecting crops, livestock, and trees; disease in canines, cattle, goats, and sheep, increase in post-harvest losses and spoilage of animal products, shifts in planting/harvesting seasons with devastating impacts on productivity, etc.)

### Water Resources

- Reduced water availability for crop production and livestock rearing due to increased frequency of floods and droughts affecting water availability for crop and livestock production.
- Reduced surface runoff and groundwater recharge leading to reduced soil moisture content and quality of water in shallow wells and springs due to increased run-off and evaporation rates, increased demand on water resources, and increased salinity levels in the water table.

### Forest (increased fire risk; drier conditions will increase fire risk, making forest fires more frequent and more intense)

- Increased frequency of droughts leading to reduced soil moisture content and quality of water in shallow wells and springs due to increased run-off and evaporation rates.
- Increased fire risk; drier conditions will increase fire risk, making forest fires more frequent and more intense.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

Atlas as a tool for understanding climate risks to food security at lower administrative levels in the HoA.

It shows the relative vulnerability of the region's food-insecure population to climate risks at the district level. The HCVI analysis goes beyond traditional approaches that focus on crop yields and provides a multi-dimensional approach to assessing vulnerability, while highlighting the importance of exposure in determining vulnerability. The approach provides an important starting point for monitoring vulnerability over time as well as identifying adaptation strategies to address climate change.

It is based on the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change's (IPCC 2013) definition of vulnerability: "the degree to which a system, population or individual is susceptible to harm from a hazard and unable to cope with that hazard". The HCVI uses three components: exposure (frequency of floods and droughts), two for sensitivity (forest cover and rained agricultural land) and two for adaptive capacity (population below poverty line and rural population), as summarised in the table below.

## Table B.12: Indicators considered in the Hunger and Climate Vulnerability Index (HCVI) for the HoA

| Component | Profile | Indicator |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Exposure | Climate hazards | Frequency of droughts* |
|  |  | Frequency of floods* |
|  |  | Mean annual temperature* |
|  |  | Frequency of heat waves |
|  |  | Long-term trend in temperature |
| Sensitivity | Agriculture | Death per 10,000 inhabitants |
|  |  | Economic losses (US $) |
| Environment |  | Land cover |
|  | Forest cover (% of total area) | Rain-fed agriculture* (% of total agricultural land) |
| Adaptive capacity | Infrastructure | Crop yields (yield per ha - kg/ha) |
| Market access* | Water access rural population (%) | Water access urban population (%) |
|  | Paved roads (% of all roads) | Communication infrastructure |
|  |  | Total population below poverty line (S 2 per cent) |
| Socioeconomic indicators | Rural population* (%) | Decade population growth rate (%) |
| Hunger and Climate Vulnerability Index (HCVI) = Exposure * Sensitivity + Adaptive Capacity |

*Indicators are shown with good correlation (correlation coefficient >0.5).

## Sensitivity and Adaptive Capacity in East Africa

### Exposure, Sensitivity, and Adaptive Capacity

Five countries are in the range of high to very high exposure to climate stress. In southern Sudan, where high to very high exposure is more pronounced in the western parts of the country, eastern Ethiopia (north-central), northern Somalia, and certain parts of western Kenya, the eastern parts of the country suffer from frequent floods and droughts. Very high exposure to frequent floods and droughts in Kenya is most pronounced in the south-eastern parts of the country.

### Sensitivity

In terms of sensitivity measured in terms of forest cover and rainfed agriculture (Figure B.1.50), countries with high sensitivity include Ethiopia, northern Somalia, and certain parts of western Kenya. In terms of adaptive capacity, countries such as Ethiopia have a low percentage of the population living in rural areas - South Sudan and Ethiopia have high to very high adaptive capacity compared to other countries.

### Adaptive Capacity

The High Vulnerability Climate Index (HVC) includes three components: exposure, sensitivity, and adaptive capacity. The results for Ethiopia show that part of the western highlands is highly vulnerable compared to the rest of the country. In Kenya, pastoral areas are more vulnerable compared to the rest of the country, taking into account the three components (adaptive capacity, sensitivity and exposure). Parts of Oloolokwe in northern Kenya have a very high vulnerability score due to the fact that climatic shocks are prominent in most areas. The north is more vulnerable to climatic shocks than the south.

### Vulnerability

In Sudan, the HVC identified many areas of the country as vulnerable due to high levels of food insecurity, underdeveloped agriculture and infrastructure, among other factors. Sensitivity to climate-related risks is high in agricultural production areas in the west and south.

### 21. Green House Gases (GHG) Emissions Profiles

Emission profiles for the five countries in the HoA, showing Ethiopia's emissions at 204.7 MTCO₂e with agriculture as the main contributor (112.5MTCO₂e), followed by energy (38.9MTCO₂e), waste (26.5MTCO₂e) and industrial processes (23.5MTCO₂e). Sudan recorded emissions of 103.4 MTCO₂e, with the largest contribution from land use change and forestry (LUF) (21.0MTCO₂e), waste (9.8MTCO₂e), industrial processes (6.2MTCO₂e) and energy (5.3MTCO₂e). In Somalia, emissions were 47.5 MTCO₂e with the largest contribution from LUF (35.1MTCO₂e), followed by waste (9.8MTCO₂e), industrial processes (2.6MTCO₂e) and energy (5.3MTCO₂e), with smaller contributions from industrial processes (0.2MTCO₂e) and bunker fuels (0.1MTCO₂e). In Djibouti, the contributions were 47.8 MTCO₂e, with LUF (29.5MTCO₂e), waste (6.3MTCO₂e), industrial processes (2.8MTCO₂e) and energy (2.0MTCO₂e). The total emissions for the five countries were 417 MTCO₂e in 2019, with contributions of 204.7 MTCO₂e, 103.4 MTCO₂e, 47.5 MTCO₂e and 47.8 MTCO₂e respectively, totalling 1.4MTCO₂e for 2019.

## At current emission levels, these countries plan to keep their respective emissions close to the world average of 7.5 t MCO2e by 2030 or neutral by 2050. This will be achieved by reducing their respective carbon footprints and by putting green growth pathways in all sectors, particularly agriculture, forests and other land uses (AFOLU), energy, transport and industry.

By supporting countries to increase tree cover, BREFOL will help to improve GHG sequestration and thereby reduce emissions. BREFOL will support the implementation of climate-smart agricultural practices that can increase carbon stocks in soils while increasing tree cover, and increased household income from wood and non-wood farm products, thereby promoting livelihoods.

## Adaptation integration needs

The proposed programme, Building Climate Resilience for Food and livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BREFOL), is highly consistent with the countries' legal frameworks or action plans on climate change (Table B.13). In particular, the focus on natural resource and ecosystem-based approaches to adaptation, including agroforestry, livestock management, and diversified livelihood development; disaster risk management; research, knowledge management and technology transfer; co-management of resources; and gender equality is consistent with the IGAD Drought Disaster Resilience and Sustainability Initiative (IDDRSI) strategy and the Horn of Africa Regional Resilience Framework 2017-2036. The programme also aligns with the ADB's Ten-Year Strategy (2013-2022), which aims to catalyse climate-resilient actions through a triple focus on agriculture, food security, fragile states and gender. In addition, the programme is aligned with the ADB's Africa Adaptation Acceleration Programme, which aims to catalyse climate-resilient actions through a triple focus on agriculture (agriculture and food security, fragile states, and gender). The programme will also contribute to achieving three of the ADB’s High 5 development priorities: Feeding Africa, Integrating Africa and Improving the Quality of Life for African People.

### Root causes to climate change adaptation and mitigation

#### Root Cause No. 1: Extreme poverty and food insecurity
Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and South Sudan are among the most vulnerable countries in Africa, with a combined population of over 300 million, which is projected to increase by 5% by 2050. Within this population, an estimated 160 million people (or nearly 64%) live in rural areas where they depend on rain-fed agriculture for their livelihoods and food security.

### Root Cause No. 2: Climate change-induced land degradation
This has been exacerbated by the ongoing drought of Horn of Africa (HoA) and its rural poor. Over the past two decades, severe droughts have become more frequent, including in 2002-2003 in Ethiopia, 2005-2006 in Eritrea, East Sudan, South Sudan, Somalia, Djibouti, and Kenya; 2010-2011 in the Horn of Africa; and 2014-2017 in Ethiopia. These droughts have led to prolonged droughts in Somalia, southern and south-eastern Ethiopia, and northern and eastern Sudan. The most recent drought in East Africa (October 2020 – March 2021) has been more severe than those in South Sudan in 2020 and 2021, have caused extensive damage to infrastructure and loss of life. In the Horn of Africa, the drought has led to food insecurity, malnutrition, and destitute communities, leading to crop failure, famine and increased migration to urban areas.

### Root Cause No. 3: Fragility
According to the OECD (2022), fragility is the combination of exposure to risks and insufficient coping capacity of the state, system or community to manage, absorb or mitigate these risks. The Horn of Africa has been affected by drought since October 2020, with millions of livestock lost.

32. **Description of the problem:** The Horn of Africa is one of the most disaster-prone regions in the world with cropping food insecurity resulting from multiple hazards in the region. Since the 1970s, several decades of drought have been followed by floods and other extreme weather events. It has become evident that the frequency of these hazards and disasters has recently increased to every 3-5 years. The development of programmes that can strengthen the resilience of communities in the region is of critical importance for ensuring food security and reducing vulnerability.

The IGAD Drought Disaster Resilience and Sustainability Initiative (IDDRIS) Strategy, which was adopted by the IGAD member states in December 2016, predicts that between 18.6 and 21.1 million people in Ethiopia, Kenya and Somalia are likely to face crisis or emergency levels of food insecurity during the current season (October-December), as early projections suggest is likely. As noted above, extreme poverty and food insecurity (root cause case #3) and conflict (root cause case #4) are some of the key drivers of recurrent food crises, hunger and famine in the region.

Similarly, their highly fragile nature (Somalia, South Sudan and Ethiopia) are prone and vulnerable to armed conflict. Exposure to frequent conflicts and droughts has led to a significant loss of livelihood assets and resources, which limits the capacity for sustaining green/circular economy activities, sustaining wealth creation and green job opportunities. For example, in Ethiopia, 50% of the population is living below the poverty line with limited access to productive farming activities, lack of access to financial resources often limits their ability to adapt. The end result is either a complete loss of livelihood assets or migration to urban areas, which leads to increased vulnerability to vicious poverty traps.

32. **Preferred adaptation solutions:** The preferred short-, medium- and long-term adaptation solutions to the above issues at the regional level, particularly for IGAD member states, are contained in the IGAD Drought Disaster Resilience and Sustainability Initiative (IDDRIS) Strategy (Annex 1). The preferred adaptation solutions are expressed in each country's legal framework or action plan on climate change (Table 3.1). BREFOL is key to the implementation of the regional strategy for adapting to climate change at the global level. Specifically, at the global level, BREFOL contributes by increasing the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities and households through increased access to water resources as well as food security of the target population; and 3) the environment and ecosystems and environmental services are protected and restored through improved land management practices and through reduced emissions from: 1) facilitating access to renewable energy (solar and bio-digesters), 2) supporting sustainable forest management, 3) supporting sustainable agriculture (irrigation systems, etc.), and 2) supporting sustainable forest management; 1) and 2) supporting sustainable management of agro-ecosystems and water resources through improved land use planning and management, extension and advisory services, especially on markets and trade; 4) developing entrepreneurship skills and increasing access to finance for vulnerable groups, especially women and youth; and 5) increasing access to climate information services (CIS) and early warning systems (EWS). ### Potential Barriers Addressed by BREFOL:

#### Barrier No.1. Lack of access to concessional finance for smallholder farmers and pastoralists

Access to concessional finance is critical to cope with the depleting effects of climate change and extreme weather events that characterize this region. Not infrequently, smallholders in the region are unable to obtain credit from a public or private source required to implement climate-smart seeds, fertilizers and farm implements, and to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices. This is further compounded by statistics on access to finance from the World Bank’s Global Findex Database, which shows that on average only 20% of adults in Sub-Saharan Africa have a bank account, with only about 7% able to borrow from a financial institution, while only about 6% of the active farming population is able to borrow to start, operate or expand a farm or business. Given this context, smallholder farmers are often unable to access credit for climate-related events (drought and floods). Frigidity, conflict and population explosion, building resilience at scale is almost impossible. The main constraints that prevent smallholders from accessing concessional finance include: i) the limited capacity of financial institutions to provide credit to smallholders; ii) the lack of management capacity of smallholders, which increases the risk of non-repayment of loans.

#### Barrier No.2. Weak governance and political instability

Weak governance and political instability are also a major barrier to achieving climate-resilient food and livelihood systems in the region. The World Bank’s Fragile States Index 2021 ranks countries by their vulnerability to conflict, fragility, and state failure. According to the Fragile States Index 2021, all five countries are among the world's most fragile states, with Somalia being ranked as the most fragile country in the world. In addition, while 35 million people in Somalia, while in South Sudan some 7.9 million people were in need of humanitarian assistance due to conflict-related displacement (UNOCHA, 2021). Weak governance exacerbates climate change impacts and makes it difficult for governments to implement policies that improve the average on the World Bank’s Worldwide Governance Indicators, particularly in terms of political stability and absence of corruption. Political instability, conflict, violence, insecurity, and weak governance hampers the implementation of climate adaptation and mitigation responses at scale.

#### Barrier No.3. Limited access to reliable weather information and limited access to climate information

Countries in the HoLa face many challenges in terms of preparedness and readiness to respond to extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and storms. Access to reliable and timely weather information in formats that are easily accessible by end-users such as farmers and pastoralists especially in the event of an emergency is often limited. Similarly, the availability of digital technology services, which have been shown to significantly improve the resilience of people and communities, is also limited in this region. The private sector has a role to play in providing these services through the development of innovative financing mechanisms such as crop and livestock insurance products that are affordable for smallholders. However, access to credit by smallholders during seasonal droughts and lean seasons, suffer from low enrolment rates due to weak institutions and systems. In addition, there is limited access to climate information and data to support climate change interventions, inadequate mainstreaming of climate change into policies and projects, and the lack of a conducive environment for public-private partnerships that can help smallholder farmers scale up mitigation and adaptation activities.

#### Barrier No.4. Lack of private sector involvement

As discussed under Activity 5.2 of the DACCS (2020 – 2030): Apart from most governments, the private sector is often the next biggest spender on the continent. For example, there are many private entrepreneurs with vested interests in humanitarian aid and disaster risk reduction. However, it should be noted that private sector investments do not necessarily as climate change adaptation and mitigation or NDC interventions. Beyond these green-cruise-to-grave ventures, the private sector also offers various types of private finance such as


---
**Page 39**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 31

debt, equity, guarantees, insurance products, etc., which have great potential to bridge the financing gap in Africa. Unfortunately, they often lack the incentives and other enabling instruments to create a conducive environment for private sector investment in climate change adaptation and job creation.

This is particularly true in fragile states, where attract even fewer private contributions because of the high-risk profile associated with them. The creation of effective win-win private-private (2P), private-public partnerships (3P) and public-private partnerships (4P) can help to mobilize additional financial resources to accelerate the achievement of results and increase the impact of green investment projects in fragile states.

The African Development Bank (AfDB) is a key partner for GCF, as it has a strong track record in providing concessional financing. However, its concessional lending capacity is limited by donor funding which have finite limits. However, there is virtually no limit to profit-seeking private capital. So, the AfDB can play an important role in mobilizing private sector resources and opening up new sources of finance for GCF projects.

Barrier No 5: Weak implementation capacity risk at local and national levels

Weak implementation capacity at both local and national levels poses a significant risk to achieving climate resilience and disaster reduction measures. At the national level, limited human and financial resources hinder the effective implementation of climate change adaptation and disaster reduction measures. At the local level, limited resources, technical expertise and institutional capacity hinder the effective implementation of climate change adaptation and disaster reduction measures.

Furthermore, inadequate coordination and collaboration among local stakeholders further hampers efforts to achieve climate resilience and disaster reduction in the HoA.

Taken together, this means that addressing the HoA problem through the combination of well-designed programmes and projects with strong national and local capacity is essential for achieving climate-resilient development pathways in the HoA. However, this assumes that: (1) extreme climate events (risk no. 1); (2) natural disasters (risk no. 3), are not occurring in programme activities; and (3) the impact of COVID-19 and other pandemics (risk no. 4).

The overall objective of this programme is to reduce the vulnerability of the region to climate-induced induced extreme weather events, such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, etc., by enhancing the resilience and adaptive capacity of the five IGAD countries in the HoA. The region will be equipped with tools and resources that will enable it to better manage and mitigate the adverse effects of other environmental hazards such as droughts, flood, locust invasion, land degradation, etc. The highlight of the programme intervention is presented in the Theory of Change (ToC, Figure B2.2).

## B.2.1. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

### 8.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

#### a) The proposed project/programme: Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BRIEF)

The Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BRIEF) project, with a total budget of USD 153.0 million, will trigger a paradigm shift towards sustainable development and increased agropastoral production and income generation in the HoA. Through the construction and rehabilitation of physical infrastructure and climate-resilient agricultural systems (e.g., irrigation schemes, water harvesting structures, livestock markets, animal health facilities, rangelands and pastures), training and capacity building for farmers and pastoralists on sustainable land management practices, the adoption of new technologies and innovations; improving access to climate finance, digital markets and digital extension services; and strengthening institutions at all levels (national, regional and local) through the creation of an enabling environment (institutional strengthening and regulatory and policy harmonization), BRIEF will contribute significantly to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

The project is expected to generate a number of proposed initiatives economic advantages such as women's empowerment (economically and financially) through access to credit facilities, including other gender-related benefits, such as the development and promotion of women-led agribusinesses and cooperatives. Women in the HoA have an excellent track record as savers, often through informal institutions such as unregulated Savings and Credit Cooperatives (SACCOs), Microfinance Institutions (MFIs) and informal savings groups.

Another compelling characteristic of women finance behavior is that they typically reinvest up to 90% of their earnings in their households. This means that women's financial inclusion can lead to up to 40% for men. This means that investing in women's businesses can transform lives and possibly increase the number of women-owned enterprises, which may have positive social, cultural, and political implications in the region.

In addition, BRIEF will contribute significantly to the achievement of SDG15 (Life on Land) by strengthening land tenure systems by encouraging the adoption of best practices in sustainable land management and agroforestry, leading to improved ecosystem services and increased soil organic carbon. In addition,

The proposal envisages the installation of 4.8 MW of solar photovoltaic systems for solar irrigation and lighting, and as well as the potential generation of biogas energy from over 3,890 m² of biodigesters.

### 47. GCF support and participation in the BREFOL programme is therefore of paramount importance.
First, concessional resources from the GCF will help to bridge the existing adaptation finance gaps for these countries. Second, the total amount required by the six priority sectors for both mitigation and adaptation need in the six priority sectors will require a total investment of USD 40.5 billion and USD 293.7 billion respectively. The estimated cost of adaptation is USD 18.6 billion, which is estimated to be covered by the international community for both mitigation and adaptation will be over $61.1 billion. Estimated costs of mitigation are USD 21.9 billion, which is estimated to be covered by the international community for both mitigation and adaptation will be over $350.7 billion.

### 48. Specifically targeting under barrier #4, the private sector has a key role to play in scaling up adaptation finance, especially in fragile contexts such as the HoA region.
Ultimately, the private sector can help to scale up adaptation finance by providing access to resources for climate-sensitive sectors in the region. Again, concessional resources from the GCF are critical to unlock these opportunities and provide access to resources for the private sector.

### 49. Third and finally, GCF funds will support the training of more than 4,395 cooperatives and SMEs in the HoA region.
The training of over 4,395 cooperatives and SMEs is critical to mobilize financial resources from other commercial banks for climate adaptation projects. In addition, expect this critical input of well-trained cooperatives and SMEs to serve as trainers of trainers (ToTs) for other cooperatives and SMEs in the HoA region and beyond, including through local training institutions (LTIs).

#### Output 2.1: Climate resilient infrastructure built/rehabilitated
##### Activity 3.1.1: Develop and rehabilitate the resilience of infrastructure (AIDB + GCF Financed)
This activity will include the construction/rehabilitation and management of infrastructure to secure water for agriculture, livestock, and human consumption in climate-vulnerable countries.
In Ethiopia, the programme will include the construction of six (6) water resource mobilisation infrastructures tailored to climate change adaptation. These will be constructed using a participatory approach with women and gender-sensitive boreholes, as well as the construction of rural drinking water distribution systems/networks, the rehabilitation of 239 existing small-scale demand-driven water distribution systems/networks, the rehabilitation of 150 irrigation wells, and the development of 150 ha of irrigable land. It will also support the operationalisation of 35 efficient irrigation water user associations (IWUAs) through the formulation of resource management guidelines in collaboration with IWUAs and the construction of 42 water pans/irrigation channels.
In Sudan, activities include the construction of 10 boreholes, the rehabilitation of 6 existing small-scale demand-driven water distribution systems/networks, the rehabilitation of 35 irrigation wells, and the development of 75 ha of irrigable land. Activities will also involve training of water infrastructure management committees (with 30% women participation) and technical studies for water infrastructure projects.

## 94. Activity 1.T.2 Construct or rehabilitate and manage community irrigation schemes (AIBs & GCF Financed): The main objective of this activity is to improve water control and efficiency through the construction and rehabilitation of small-scale irrigation systems in Ethiopia, with a focus on the development of 240 hectares of climate-resilient irrigated perimeters. This will include the installation of hydric structures such as ditches, dams, and ponds, as well as the installation of six (6) drinking troughs and standpipes downstream of water sources. The project aims to rehabilitate and construct irrigation schemes in 12 villages that can support up to 390 hectares of climate-resilient irrigated perimeters, including multifarm cultural land, through a public-private partnership with the Ethiopian Agricultural Productivity Enhancement Program (EAPEP). This will also include small-scale irrigation for crops and fodder. In Kenya, activities will include the rehabilitation and development of 125 ha of water resources in 30 villages, as well as the construction of 4 km of roads leading to irrigation schemes and the rehabilitation of 112 reservoirs, boreholes, shallow wells and small dams. The project also aims to rehabilitate and construct 6 km of roads and bridges, improve access road from Awil town to a rice scheme and the installation of a chain-link fence for the rice perimeter.

## 55. Activity 1.T.3 Construct or rehabilitate and manage community livestock infrastructure (ADB Financed): This activity will facilitate the development of community-driven infrastructure to improve livestock productivity in Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia, Sudan, and South Sudan. It aims to improve technical studies on the management of community livestock infrastructure, including the procurement of animal health services, veterinary drugs, feed, and other inputs for livestock production. In Ethiopia, AIDB funding will support the construction of 15 demand-driven animal health posts in the regions of Afar, Amhara, Benishangul-Gumuz, Harari, Oromia, Somali, Tigray, and Dire Dawa. In Ethiopia, AIDB funding will support the construction of 15 demand-driven animal health posts in the regions of Afar, Amhara, Benishangul-Gumuz, Harari, Oromia, Somali, Tigray, and Dire Dawa. In Ethiopia, AIDB funding will support the construction of 15 demand-driven animal health posts in the regions of Afar, Amhara, Benishangul-Gumuz, Harari, Oromia, Somali, Tigray, and Dire Dawa. In Kenya, the budget will cover the construction of 20 community livestock infrastructure projects (CLIPs) in the counties of Baringo, Embu, Kajiado, Kilifi, Kirinyaga, Laikipia, Makueni, Meru, Nakuru, Siaya, and Taita-Taveta. In Somalia, funds will be used to construct 10 CLIPs in the regions of Bay, Gedo, Jubbada Hoose, Jubbada Dhexe, Lower Shabelle, Middle Shabelle, Nugaal, Puntland, South West, and Sool. The project aims to procure equipment for 7 diagnostic laboratories and holding facilities. In Somalia, funds will be used to construct 10 CLIPs in the regions of Bay, Gedo, Jubbada Hoose, Jubbada Dhexe, Lower Shabelle, Middle Shabelle, Nugaal, Puntland, South West, and Sool. The project aims to procure equipment for 7 diagnostic laboratories and holding facilities.

In Southern Sudan, funding will support the construction/rehabilitation of 15 veterinary clinics (in the states of Bahr el Ghazal North, Bahr el Ghazal South, Jonglei, Lakes, Upper Nile, Unity, Warrap, and Kapoeta), 14 abattoirs (in Southern, Western and Eastern Kapatso) and 2 livestock quarantine centres. The project aims to improve the capacity of communities in these areas for sustainable livestock production.

## 56. The World Bank's support to TADs: The World Bank has supported the implementation of the Cross Border Pastoral Areas and Livestock Development (CBPALD). The aim is to address transboundary biodiversity conservation, land degradation, and climate change issues through cross-border development programmes that contribute to sustainable peace in cross-border border communities. These include: (1) Facilitating the establishment of TADs in 20 regions across the region; (2) Strengthening TADs against animal diseases (TADS). This has helped to synchronise vaccination and reduce outbreaks of TADS within the TADs; (3) Improving access to markets for livestock products through the development of cold storage facilities, which have been established in most of the cross-border areas in the region. Under the CBPALD, 10 regions across the region have established TADs. Communities on both sides of the border have participated in joint activities and shared range/resource locations, including water and grass and forage. These activities have helped to strengthen community relations and peace; and (4) SOFAZ has been strengthened through the establishment of a regional secretariat in Nairobi. This has facilitated local trade in livestock and livestock products and other household goods. These cross-border community interactions are the basis for peace and amicable conflict resolution.

# Output 1.2: Improved sustainable land management practices

## Activity 1.2.1: Train communities on sustainable land and management best practices (AIDB + GCF Financed): This activity aims to scale up the adoption of sustainable land management (SLM) best practices by farmers and pastoralists. This will be done through a demand-driven approach to support improved land use planning, soil conservation, and water harvesting techniques in order to improve land management systems and practices. The project will train approximately 31,000 farmers and pastoralists on SLM best practices. In addition, the project will provide training for extension workers. The management approaches will be developed with the International Livestock Research Institute (ILRI), using participatory methods. A national SLM training manual will be developed. The training programme will develop and deliver training of trainers (ToT) to over 100 regional and zonal extension officers, who in turn will train local extension workers. In addition, the project will provide backstopping training sessions for district and grassroots staff. In Kenya, support will be provided to accelerate SLM adoption through the establishment of Community Land Management Committees and Management Committees and Platforms (CLMCMPs). This will include sensitising communities on land rights and interests, organising outreach campaigns to train farmer organisations in small-scale irrigation management practices, and providing training for local extension workers. The project aims to cover approximately 6000 hectares. In addition, degraded rangelands will be identified and mapped covering an area of about 150,000 hectares. This will include the identification of areas suitable for reseeding through training sessions involving 50 stakeholders per county. Reseeding of degraded agropastoral land is expected to cover approximately 24,000 hectares in Kenya and 36,000 hectares in Uganda. In developing local capacity to manage land degradation, organising stakeholder consultations, training on the use of appropriate technologies for soil conservation, water harvesting, land rehabilitation, reutilisation and restoration. In the IGAD region, the focus will be on developing and disseminating costs and approaches for managing rangelands through community-based extension training of trainers (ToT).

## Activity 1.2.2: Support to improve range management and agroforestry (AIDB + GCF Financed): This activity aims to sustainably improve land and rangeland productivity. It will support various initiatives to improve rangeland management and regeneration at regional and national level, including the establishment of demonstration plots for soil conservation measures, enrichment and management activities on 25 kilometres of rangeland, including planting trees and shrubs, terracing, contour bunding, and other land preparation techniques that will be used by farmers to regenerate, enrich and manage a further 500 hectares of rangeland, construct a green wall, establish mulching plots, and plant grasses. The project will also support the establishment of demonstration plots on 10,000 hectares. GCF funding will promote physical and biological soil and water conservation techniques in degraded areas to improve land productivity. This includes the construction of terraces, planting of invasive plant species. In Kenya, activities will include identification and mapping of degraded rangelands, support for land use planning, construction of erosion control structures, support for spring protection measures, establishment of demonstration plots on 25 kilometres of rangeland, and the construction/rehabilitation of 3,500 hectares of rangeland. This will be carried out along with dissemination and training activities to farmers and pastoralists in the region. In Uganda, GCF funding will support the construction/rehabilitation, and 3,000 hectares of rangeland will be rehabilitated and managed using participatory approaches. The project will also provide technical assistance for the implementation of the IGAD Regional Rangeland Management Strategic Framework, improving the competitiveness of livestock value chains, and assessing cross-border veterinary laboratories.

## Output 1.2: Increased adoption of climate-smart innovations

### Activity 1.2.1: Scaling up the adoption of climate-smart innovations to improve agriculture and food production (AIDB + GCF Financed): This activity will address a major barrier to the uptake of innovation in the region. This is mainly due to limited knowledge of CSA technologies and their benefits, as well as lack of access to information on CSA technologies. According to the World Bank (2021), scaling up support for climate-smart agriculture (CSA) across the

**C**

agri-food value chain through robust policy and technology interventions can deliver robust side-win benefits: increasing productivity, reducing greenhouse gas emissions, and improving resilience to climate change. To achieve these objectives, the Green Climate Fund (GCF), in partnership with BREF, will focus on developing and disseminating tools from the catalogue of technologies and approaches (TAAT) that are well aligned with the TAAT programme to producers, including women (<30%) and youth (~38%) in the context of climate change. The GCF will support 12 countries in implementing the following activities: 1) Development of fodder seed production, and purchase and distribute 1,000 improved breeds of goats for restoring and improving livestock productivity; 2) Procurement of 500,000 seeds of high-yielding legumes for increasing production using innovative TAAT technologies (see Annexes 3.1); 3) TAAT achievements across the continent will be shared through a series of regional workshops and conferences; 4) Development of Agri-Best Practices (ABPs), and 2) Procurement of 80 machinery/equipment to improve fodder production and livestock productivity in selected countries (GCF). The GCF will invest in capacity building of NAIRES officers, extension workers and farmer organizations on TAAT technologies, as well as access to and availability of inputs for the adoption of these technologies. In addition, GCF funds will be used to produce high-quality, low-cost fish feed and improved processing techniques. GCF funds will be used to invest in capacity building of NAIRES officers, extension workers and farmer organizations on TAAT technologies, as well as access to and availability of inputs for the adoption of these technologies.

In Kenya, the GCF will invest in capacity building of NAIRES officers, extension workers and farmer organizations on TAAT technologies, as well as access to and availability of inputs for the adoption of these technologies. In addition, 50 tonnes of high-quality fish feed will be produced and distributed to small-scale fish farmers. The GCF will also provide resources to develop an interactive online tool that will be used by countries to identify the most appropriate and easy-to-use TAATs and assist in organizing an innovative regional technology fair. Complementary GCF investments in other countries will support the development and dissemination of extension materials on innovative technologies, including supporting the development of a mobile app.

### 62. Component 2: Supporting Low Carbon and Resilient Development of Agribusinesses

There is a growing emphasis on developing the agribusiness sector to enable vulnerable communities and people to benefit from increased productivity, improved market access, better integration into markets, entrepreneurship development and access to innovative financial instruments, has the potential to contribute significantly to climate change mitigation. The GCF will support the development of agriculture and food production systems, especially after COVID-19, and address climate change. This is an important opportunity for countries to develop a more resilient agribusiness sector through finance solutions in line with IDDRS Priority Intervention Area 2. This means facilitating market access, trade and financial resources. Thus, in line with IDDRS VPA Component 2 of BREF will deliver the following outputs:

### Output 2.1: Increased access to digital advisory services and markets.

### 64. Activity T1.7: Establish and deploy digital advisory services using PPM mechanism (ADEB & GOF Promoting)  
Promoting sustainable and resilient agro-pastoral value chain development requires timely access to information on weather, market prices, and other relevant data. This will be achieved through the establishment of an Early Warning System (EWS), etc., for all stakeholders. This activity will therefore respond to these important needs which for the PPF is adopted to ensure sustainability. The implementation of this activity will build on the existing work done by the African Union’s Acceleration Agenda and the Africa Agriculture Acceleration Programme (AAAP, see Appendix 2.3). The activity will support the development of digital solutions that can be scaled up across rural communities.

### 65. Proposed Public Private Partnership Approach for scaling Digital Tools:  
Despite the significant growth of digitalization in Africa’s agricultural sector, only 33 million of these rural smallholder farmers have access to digital tools and services. This is a major challenge which has been recognized by the CTA/Dalberg Advisors report. However, the report recognizes that “digitalization could be a game changer for Africa’s agricultural sector, but it will not happen without policy change and economic stresses.” Both the EU Africa Task Force on Rural Africa (TFRA) report and the Global Forum on Food and Agriculture (GFFA) communiqué highlighted the power of digitalization to transform rural and urban economies. Figure 8.3.1 illustrates the importance of digitizing the agricultural value chain.

| Digital infrastructure | Ag data | Advisory & extension services | Financial services | Supply chain management |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Mobile phones, SMS, text messages, etc., (with maps, weather forecasts, crop calendars) | Crop data (yield, quality, etc.) | Advisers and extension officers | Financial transactions (e.g. mobile money) | Agricultural inputs (e.g. seeds, fertilizers) |

### 66. One of the reasons for the low level of digitization in African rural communities is the lack of appropriate public-private partnerships to scale up the adoption of digital solutions. Other challenges that have been identified with lack of traction in the adoption of digital solutions include the following:  
* The cost of digital solutions is often prohibitive, especially when it comes to providing limited agricultural challenges in most project countries. In some countries, such as Kenya, the emergence of mobile money has made it possible for farmers and pastoralists to access financial services that have not been able to scale beyond a few hundred users.
* Most of these solutions are technology-driven (e.g., SMS, mobile apps) and NGO driven, without a corresponding sustainability strategy to ensure the continued use or scaling of the digital solutions beyond the life of the project.
* Lack of privately developed infrastructure to support agricultural services and general lack of public-private partnerships for business model innovation.
* Lack of access to credit facilities by smallholder farmers and pastoralists.
* Limited critical information services for smallholder farmers and agropastoralists on climate smart agriculture, market prices, etc., which is a major challenge among African rural communities.

### Activity 2.1.2: Construct rural markets (AIDB + GCF Financed). Limited access to markets is a major constraint for farmers and pastoralists to get the right price for agropastoral products. This activity will address this issue by constructing rural markets in different regions of the participating countries, with the main objective of facilitating trade between producers and consumers. Activities include the construction of 10 livestock markets in five community markets and the rehabilitation of rural market roads, as well as the development of livestock infrastructure such as cattle pens, feeders, water troughs, and other facilities to improve access for feeder and release stock, six livestock market centres and 30 fodder market centres. Kenya will receive support from AIDB and GCF to construct 12 demand-driven rural markets in the country. In Ethiopia, activities will include the construction of 10 livestock markets in five community markets and the rehabilitation of rural market roads, as well as the development of livestock infrastructure such as cattle pens, feeders, water troughs, and other facilities to improve access for feeder and release stock, six livestock market centres and 30 fodder market centres. Somalia will receive support from AIDB and GCF to construct 14 demand-driven rural markets in the country.

## 70. Out2.0: Developed inclusive MSMEs and cooperatives with access to climate financing.

### Activity 2.2.1: Train women and youth on entrepreneurial skills in renewable energy technologies and/or AVE development including access to credit line (AIDB and GCF Financed). Low technical


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

and microfinance capacity has been a major constraint for women and youth in developing SMEs for agricultural value chain (AVC) activities and job creation. Under this activity, BREFOL will train over 2,500 women and youth on business development services, entrepreneurship in renewable energy technologies (solar and biogas) and agribusiness. The aim is to create a critical mass of entrepreneurs, including women's groups, through training, structuring and awareness raising on entrepreneurship, leading to the development of viable enterprises. In addition to providing technical assistance for the establishment or strengthening existing cooperatives through capacity building and technical assistance, as well as business planning and financial management support, the focus will be on supporting regulatory processes, developing a rural energy catalogues, and training youth and women's groups in renewable energy technologies (solar and biogas). The aim is to enable women's groups to develop sub-projects, support youth associations/cooperatives in beekeeping, and provide technical assistance for the development of value chains. This will be done through capacity building and linkages to credit lines and commercial banks. This activity will build on the women's responsibilities in agriculture and their role as key actors in rural energy systems.

## 72. Activity 2.2.3: Facilitate access for women and youth groups to renewable energy (solar and biogas system) to enhance alternative income generation (ADB + GCF Financed): Limited access to renewable energy technologies is a major barrier for women and youth in developing countries, as it is used as a source of greenhouse gas emissions. Under this activity, more than 10,600 women and youth groups will be provided with access to renewable energy systems for alternative income generation for staple food processing (4.8 MW solar and 3.89kWp (<~123MW) biogas system). The project will provide technical assistance to the development of a rural energy catalogues, and sensitive financing mechanism tailored to the needs of smallholder farmers, fishers and MSMEs, including women's groups. This activity will build on the women's responsibilities in agriculture and their role as key actors in rural energy systems.

## 72. Activity 2.2.5: Facilitate access for women and youth groups to renewable energy (solar and biogas system) to enhance alternative income generation (ADB + GCF Financed): Limited access to renewable energy technologies is a major barrier for women and youth in developing countries, as it is used as a source of greenhouse gas emissions. Under this activity, more than 10,600 women and youth groups will be provided with access to renewable energy systems for alternative income generation for staple food processing (4.8 MW solar and 3.89kWp (<~123MW) biogas system). The project will provide technical assistance to the development of a rural energy catalogues, and sensitive financing mechanism tailored to the needs of smallholder farmers, fishers and MSMEs, including women's groups. This activity will build on the women's responsibilities in agriculture and their role as key actors in rural energy systems.

74. In addition, under this activity, BREFOL will encourage existing climate insurance providers to scale up weather insurance (WII) coverage for livestock production. #### 77. The financial context of the participating countries under the AfDB and the selected PFIs

##### 80. In Nigeria, the financial sector is dominated by the banking sector, which holds more than 95% of financial sector assets. As of July 2019, the financial landscape consisted of 34 commercial banks (including 6 Islamic banks), 7 development finance institutions, and 10 Islamic banks. The non-bank financial sector consists of 20 licensed financial auxiliaries (foreign exchange bureaus and/or remittance offices), 3 microfinance institutions, 2 insurance companies, and an electronic money institution. However, the access to formal credit in Nigeria is constrained by several factors, including: (i) unemployment and poverty; (ii) low mobile phone and internet penetration rates; and (iii) poor infrastructure.

##### 81. Despite finance also remains underdeveloped, partly due to limited internet and mobile penetration and a lack of institutional and technical capacity (barrier #3). Access to finance for SMEs is a recurring problem in Ethiopia. The total number of bank branches was 755 with more than a third (35.3%) located in the central region. In terms of banking institutions, there are 18 banks, including 2 Islamic banks (of which are private) and 532 agencies. Most micro-, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) and large enterprises have access to formal credit; however, access is limited for SMEs. For example, access to credit is more limited, especially for MSMEs: only 30% have access, compared to 68% of large enterprises. The main barriers are the high interest rates charged by banks, barriers remain, including regulatory limits on daily transactions and mobile bank account balances.

### 82. Criteria for the selection of sub-beneficiaries under activity 2.3.1:

#### Definition of Smallholder Farmers: The definition of smallholder farmers varies from country to country. For the purposes of this programme, the most widely used definition of smallholder farmers has been adopted by the World Bank and the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) as those who cultivate. Alternatively, they can be defined as farmers who operate under structural constraints, such as sub-optimal land size, limited access to inputs, poor infrastructure, and low levels of education compared to other farmers in the sector. Farming less than 2 hectares.

#### Agro-cooperative (AC): Defined as a group of smallholder farmers that have established themselves within a given territory to address the common needs of their members (smallholder farmers).

#### Agro-dealer (AD): Defined as a locally based entrepreneur who sells seeds, fertiliser and agrochemicals to poor farmers in rural areas.

## Medium Enterprise
- Employs thirty to three hundred people; has a total annual turnover in the range of US$20,000 to $50,000 and with fixed assets not exceeding $100,000.

## Small Enterprise
- Employs between ten to twenty-nine people; has a total annual turnover in the range of US$3,000 to $20,000 and with fixed assets not exceeding more than $10,000.

## Very Small and Micro Enterprises
- Employ fewer than ten people; has an annual turn-over between $500 and $2,000, and with fixed assets not exceeding $3,000.

Source: Adapted from Alliu-Koomson et al. (2014)

### Criteria for the Selection of MSMEs
- A formally registered business entity with more than 51% women's and youth* ownership (more than 51% of the firm’s assets holding or shares are owned by a woman or a youth). This will be further defined in terms of the shareholding structure within the relevant MIGA in charge, and the share of profits during the tenor of the sub-facility.
- The criteria for selection of MSMEs will be like that of the MSMEs as follows:
  - A business entity with FSB or a member of a women’s culture or youth* relevant body. The association must not be less than 5 members and at least 60% of the members being women or youth;
  - Activities are mainly carried out along the agricultural and food systems value chains, including rendering services.
  - Record track of loan repayments; Credit history should be more than two years;
  - Records and bookkeeping;
- *Defined as individuals between 18 and 35 years of age.

See Annex 26 for possible eligible activities or investments under the Credit Line Facility.


## Activity 2.2.4: Provide technical assistance to MSMEs, local PFPs, and cooperatives to create an enabling environment for credit access and sustainability (AIDB + GCF Financed): Financial, particularly microfinance institutions, will provide financial services that are tailored to the agricultural value chain to add value and increase income. This activity aims to support these actors to ensure that they have a clear understanding of the credit facility and how it can be used in conjunction with the credit. It will therefore provide technical assistance and allocate funds to finance early-stage agribusinesses, including MSMEs, PFPs, cooperatives, and other relevant service organisations. It will also conduct a needs assessment to identify eligible agribusiness MSMEs, PFPs and cooperatives, coordinate ongoing dialogue, facilitate the review of legal and policy.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 45

### Component 3: Strengthening Rural Communities' Capacity to Adapt to Climate Change

#### Effort 1: Enhance Early Warning Systems and Information Services for Vulnerable Communities in the Hoa

The Hoa is a region of Ethiopia that is highly vulnerable to environmental changes exacerbated by climate change. The Hoa is characterized by its high population density, poverty levels, and reliance on agriculture as the main source of livelihoods. This makes it central to enhancing resilience and food security in the Hoa. Climate-related disasters such as droughts, floods, and heatwaves have a significant impact on agricultural productivity and household livelihoods in the region.

By prioritizing DRM initiatives, focus can be early warning systems, such as weather stations, rainfall monitoring, and satellite imagery, to enhance the capacity of communities to adapt to climate change. This will help to mitigate the impacts of climate change on agropastoral communities. Such efforts aim to enhance adaptive capacity by providing timely information about climate-related hazards and their potential impacts.

### Output 3.1: Enhanced access to climate information and early warning systems

The Hoa region is currently lacking in meteorological stations that generate data for climate advisory services. It is essential to expand the network of agro-meteorological stations to increase the number of weather stations from three (3) to six (6). This will improve the capacity of communities to adapt to climate change by improving resilience and agricultural productivity in Ethiopia.

### Activity 3.2: Integrate and disseminate climate information and early warning into established digital platforms
