### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

#### 95. Output 3.2: Strengthened regulatory and policy frameworks and institutional capacity for climate resilience

**Activity 3.2.1:** Support delivery of climate regulatory framework and mainstreaming of climate change adaptation into sector policies, strategies and plans.

This activity will support the development of a national MRV strategy in Ethiopia, with the objective of BREFCLC, especially for the mobilisation of the private sector in the development of agribusinesses for value addition to agro-pastoral products. The main constraint to investment for SME development is the lack of reliable and timely information on climate change impacts and adaptation needs. This activity will further improve the existing investment and regulatory framework in the participating countries, including Ethiopia, by supporting the development of a national MRV strategy in Ethiopia. Coordination will take place between the National Meteorological Agency (NMA), ATI, insurance companies, agricultural extension services, private sector representatives and other key stakeholders to establish independent verification of index insurance and standardisation of insurance products.

The activity will support the mainstreaming of climate change into sector policies and strategies, including dissemination in the agriculture, water, livestock and environment sectors. In South Sudan, the activity will support the development of a national MRV strategy for adaptation and resilience at the Research Policy, the Agriculture Sector Policy and the Pastoralism Policy. At the regional level (IGAD), support will be provided to the IGAD Climate Centre to develop a climate change policy framework that is processed. Documentation and sharing of best practices on adaptation and resilience will be supported by the IGAD Climate Centre in collaboration with other key stakeholders.

Facilitate dialogue on climate change resilience in the region and develop and disseminate a report on the status of climate change adaptation policies, strategies and plans at national level. Facilitate stakeholder engagement on existing regional and national climate change and adaptation policies and strategies. Support will be provided for the development of a National Climate Change Strategy and the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy. Support will be provided for making information available to stakeholders in order to mainstream climate change into sectoral policies, and for establishing platforms for sharing data and information that can facilitate climate change into sectoral policies and strategies. A tracking platform on regional and national policies and strategies will be established by IGAD. The IGAD Climate Centre will also support the development of gender-aware pro-poor programmes and the fact that this marginalised group plays a crucial role in adaptation to climate change, especially in pastoralist communities. The successful implementation of gender-sensitive pro-poor programmes and policies will contribute to the economic and social resilience of these communities.

The IGAD Climate Centre is therefore requested from the GCF to conduct feasibility studies in the five IGAD countries to assess the feasibility of establishing developing new cooperative credit security banks under the management of women-led organisations.

102. **Activity 4.4.4: Knowledge Management and Communication** - This activity will focus on knowledge management and communication to facilitate project feasibility and knowledge sharing. This initiative will involve the establishment of robust systems for collecting, organizing and disseminating project-related information, including lessons learned, best practices, technical specifications, milestones and results through effective communication channels. This proactive approach to knowledge management is expected to ensure that all stakeholders are informed in a timely manner about relevant and important information for informed decision-making. In addition, by promoting knowledge sharing among the various stakeholders involved in the project, it will contribute to learning and innovation, ultimately improving the overall effectiveness and sustainability of the project.

103. **Need for the proposed programme intervention:** Both the baseline investment and the GCF leveraged investments are expected to be significant. The scale-up of the PFRF portfolio and additional resources will be needed to achieve significant economic benefits. In particular, the programme design uses a blended financing approach, introducing innovative financing instruments such as credit lines, matching grants and small or flexible loans (e.g., through microfinance institutions) and equity investments in banks). This approach will help to de-risk and crowd in private sector capital for resilience building in various sectors of the economy. The scale-up of the PFRF portfolio is expected to be significant.

104. **BREFOL will provide a credit line of USD 24.35 million to leverage an additional USD 87.2 million from other sources, including the GCF and private sector investors.** This will help to scale up the PFRF portfolio by providing additional leveraged resources that will help increase the PFRF’s loan portfolio to agriculture business MSMEs and other smallholder farmers in the HDA. The credit line will be used for financing agricultural inputs, supporting MSMEs in preparing business plans for PFRL, and beneficiary access to the funds will be subject to a matching grant of USD 10 million from the GCF.

In total, the GCF requested amount of USD 24.35 million in credit lines will help to leverage 35.8% more resources than what was initially proposed (USD 17.9 million).

106. **BREFOL will also deploy technological innovations as sub-projects to deliver critical climate-adaptation advisory services, market linkages, crop protection, gender and rural/rustication services.** These include the use of mobile technology for agricultural extension services, such as weather forecasts (Table 8.3.6). It will also scale up the successful technology dissemination approach of the Technology Innovation Accelerator Programme (TIAP) in the HDA to support the scaling-up of the activities of smallerholder farmers and MSMEs in the HDA. Other key innovations to be scaled out include: the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices; the use of mobile technology for advisory services, such as weather forecasts; and the use of digital tools for market linkages. BREFOL will also support the Scaling Up Agricultural Innovation Accelerator Programme (AAIP) for digital advisory services, and the Affirmative Finance Action for 100 Women Initiative (AFAWI) to provide financial services to women entrepreneurs through training to increase business productivity and growth.

### B.4. Implementation Plan (Approximately 1,500 words; approximately two pages plus diagrams)

#### 108. The Accredited Entity (AE): AIDB - The African Development Bank Group (hereinafter referred to as the AIDB) is a regional development institution that supports its member countries in their efforts towards poverty reduction and growth and social progress across the continent. It is made up of three institutions, namely: i) The African Development Bank (ADB), established in 1961; ii) The AfDB Group, established in 1985; iii) The African Export-Import Bank (Afreximbank), established in 1973; and iv) The Nigeria Trust Fund (NTF), established in 1978. It currently has 81 member countries, including 46 of the 54 countries that make up Africa. AIDB is headquartered in Abidjan, Côte d'Ivoire (with regional members).

The AIDB focuses on five development priorities as part of its ten-year strategy (2013-2022) which are: i) Access to Finance; ii) Energy and Water; iii) Transport and Trade; iv) Agriculture and Food Security; v) Light and Power Upgrades, Feed, Fertilizers, Industrialisation, Integrate Markets, Improve the Quality of Life for People of Africa.

The AIDB has supported several projects in the Horn of Africa region. These include: i) Improving access to energy services (such as roads, irrigation, electricity, storage facilities and access to markets) to improve agricultural productivity; ii) Improving access to water infrastructure (such as roads, irrigation, electricity, storage facilities and access to markets) to improve agriculture and food security, particularly to improve the livelihoods of rural populations in Regional Member Countries; iii) Improving access to finance for smallholder farmers and women entrepreneurs.

The Horn of Africa region is highly vulnerable to extreme weather conditions and uncertainties. It will be unfortunate to pursue an integrated approach that does not consider climate change adaptation as a priority. The AIDB portfolio in the Horn of Africa includes projects aimed at improving access to energy services, water infrastructure, products, access to affordable finance, especially for women, and services to mitigate the impact of climate risks.

The AIDB has a total portfolio value of USD 12 billion in the Horn of Africa. The AIDB's portfolio in the HCA is estimated at about USD 4.2 billion and is distributed as shown in Table B.4.1.

12\. **Intergovernmental Authority on Development (IGAD):** The Intergovernmental Authority on Development and Environment (IGADE) in Eastern Africa was established in 1996 to replace the Intergovernmental Authority on Drought and Development (IGADD). IGAD is a regional organization that has been instrumental in addressing recurring natural disasters that caused widespread famine, environmental degradation and economic hardship in the region. Ethiopia became the seventh member of IGAD in 2003.

13\. In view of the emerging political and socio-economic challenges, the Assembly of Heads of State and Government of IGAD decided to establish a new regional organization with a broader mandate for cooperation between Member States. The new and revitalised IGAD was launched at the 5th Summit of the IGAD Heads of State and Government in Addis Ababa on 20 June 2013.

14\. The Assembly of Heads of State and Government endorsed the decision to strengthen regional cooperation in three priority areas: food security and environmental management, peace and security and economic development and poverty reduction.

15\. IGAD is comprised of four hierarchical policy organs as follows:

* **The Executive Secretary:** The head of IGAD. He is the supreme executive regulatory organ of the IGAD. It determines the objectives, guidelines and programmes for IGAD and meets once a year.
* **The Council of Ministers:** Comprises the Ministers of Foreign Affairs and one other Minister designated by each Member State. It formulates policy decisions on an annual basis and approves the budget of the Secretariat during its biennial sessions.

16\. The Assembly of Heads of State and Government is the supreme decision-making organ of IGAD. Member States' Ambassadors in Addis Ababa or their Permanent Representatives accredited to the country of IGAD Headquarters, it convenes as often as the need arises following a request by any member state.

17\. THE SECRETARIAT is headed by an Executive Secretary appointed by the Assembly of Heads of State and Government on the recommendation of the Council of Ministers. The Secretariat implements regional projects in the priority areas, facilitates the coordination and harmonization of development policies, monitors implementation of regional projects and programmes approved by the Council and reinforces national infrastructures necessary for implementing regional projects and policies.

18\. The Executive Secretary is assisted by Divisions responsible for:

* Agriculture and Environment;
* Economic Cooperation and Regional Integration;
* Health and Population;
* Peace and Security;
* Administration and Finance; and
* Planning Coordination and Partnerships

19\. In addition to the above-mentioned departments at the headquarters in Djibouti, IGAD has a number of implementing agencies that are established by other member states. These include the IGAD Conflict Early Warning and Response Mechanism (CEWARN), the IGAD Security Sector Programme (SSP), the IGAD Centre for Peace and Security (IGAD-CPS), the IGAD Climate Prediction and Application Centre (ICPAC) and the IGAD Regional Aids Programme (RAP). In addition, due to the devastating drought that hit the Horn of Africa in 2011, IGAD established the IGAD Drought Early Warning System (IDAWS) at all levels. IGAD launched the IDAWS to end drought emergencies through the introduction of innovative sustainable development strategies and policies. The IGAD Drought Early Warning System is a multi-sectoral and multi-stakeholder building initiative to future climate and economic shocks.

21\. At the country level, the following Ministries will act on behalf of the various agencies at the EEC:

* Ministry of Trade and Industry (MoTI) and Ministry of Commerce Economics and Finance (MoCEF);
* The Ministry of Agriculture, Water, Fisheries and Livestock (MAWFL);
* The Ministry of Environment, Forests and Climate Change (MoEFCC) in Ethiopia (MoFEF) and the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MoALF);
* The Ministry of Planning and Development, Ministry of Treasury and Economic Planning (NTP) and Ministry of Agriculture, Water, Fisheries and Livestock, and Cooperatives (MAWFLC);
* The Ministry of Environment, Forests and Climate Change (MoEFCC) in South Sudan (MoFEF) and the Ministry of Livestock, Forestry and Range (MLFR);
* The Disaster Management Agency (DMA) acting through its Ministry of Finance and Planning, South Sudan (MoFPF) and Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAAF).

### Program Implementation: The implementation of the regional activities will be carried out by IGAD as the regional implementing agency, working in close liaison with the five national governments. In addition to the IGAD Secretariat, the implementation of the programme will be supported by the institutions of the GAD Member States which are responsible for the implementation of the activities at the national level. The IGAD Executive Secretary (ES) and the IGAD Director General (DG), in consultation with AIDB and IGAD based on the grant amount and associated conditions, the IGAD Executive Secretariat (IES) and its secretariat will be responsible for the overall coordination of the programme implementation.

### 12.4 The IGAD Secretariat, through the IDRDR Platform Coordination Unit (PCU), will be responsible for coordinating the implementation of the Regional Programme. The Regional Programme Coordination Unit, under the PCU, will be responsible for the day-to-day management and coordination of the programme. The role of a Regional Programme Steering Committee (RSSC), the PCU will provide coordination support and overall guidance to the RSSC in its deliberations on the implementation of the regional activities. The main responsibilities of the RSSC include the approval of programme budgets, programme activity reports and annual accounts, as well as any other matters related to the implementation of the Regional Programme. The applicable instrument and its secretariat will be provided by a Regional IDRDR Committee. During the implementation phase, the PCU will provide technical assistance in the form of a regional focal point for technical assistance support within the framework and under the terms of a Memorandum of Understanding with the IGAD Secretariat. The PCU will also ensure that the IGAD Secretariat is informed on any issues related to the participating countries sharing the border.

The PMU will be responsible for the overall management and coordination of the programme. Among other things, the PMU coordinates directly with the EEs and project stakeholders and is responsible for ensuring that all activities are carried out in accordance with the approved work plan and effective stakeholder engagement. The PMU normally consists of a project coordinator; an administration, finance and procurement manager; a communications assistant; a monitoring and evaluation manager; an environmental and social safeguard expert; a gender equality and women's empowerment (GEWE) focal point; a rural development and rural finance assistant; a communications assistant and support staff. ### GCF’s Assistance for GCF functions (max. 10,000 words, approximately 2 pages)

130. The Horn region is one of the most disaster-prone regions in the world, suffering from recurrent food crises caused by extreme poverty and food insecurity (root cause 1), climate change and environmental degradation (root cause 2). Unfortunately, these countries’ high levels of debt severely constrain public spending to address most of the root causes. The lack of access to finance for smallholder farmers, pastoralists, and other small-scale producers and actors along various value chains, who are typically constrained by resource limitations (Barrientos et al., 2015), is a major barrier to increasing agricultural productivity and promoting investment activity in the region and target countries. As a result, institutional investors – primarily pension funds, insurance companies, and sovereign wealth funds – have not been able to invest in the institutional investment, creating local and regional green business opportunities that will be

#### Institutional Investment

132. The resources requested from the GCF will therefore play a critical role in supporting farmers and pastoralists in accessing finance for their activities, which is essential for increasing agricultural productivity and promoting investment activity in the region and target countries. This will help to reduce poverty and food insecurity, while also contributing to climate change mitigation and adaptation objectives.

**GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 55**

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is seeking private sector investment in the HoA. A key lesson from other ongoing initiatives in the countries and region is the centrality of green blended finance in catalysing a shift towards climate-resilient development, including through public-private partnerships (PPPs). Without additional concessional and/or additional private sector finance to overcome the priority adaptation investments mentioned above will certainly be difficult due to the rising public debt situation in these countries. This has been severely constrained by the need for large-scale infrastructure projects, which are critical for building the resilience of smallholder farmers and pastoralists. In addition, the fragile nature of some of these countries makes them particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts, with the potential for being the essential trigger for larger transformative impacts within the HoA.

The private sector is a key player in this area due to its highly concessional nature of its funding instruments. In addition, GCF funding typically allows for co-financing from other development partners and/or the private sector. The mobilisation of concessional finance is essentially what is needed to catalyse the priority adaptation investments proposed in the IDORISI strategy beyond the GCF-funded activities.

**Nature and level of concessionality of the GCF financial instrument**

The concessional nature of BREFLO will help overcome critical barriers and risks to climate change adaptation and mitigation in the HoA region. In particular, the existence of large concessional funding instruments such as those provided by the World Bank (the International Development Association - IDA) and the European Investment Bank (Barrier #2), as outlined in Section B.2, prevent the region from investing in and implementing the large-scale infrastructure projects that are critical for building resilience to climate change impacts. The mobilisation of additional funding from development partners and the private sector, as highlighted in the 5th interim outcome report on the IDORISI strategy (IDORISI-5), will be essential if it is hoped to scale up the region’s adaptation and mitigation efforts.

The private sector also has a critical role to play in scaling up adaptation finance to build resilience in the HoA region, particularly due to the highly fragile nature of the region (that most predominantly manifests as weak state capacity). The GCF Private Sector Facility (PSF) will help to scale up concessional financing for the HoA region. In addition, the high indebtedness of all five HoA countries, limited access to credit and other financial instruments, and the relatively low level of private sector participation in the HoA region are critical barriers to scaling up adaptation finance in the region. Short funding horizons also make it difficult to fully integrate adaptation measures.

The GCF PSF will help to scale up concessional financing for the HoA region. In addition, the high indebtedness of all five HoA countries, limited access to credit and other financial instruments, and the relatively low level of private sector participation in the HoA region are critical barriers to scaling up adaptation finance in the region. Short funding horizons also make it difficult to fully integrate adaptation measures.

Emerging market sovereign debt levels in the HoA region are high relative to GDP (around 43% of GDP in 2019) and are projected to rise further, with a high level of short-term debt. The ratio of total public debt service to GDP is expected to increase from around 57% in 2018 to over 60% by 2022. This is due to the large share of short-term debt (around 34%) and the low interest rate on this debt, which is expected to average 10% from 2017 to 2022. The primary debt plus debt service is estimated at close to 65% of GDP as of June 2022, total public debt stood at around 31% of GDP in 2021, up from 24% in 2019. In addition, the ratio of total public debt service to GDP has risen from 72% in 2021 to 72% in 2022, mainly due to public investment in infrastructure, debt challenge management and climate change adaptation projects. The IMF’s 5th Article IV Consultation for Sudan found that the country’s external debt is projected to rise further, with total external debt expected to reach around 43.3% of GDP in 2024 above the 30% sustainability threshold. South Sudan's debt risk rating improved from “high” to “moderate” by the World Bank in June 2021 and is now classified as “medium”. The commercial debt with Qatar National Bank, which accounts for 48% of external debt. The debt restructuring agreement signed between the government of South Sudan and Qatar National Bank in May 2021 saw the country’s total external debt fall from around $3 billion to an estimated 28.9% of GDP in 2022. Commercial loans accounted for 81% of total external debt in June 2022, followed by multilateral (56%) and domestic (11%) loans. Based on the above, it is clear that South Sudan’s public debt situation remains a critical barrier to scaling up adaptation finance.

Several categories of development and training activities will be undertaken, including:

* Production, dissemination and application of agro-meteorological information;
* Training for climate change adaptation in agriculture for government agencies, emergency services, aid agencies, agricultural NGOs, extension services, farmers' associations, etc.;
* Application of TAA/T COMPACT technologies and innovations. Global Centre on Adaptation (GCA) and the Alliance for Agricultural Productivity and Agri-Business Programmes (AAP);
* Climate-resilient and high-quality gender-responsive business planning and development;
* Promotion and adoption of climate-resilient and high-value practices and technologies;
* Preparation of agribusiness development plans; and,
* Access to finance.

## 151.

Each category targets a different group of beneficiaries, including:

* Meteorological services, national and regional institutions and insurance providers;
* Farmers and other local value chain actors, smallholder farmers, farmer-based associations, cooperatives, producer organizations (POs), input dealers, producer organisations (POs), micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs), commercial farms, agribusinesses, etc.;
* Extension services, including extension officers from ministries of agriculture, water resources, rural development, etc., agricultural research institutions, village extension officers, and private external providers, including NGOs;
* Women-led agriculturalists and districts; and,
* Ministries and their auto-national departments.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the programme/project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Total Investment Framework.

## 153. BREFOL will contribute to GCF impact level E2: Scalability, replicability and very high sustainability.

In terms of scalability, there are key innovations introduced by BREFOL, including Smallholder Adaptation Accelerator Programme (SAAAP) through digital extension services, the Technology Innovation Platform (TIP), the Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP), innovative technology dissemination approach and AFAW's approach to women's empowerment. The SAAAP is a unique model that combines climate change adaptation with economic development. This comes with significant economic benefits even with less resource commitment. It's therefore very attractive for replication in other sectors, markets, geographical regions or countries. In terms of sustainability, the programme will be implemented over 10 years and will have a strong focus on the sustainability of the programme.

## Mitigation potential

The mitigation potential of the programme is expected to install a total of 4.8 MW of solar PV for solar irrigation, solar lighting and processing systems, and to generate a total of 3,890 MWh/year of electricity from these installations. This will result in an annual reduction of 2,715 tonnes CO2e in GHG emissions avoided at the end of the Mitigation Commitment Period (MCP) under agreement. Table D.1.1 summarises the mitigation potential of the programme from two GCF climate change adaptation projects: Irrigation Systems - Light and Processing Systems - Mwef, and Rehabilitation of irrigated land - m².

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 sentence)

161. Potential for scaling-up and replication: As shown in Table E.5.A., a very feature of the project is that it will provide an opportunity to scale up climate change adaptation strategies in five IGAD member states in the Horn of Africa (HoA) region. This will be done through financial innovations that provide the right incentives and enable instruments that are attractive to private


---
**Page 71**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 63

sector investors, such as private public partnerships (39%) and public-private producer partnerships (4%). These models bring together combined expertise and resources to accelerate the scaling up of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) and green investment in areas of high wealth creation and job growth, especially for the most marginalised groups (women, girls, youth). These models are strongly aligned with the GCF’s goal of funding initiatives that catalyse climate impact at scale. The BREFOL Direct Insurance Credit Window (BDCW) model is to use existing credit lines to provide insurance products to smallholder farmers and other vulnerable sectors investors such as agribusiness MSMEs and weather-based insurance promoters, with a vested interest in the success of CSA.

162. With the initial investment of GCF funds and co-financing ($33 259 million), BREFOL aims to scale up climate-smart agriculture (CSA) across five countries in Africa. These projects will implement additional climate change adaptation initiatives along the energy, agriculture, forest and water sectors. The combination of these two types of investments, which are complementary, represent a profound opportunity to use the 39% and 4% models to shift the status quo trajectory in five IGAD countries towards a low-carbon, climate-resilient pathway that will ensure that the energy sector is not left behind as the continent transitions to a low-carbon economy. With this investment, this GCF investment will already leverage an additional US$25 23 million from direct private sector investors and co-financing partners. The combination of public and private sector financial innovation models will contribute to the larger adaptation initiative of the IDRS for the next five years. This is a key opportunity to scale up climate-smart agriculture (CSA) initiatives within the continent, further contributing to this potential paradigm shift through further scaling and replication in other countries across Africa.

163. It should be noted that the BREFOL model can be scaled up immediately with the support of the GCF. The BREFOL initiatives in the five countries and the SADC region are provided in Annexes 2.1-2.8.

164. Another key feature of the programme’s up-scale and scaling-up is the integration of the technology transfer and capacity building (TTCB) component into the project design, with specialists, inter alia, in the scaling up and dissemination of proven agricultural technologies and innovations. The TTCB component has been designed to be implemented through a network of 27 low-income African countries. Under TATAV, the average productivity gains recorded by farmers participating in the programme have ranged from 10% for maize, 35% for rice, 48% for small livestock, 38% for both cassava and maize, 40% for orange and fresh sweet potato, 42% for rice, 44% for aquaculture (stilapia), 64% for sugarcane, 71% for fodder, 80% for millet, 100% for sorghum, 153% for cotton and 190% for groundnuts. The TTCB component is also being implemented in other countries in the region.

165. Similarly, the introduction of AFAWA (Alternative Finance Action for Women in Africa) to contribute to the economic and financial empowerment and the empowerment of marginalised women across five IGAD countries, where gender discrimination is rampant. AFAWA’s sole mandate is to bridge the gender gap in access to finance and other resources that are critical for women’s participation in the agricultural sector. This gap is currently estimated at $42 billion for African women across all business sectors, including $15.6 billion in agriculture alone. AFAWA therefore aims to unlock up to $3.2 billion of private sector investment through its innovative financing model and the BREFOL programme cannot be overstated in terms of AFAWA’s scalability and replication potential.

186. **Potential for knowledge sharing and learning:** BREFOL’s potential for knowledge creation and learning can’t be overemphasised. It is an integral part of several of the programme's planned activities. The programme will provide access to affordable finance, which will contribute to the development of agribusinesses. Key elements of this activity with deep knowledge sharing and learning capabilities are: 1) strengthening access to digital extension services and markets; 2) building capacity for climate change adaptation in smallholder farmers (SFFs), women, youth and Departments and Agencies (MDAs) in the five countries; 2) training women and youth in business skills and financial literacy; 3) providing information on alternative livelihoods and alternative income from clean energy; and 4) designing innovative credit lines to local PFIs for on-lending to SFFs. The programme will also provide access to affordable finance, which can be shared from its innovative credit line wind for other ongoing ADB programmes in the Congo Basin countries.

187. Another key activity of BREFOL is to improve access to climate information and early warning systems (EWS) through a range of strategies including: 1) generating, managing and disseminating climate advisory information; 2) providing training on how to use and interpret climate services for forecasting, drought monitoring and forecasting, crop and rangeland monitoring, and seasonal forecasts; 3) developing and disseminating climate advisories in an actionable format, including support for the organisation of interactive seasonal user forums; will ensure that climate advisory information is available through a range of channels. These challenges in the Hoik will improve future climate-smart planning, even after the programme ends. The programme will also provide access to affordable finance and technical assistance to government and national, regional and continental climate research institutions, including the IGAD Climate Prediction Centre (ICPC) and Cimel-Africa. To ensure the exchange of information, BREFOL will establish a National Hydroclimatic Services Network (NHSN). The NHSN and warnings for farming agencies, emergency services, aid agencies, agricultural NGOs, extension workers, farmers, women’s groups, youth and other stakeholders. Climate advisory systems developed under this key activity will enable the National Hydroclimatic Services of each participating country to provide climate information and early warning services to their programmes on climate change adaptation for different types of users by BREFOL.

188. **Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment:** BREFOL will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment in three ways. The first is by addressing a critical challenge currently facing the SFFs, which are women and youth. Women and youth face significant obstacles to access to affordable finance to grow and feed Africa. With an estimated $42 billion financing gap for African women across business value chains, including $13.6 billion in agriculture, women’s groups are often excluded from accessing credit. In addition, women farmers who have traditionally been involved in subsistence farming or small-scale commercial agriculture is perceived as riskier, so they face prohibitive interest rates. In addition, women often lack the financial and business skills to access finance and manage it effectively. This makes them less able to understand and respond appropriately to women entrepreneurs, who also often lack the financial and business skills necessary for accessing credit and managing it in a sustainable manner within their environment. In many African countries, legal and regulatory frameworks hinder women’s full participation in private sector growth.

189. The programme will be designed to improve access to finance for smallholder farmers to build their adaptive capacity, especially women (Barrier #4), and to help reduce gender discrimination (Barrier #5). The programme will provide women with access to affordable finance through the establishment of a credit line wind for other ongoing ADB programmes in the Congo Basin countries. This will also contribute to women’s empowerment. This will facilitate the creation of green jobs, especially for young girls and youth who are often excluded from accessing finance. To ensure that women have access to affordable finance, the programme will work with local private financial institutions (PFIs), in collaboration with the

#### D

**170.** The enabling environment created by BREFOL is: the BREFOL Dedicated Direct Credit Window (BDCW), specifically designed to provide local Private Financial Institutions (PFIs) with stable and predictable credit lines, which will be used to finance projects in the agriculture sector using the AGRICOM and AFOLU sectors. This will facilitate access to credit for the purchase of products, improve the creditworthiness of women and young girls, and increase the turnover of smallholder farmers. The BDCW will also provide PFIs with a risk-sharing mechanism that allows youth to actively participate in food production using appropriate clean energy technologies will be particularly beneficial for women and young people who are often excluded from these groups in society.

**171.** The second enabling environment created by BREFOL is: 1) facilitating access to digital extension services and information technology (IT) platforms, including mobile phone-based insurance schemes to protect against the adverse impacts of climate shocks on food and agriculture systems; 2) strengthening national climate monitoring and reporting systems; 3) developing a national climate change adaptation strategy and action plan; 4) supporting countries commitments to implement the Paris Agreement: 5) constructing and rehabilitating 450 water treatment plants, 180 boreholes, 600 km of irrigation canals and rehabilitation of 200 km of drainage systems; 6) development of 6.000 ha of irrigated land and rehabilitation of 200 km of drainage systems; 7) strengthening the capacity of livestock farmers to improve animal health and market access infrastructure (e.g. livestock markets, animal health posts, mobile clinics, veterinary services); 8) improving the capacity of smallholder farmers to produce forays or haystacks, feeder roads; 9) facilitating and operationalising 3 cross-border Memoranda of Understanding (MoU) on the management of transboundary pests and diseases, including zoonoses in border areas; and 9) establishing 3 cross-border veterinary laboratories in 3 GAD border clusters and increasing the competitiveness of livestock value chains in the GAD region.

**172.** Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies: BREFOL is consistent and in alignment with ECOFIN Strategy and the Hohlu Regional Framework as illustrated in Table B.1.4.

**173.** Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with national priorities and strategies: The proposed project will contribute to the achievement of Programme ToC, addressing the HoA problem through the combination of well-designed interventions and policy reforms that are expected to trigger a positive impact on low-carbon and climate-resilient development pathways. The programme assumes that 1) extreme climate events (risk no. 1); 2) insufficient participation of women and youth in programme activities (risk no. 2); and 3) lack of institutional capacity for project implementation (risk no. 3) are well managed during programme implementation.

174. BREFCL will reduce the negative environmental impacts of rangeland degradation by improving the behavior and practices of farmers, pastoralists and communities. It will also have significant positive social and economic impacts on rural households in the region. The baseline programme will therefore directly contribute to SDG 6 by benefiting an estimated 308,531 people (50% women, girls and youth) with access to clean and reliable energy, safe water and sanitation services. The programme will also contribute significantly to other SDGs through solutions and technological innovations (SDG 9; Climate-smart planning will be improved, including the development of a climate change adaptation plan for the region). It will also contribute towards achieving SDG 13. In addition, the long-term sustainability of the programme will be ensured by empowering women and girls to participate in decision-making processes at all levels. The degraded rangelands/pasturelands will contribute to the achievement of SDGs15 (halt and reverse land degradation) and 2 (end hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition). It will also contribute towards achieving SDG 7 through carbon sequestration and contribute to climate stabilisation). The programme will promote climate-resilient nature-based solutions that can help mitigate floods, droughts, erosion and landslides. These solutions will provide a range of environmental services and social benefits for the environment and local communities. This will promote gender equality in access, use and control over resources and land tenure rights. It will also contribute to improved health benefits, building on existing knowledge (SDG 5).

175. In many rural areas, men and women differ significantly depending on their different roles and responsibilities at the household and community levels. Women play a more active role in managing natural resources than men do. However, women are often less dominant in promoting action to tackle the impacts of a changing climate. However, women are also more vulnerable to the effects of climate change because they tend to be less educated and not involved in critical political and household decision-making processes that affect their lives. Women are also more likely to be responsible for food security and have a greater ability to make quick and effective decisions in the event of disasters.

176. BREFCL, through its focus on gender mainstreaming and women's economic empowerment programme components, will ensure that women are not only beneficiaries but also active participants in the project. The programme will provide financial resources and finance that hinder women's ability to manage current climate risks and adapt their livelihoods to long-term changes. It will do this by providing training for women on how to access microfinance services and by ensuring that at least 30% of all funds are allocated to women-led MSMEs/ESAL, WABEs, cooperatives and POIs. The other 50% will be allocated to men-led enterprises. This will ensure that women have the skills and resources with which they can participate in the programme (Abstracted from BREFCL Project Document). Specific actions have been integrated into the programme aimed at providing them with appropriate tools to identify and develop bankable business plans and improving their access to finance.

D. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

177. Timely access to finance is critical to cope with the debilitating impact of climate change and improve livelihoods in rural areas. Women are particularly vulnerable (Reid et al., 2011). Frequently, smallholder farmers, especially women, including women in female-headed households, do not have access to credit facilities to purchase agricultural inputs such as climate-smart seeds, fertilizers, tools and equipment or to finance their livelihoods and practices to cope with sudden disasters such as seasonal droughts and flash floods. They also lack the skills and knowledge required for accessing these services.

The situation is further exacerbated by climate change and the recent global COVID-19 pandemic. Women are more likely than men to be employed in informal sectors, which are food systems. Thus, without concrete measures to ensure greater access to finance and to strengthen women's capacity to access credit facilities, they will not be able to develop climate-resilient livelihood options, the development of agriculture and food systems will

### D.3 Country ownership (max: 200 points; opportunity level: 1)

**18A.** A mission from the African Development Bank (AfDB) Group visited the five IGAD countries involved in the programme from 10 to 19 June 2019, to identify the components of the proposed project and to discuss the modalities for its implementation.

The mission to identify the HoF programme followed the AfDB's announcement at the February 2019 meeting concerning the Sahel Region Development Plan (SRDP) (HoF) (2015–2030), and to support the preparation of the Priority Programme Catalogue in the Sahel (PPC) for 2020-2026.

**18B.** The AfDB mission was conducted in close cooperation with the Secretariat and institutions of the Intergovernmental Agency for Development (IGAD), namely the Agriculture and Empowerment Commission (AEC), the Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management Commission (CCDRC), the Economic Cooperation and Integration Department (DEC) and the Social Development Commission (SDC). The mission also included representatives from the IGAD Climate Prediction and Application Centre (ICPAC), and the ICIPRLD. The AfDB mission team also liaised with the relevant ministries responsible for agriculture, environment, disaster risk management, social development, and climate change in each country. See Annexes 1.1-5 for the No Objection Letters issued by the National Development Planning Commission (NDPC) of Ethiopia, the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Resilience Groups in Kenya, Djibouti and Ethiopia. The national meetings were facilitated by the IGAD Secretariat's National Focus Points. All the stakeholders met expressed their support for the new programme.

**18C.** The IGAD Secretariat welcomed the Bank’s initiative in preparing the HoF programme and proposed that it be implemented as a flagship project of the Bank. It also supported the need to strengthen agriculture, agribusiness, climate services, value chain and marketing for the most vulnerable populations in the region. The Secretariat further underscored the importance of synergy with ongoing strategies and actions at both regional and country levels.

**18D.** Considerations for the HoF programme include:

- The need for aggregation/integration of interventions; concentration of project activities
- Strengthening the quality of project entry, which requires upstream socio-economic, environmental and social studies, as well as financial structuring studies and detailed designs.

## 195. In order to assess the financial and economic viability of the proposed interventions, including the effectiveness of the GCF investment in the Programme, an Economic and Financial Analysis (EFA) was carried out (see Annexes 3a/b for the EFA summary and Excel worksheets). A summary is provided below.

The EFA aims at assessing the overall cost-effectiveness of the programme by quantifying benefits derived from the activity level models, in terms of incremental benefits relative to the baseline scenario. The analysis also assesses the financial viability of the programme on a regional scale of the project and its objectives (total area under cultivation in hectares), in order to assess the overall economic impact of the programme.

The results are compared with the project costs (estimated from the project budget) to assess the overall cost-effectiveness of the programme. The analysis is based on three financial indicators: Net Present Value (NPV), the Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) and the Modified Internal Rate of Return (MIRR). The financial and economic models of the crop and a summary of the assumptions are provided in Annex 3a.

The financial analysis (FA) has three objectives. First, to assess the financial soundness of the project. Second, to determine whether the project is financially viable on an individual farm basis or not. Third, to provide a strong analytical framework for the economic evaluation of BREFOL in the region. Such an evaluation will be more robust and reliable than one based only on the cost-benefit analysis of an individual farm.

The financial analysis has been considered for each FA, as shown in the attached Excel worksheets (Annex 3a). These are: a) 'model assumptions', which provide the general assumptions and parameters currently used with project work; b) 'WOP scenario', which is the baseline scenario that will be compared against the 'project' WOP scenario, which includes all activities and interventions of the programme. Note that the WOP is the business-as-usual (BAU) scenario for each farm in the region. The WOP scenario assumes that no new investments or changes will occur in all cases; c) 'Irrigation as a "driving factor" for both scenarios, given the aridity of the region and the need to increase water productivity on farms. In this case, we have included irrigation as a 'driving factor' for both scenarios, given the aridity of the region and the need to increase water productivity on farms; d) 'WOP scenario', which includes all potential and returns to farm labour would be in the WOP scenario. In addition, the use of the WOP scenario is used to quantify the net benefits of the programme's 'business-as-usual' situation at the individual and aggregate levels.


---
**Page 79**
---

198. The FA results show that most of the crop models are profitable from the farmers' perspective. The cash flows show that farmers will be able to cover the necessary operating costs. Furthermore, the inclusion of additional income sources (e.g., CO2 sequestration, carbon credits, investments, etc.) into the programme, the potential of the selected crops would be doubled, implying more cash inflows to farmers. The FA results also confirm that the proposed production packages are financially viable and will generate positive financial returns for participating beneficiary farmers of the activities implemented under BREFOL. The beneficiaries are financially attractive in terms of their net present value (NPV) and internal rate of return (IRR). They cover operating costs, especially with the increased yields associated with the introduction of TAAT CIMPY (e.g., 10% for maize; 25-30% for beans; 40-60% for groundnuts; 70-80% for cabbage and lettuce and tomatoes) with stable crops or agroforestry to generate more income. However, as our study shows, the profitability of these activities is highly dependent on the discount rate.

199. At an aggregate level, the FA calculates an overall Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) of 28% for the programme's baseline scenario. The FIRR is calculated at a discount rate of 6%, $477.46 million at a discount rate of 9.6%, $372.13 million at a discount rate of 6%, $586.76 million at a discount rate of 7.9% and $188.11 million at a discount rate of 19.1%. Further sensitivity analysis shows that the FIRR is highly sensitive to changes in the discount rates: as low as 10% and 20% cost overruns, benefits increase, benefits decrease and -1- and 2-year benefit delays can reduce the FIRR by up to 50%, while a 3-year delay can reduce it even further. In most cases much higher. The NPWCS were also always positive. This shows that the programme is financially attractive in terms of its net present value (NPV) for the smallholder beneficiary but would also remain viable under a wide range of alternatives.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 74

## Replicability

Currently, smaller farmers and pastoralists have limited access to innovative programmes of action and climate-smart land management technologies. Uniquely, the project proposes innovative financing mechanisms that help integrate climate smart technologies into existing services and support on services credit.

Given the regional nature of the GCF proposal, it is designed to be replicable in IOAG member countries and beyond. Thus, almost all of the proposed outputs will contribute towards national, subnational, country, regional and continental levels (beyond Kenya) by: 1) increasing access to climate risk finance and insurance mechanisms; 2) rehabilitating climate-resilient infrastructure (water supply, irrigation systems, water harvesting facilities and dams, roads network, ICTs, etc.) for climate-smart land management; and 3) increasing access to digital technologies and advisory services for climate-smart land management.

The project will contribute to reaching this target scenario through:

* Output 1: Climate resilient infrastructure and rehabilitation of climate-resilient infrastructure (water supply, irrigation systems, water harvesting facilities and dams, roads network, ICTs, etc.) for climate-smart land management.
* Output 2: Developed inclusive MSMEs and cooperatives in response to climate financing.

## Sustainability

Due to the six barriers described in C3, the adaptation of climate-smart technologies by farmers and pastoralists is not sustainable. This is due to the climate hazards identified in this proposal and because no other activities are being implemented.

The sustainability of the proposed outputs will require:

* Output 1: Increasing access to advisory services and digital technologies for climate-smart land management.
* Output 2: Increasing access to finance for women and youth, and increasing access to insurance mechanisms for climate risk finance and insurance mechanisms.

The project will contribute to reaching this target scenario through:

* Output 1: Increased access to advisory services and digital technologies for climate-smart land management.
* Output 2: Developed inclusive MSMEs and cooperatives in response to climate financing.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 95

## Activity 1.2.1: Support sustainable rangeland management and agroforestry
Transformation to enhance resilience and adaptation to climate change, while promoting sustainable land use and development plan at community, mapping exercise to identify Farm Based Aggregated (FBA). Enhancement of the FBA into Farm Based Aggregated (FBA). Enhancement of the FBA into 3 cross border rangelands (cluster) sustainably remanitized and managed under agroforestry and pastoralism
28,900 ha of degraded land/ forest remanitized and managed under agroforestry and pastoralism

## Activity 1.2.1: Provide extension services and develop climate smart innovations to improve agricultural and fodder production
The three transformative areas under this activity include investing in the development of digital solutions through PPP mechanisms, management techniques to fight against the aridness, advocates on integrated pest management control and facilitating the establishment of a dedicated credit facility for smallholder farmers. Research missions, and Utilization Technologies for African Arid Lands (TATA) will be used to support smallholder and smallholder farmers in the HOA. This activity will also support the development of digital solutions through PPP mechanisms; access to credit line
278,000 small holder farmers and pastoralists benefiting from extension services
250 trainers posted on tools for extension services
142,000 small holder farmers and pastoralist benefiting from established digital advisory services

## Activity 1.3.2: Disseminate resilient varieties to improve agricultural and fodder production
N/A

## Activity 2.1.1: Establish and develop advisory services using PPP mechanisms
This activity will support the development of digital solutions through PPP mechanisms. The initiative will also support smallholder farmers (women and youth) in accessing credit lines for investment in renewable energy technologies (Solar and Biogas). The focus is to empower producer organizations, women and youth groups in rural areas by training and raising awareness of entrepreneurship, leading to a sustainable income generation. BREFCO, will support the establishment of a Dedicated Direct Credit Facility (DDCF) for smallholder farmers in the HOA with stable lendable resources to meet the financing needs of smallholder farmers, pastoralists, MMDAs, FMA and other 50 of digital advisory services established through PPP mechanism
142,000 small holder farmers and pastoralist benefiting from established digital advisory services

## Activity 2.1.2: Construct rural market infrastructure
N/A

## Activity 2.1.3: Train women and youth on entrepreneurial skills in renewable energy technologies and/or AVC development and access to credit line
Under this activity, AESB and GPO will provide training to over 1985 women and youth groups in the HOA on renewable energy and/or AVC development and having access to credit lines.
470 groups women and youth (and their members) trained on renewable energy and/or AVC development and having access to credit
362 agricultural MSMEs trained on renewable energy technologies and/or AVC development and having access to credit

## Green Climate Fund (GCF) Proposal V3.0 | PAGE 96

### Energy (Solar PV systems) to enhance energy generation

#### Activity 2.2.4: Provide technical assistance to local PFSs, local PFIs, and cooperatives as an enabling environment for credit access and sustainability
- **Activity 3.1.1:** Expand agrometeorological stations network to improve climate resilience, agricultural planning, and weather data collection.
- **Activity 3.1.2:** Integrate and disseminate climate information services into advisory services.
- **Activity 3.1.3:** Support the development of a regulatory framework and institutional arrangements to advance agricultural policies and strategies.

#### Project will support expanding agrometeorological stations network to improve climate resilience, agricultural planning, and weather data collection in Ethiopia, Kenya, and Sudan.
- The project aims to generate accurate and localized weather and climate data for advisory services. It will also integrate and disseminate climate information into advisory services using digital solution platforms established through the PPP model.

#### Investment will be directed to facilitate mainstreaming policy dialogues with stakeholders on climate resilience and insurance to advance action reforms in Ethiopia.
- There will be coordination between the National Meteorological Agency (NMA), AIF, insurance service providers, and other relevant stakeholders.

E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

203. Monitoring of the programme

All projects financed under the Facility will be monitored by the ADB's portfolio management team in accordance with relevant internal policies and procedures. The project executing agency will be responsible for daily monitoring implementation conditions and reporting periodically to the GCF under terms to be agreed between the ADB and the GCF. All projects financed under the Fund will comply with ADB standards and procedures for appraisal, approval, monitoring, and supervision, representing all or relevant teams (engineers, environmental specialists, social specialists, etc.), as well as EAS staff (e.g., project managers, finance officers, social management officers, supervision and monitoring specialists). The implementation and monitoring of each stage of the project will be guided and supervised by the ADB. The responsibility for project execution, supervision, and monitoring will be located at Headquarters and in the IGAD regional and country offices.

204. Reporting

1) Reporting by project companies to the ADB will be in line with the standard loan agreement and the ADB will conduct a semi-annual supervision.
2) AIDB reporting to the GCF: The AIDB will comply with the relevant GCF policies (as specified in the AMA) in the reporting and evaluation arrangements for this framework. The AIDB will submit the Annual Performance Report (APR) to the GCF during the seven-year implementation period of the Fund, as well as a mid-term independent evaluation report. For the TA component, reports from the beneficiaries will be consolidated by the AIDB for reporting to the GCF. In addition, an inception report and a final evaluation report, and financial information reports will be submitted (semi-annually throughout the life of the program), as agreed under the AMA and FAA.

205. Evaluation

The evaluation arrangements for this framework will be in line with the relevant AIDB and GCF policies. Both the mid-term and final independent evaluations will be carried out by the AIDB's Independent Evaluation Department (IDEV). The AIDB's independent evaluation work will be guided by internationally accepted principles for the evaluation of development assistance, in particular the Organisation for Economic Co-operation

# F.1 RISK FACTORS AND MITIGATION MEASURES (inc. 5 pages)

## F.1.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures

Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), sanctions, prohibited goods, and other risks that might prevent the project/programme objectives from being achieved. Also describe the potential impact of these risks.

For probability: High has high significant probability; Medium has moderate probability; Low has negligible probability

For impact: High has high significant impact; Medium has moderate impact; Low has negligible impact

### Selected Risk Factors

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### 206. This risk arises when agriculture, livestock, and food production systems are affected by many uncontrollable events, often related to weather variability and the environment, including excessive or insufficient rainfall, extreme temperatures, diseases and zoonoses, and land degradation. This may also be due to changes of climate conditions, biological and environmental hazards, technology levels, natural disasters, finance demand, etc.

#### 207. The mitigation measures put in place through the program include:

- Increasing access to affordable inputs for smallholders, pastoralists, and other agribusiness MSMEs to adapt to climate change.
- Enhancing resilience of livestock systems in agriculture and food production systems
- Introducing sustainable land management practices such as agroforestry and carbon farming in degraded lands
- Facilitating cost-effectiveness of integrated pest management technologies to fight against fall armyworm, arboviral diseases, etc.
- Increasing the competitiveness and resiliency of smallholder farmers, pastoralists, and institutions to adapt to climate change and extreme climates
- Promoting diversification and resilience through diversification in green-economy circular building activities.
- Integration of technological innovations in agriculture such as the Technologies for African Agricultural Transformation (TAAT) and the Africa Climate Innovation Centre (ACIC)
- Smallholder Adaptation Accelerator Program (AAAP) to scale up digital advisory services and market access
- Provision of access to improved seed varieties, pest control and surveillance services through cross-border collaboration
- Scaling up and disseminate proven agricultural technologies and innovations to millions of smallholders in Africa
- Introducing cross-border memorandums of understanding to better control transboundary animal diseases and zoonoses in border areas.
- Capacity building for smallholder farmers that promotes crop diversification and therefore reducing risks to crop diseases and weather shocks.
- Introducing social protection mechanisms such as weather-based crop/livestock insurance programs

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Medium | Medium |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 100

## Description

208. Timely access to finance is critical to cope with the debilitating impacts of climate change and extreme weather events that characterise the Hoa region. Not infrequently, smallholder farmers do not have access to credit or insurance products for purchase of inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, tools and farm implements, and to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices to cope with sudden disasters such as floods and droughts. The situation is further exacerbated by the fact that only 20% of households in the World Bank's Global Findex Database, which shows that on average only 22% of smallholder farmers have access to a formal financial institution, while only about 6% of the active farming population is able to borrow to start, operate or expand a farm or business. With such low access to credit in an environment characterised by extreme weather events and climate change, coupled with conflict and population explosion, building resilience at scale is almost impossible.

## Mitigation Measures

209. The mitigation measures put in place through the program include:

- Integration of innovative financial instruments to help "crowd-in" additional private sector finance for agriculture
- Introducing carbon credit schemes to help increase access to finance and building of the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers
- Establishing dedicated credit windows to increase access to finance to invest in different value chains
- Training farmers and business holders on the principles of business and financial planning and management
- Ensuring diversified sources of income for the participants such as through carbon farming, use of renewable energy and other production processes
- Broadening climate finance access especially for smallholder farmers, pastoralists, agribusiness M&E's etc., from different financial sources such as local PFIs, private investors, insurance products and microfinance groups.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

### Description

210. This risk can arise from changes in government sector strategies and policies, the political and governance context of countries (inequality and corruption), or conflicts with the World Bank's policy guidelines and operational procedures. It may also be due to limited interaction between agencies and stakeholders, and weak implementation capacity within Project Management Units (PMUs).

## Mitigation Measures

211. The mitigation measures put in place through the program include:

- Building communities' resilience by strengthening the security for the region for the and for five participating IGAD countries. The involved governments are maintaining adequate political commitment to ensure that the project is aligned with national development plans, sectoral strategies and policies conducive for the implementation of BREFOL.
- DRSLP: The program has been designed in a way that it addresses the challenges of insecurity and conflicts. The Program is itself expected to foster peace among conflicting communities.
- Additional capacity building/training will be provided to the PMUs already running DRSLP so as to be strengthened with the addition of specialized staff. Additional capacity building/training will be provided. AFGS will also conduct annual fiduciary clinics to address any remaining capacity gaps.

## Section 2: Risk Assessment and Mitigation Measures

### Category: AML/CFT
**Risk:** Lack of integrity among programme beneficiaries, involvement of politically exposed persons (PEPs), presence of sanctions, civil and structural conflicts.
**Probability:** Medium
**Impact:** Medium

This risk can manifest itself in scenarios where there is a lack of integrity amongst programme beneficiaries, the involvement of politically exposed persons (PEPs), the presence of sanctions, civil and structural conflicts. Such situations may lead to financial irregularities, illegal activities, and corruption. The Bank has implemented various measures to mitigate this risk, including due diligence procedures, compliance with regulatory background checks, and AML/CFT policies.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- **Integrity assessment:** An integrity assessment was conducted on the executing agencies.
- **Due diligence:** Implemented through the program management unit.
- **Sanctions monitoring:** The Bank monitors its own sanctions lists during supervision activities. This is also strengthened by daily automated sanctions, PEPs, and adverse media monitoring.

### Category: Risk of uneven uptake among countries
**Risk:** Challenges in the use of resources during implementation, leading to uneven uptake across countries.
**Probability:** Medium
**Impact:** Low

There may be challenges with the use of resources during implementation, which could lead to uneven uptake. For example, if a country has low take-up rates for funds allocated to it, this may affect the climate impact of the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- **Flexibility in resource reallocation:** Flexibility is provided to reallocate funds to most secure areas of the country in case of conflict and security issues. This measure will be implemented if necessary, as last option after the mid-term review to transfer resources to most competitive countries.

G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

215. BREFOL is a climate change adaptation operation that aims to improve the resilience of communities and agricultural production systems with minimal negative environmental and social impacts. As a result of the programme’s design, it is expected that site-specific programme activities are expected to have only moderate environmental and social risks and impacts that can be managed through the instruments proposed in the programme's environmental and social management framework (ESMF). The ESMF includes Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA), Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMP) and Environmental and Social Monitoring Plan (ESMP). The ESMF was developed by the Bank’s team, reviewed by the participating countries and disclosed both in country and on the Bank's website in accordance with the 30-day disclosure requirement before the BREFOL was submitted to the ADB Board of Directors for consideration.

216. BREFOL will have a significant positive impact on the environment and communities in the Horn of Africa region, particularly through (i) improved access to land and water resources; (ii) improved access to infrastructure for water, animal health and access to markets; (iii) rehabilitation ranges and pastures; (iv) improved productivity/productivity in livestock, crop, forestry and pastoral sectors; (v) increased income of farmers and pastoralists using climate services; and (vi) conserve rangelands and soils and prevent desertification.

217. The disclosed ESMF (see Annex 6 for details) outlines measures to minimise and mitigate the potential environmental and social risks associated with BREFOL. The implementation of environmental measures will involve various stakeholders, including governments, local authorities, communities, farmers, pastoralists, civil society organisations, private sector actors, and research institutes, etc. The environmental monitoring of the programme will be carried out by the entity responsible for project implementation.

G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

218. Information from the gender assessment reports and consultations with stakeholders during the assessment mission in the five participating countries indicate that women in male- and female-headed households face significant barriers to accessing productive resources, credit facilities, markets, etc. These barriers include harmful traditional practices, low levels of literacy and less power over intra- and extra-household decision-making processes. Women also have limited access to land and water resources, economic empowerment and gender equality as a whole. In addition, women have less access to social infrastructure such as schools, health facilities and markets than men do. This is because women are often active in the labour market. If these challenges are not addressed during programme implementation, they could lead to increased poverty for women and undermine their ability to participate fully and equally and undermine gender equality. For this reason, BREFOL has budgeted for a gender action plan at regional and sub-regional levels that will address these issues through capacity building of women’s groups to transform harmful and disadvantageous socio-cultural gender norms and practices that significantly hinder women's empowerment and gender equality. Based on the regional components of the programme, the ESMF methodology, BREFOL has identified three main areas for implementing women’s economic empowerment activities. In these activities, women will act as the main agents of change in their communities. The first area is to improve access to productive resources through planned by the programme this role could increase women's workload, as women are already responsible for most of the agricultural and household tasks. The second area is to promote gender-sensitive infrastructure that would reduce women’s workload and poverty, and remove barriers to children’s education. This includes improved water supply systems, irrigation schemes, roads in residential areas, solar energy systems and bio-digesters. Of the 1.6 million people who will benefit from climate change adaptation services, 30% will be women (including 25% of rural women aged 18-35). In addition, a climate risk insurance scheme will be established through a public

## Private partnership to de-risk existing and agribusiness MSEs in the project areas (see Annex 16 for proposed intervention areas).

### 219., 221.
In the Bank's gender marker system, BREFOL is classified as GENII. This category indicates that, by the end of the programme period, the programme is expected to have generated several positive outcomes in terms of increased access and control over resources for women, including improved livestock and pastoral value chains (the output indicator for this outcome is the increase in annual per capita income for economically active women). The programme will also contribute to improving women's economic empowerment by increasing their access to credit and other financial services. The programme is also expected to improve women's access to social services and infrastructure, which will reduce time poverty and allow them more time to engage in productive activities such as livestock rearing and diversification activities. A general gender analysis and the corresponding gender action plan with a regional budget allocation guide are provided as Annex 8 to the FP. In summary, it is envisaged that BREFOL will make significant contributions towards achieving the SDGs by 2030 in the project areas by improving their access to productive resources and gender-sensitive infrastructure and services.

### 220.
AIDF's fiduciary and financial management measures for BREFOL in upstream engagement with programme countries during the preparation of Country Strategy Papers (CSPs), where fiduciary risks are assessed, and in downstream engagement with programme countries after approval as a key document for dialogue with the country on the risk assessment and agreed mitigation actions to address these risks. The AE has also carried out an integrity assessment using the principles of integrity due diligence, naming and shaming, and sanctions, criminal and regulatory background and AML/CFT of the EEs.

### 221.
Generally, the Financial Management Arrangement (FMA) of BREFOL requires:

- **Accounting Management:** Legal agreements require BREFOL participating countries to: (i) maintain separate accounts for the programme or sub-project under BREFOL and subprojects financed from AIDB resources and resources administered by the AID; and (ii) establish accounting, administrative, and reporting systems that are consistent with the FMA. The AE will ensure the use of funds disbursed to the programme or project and be prepared in accordance with applicable accounting standards.

- **Accounting standards and practices:** Although accounting standards and practices may vary from country to country, the accounts of the programme or sub-project under BREFOL, irrespective of the system used by the country, should provide a complete picture of the financial position. The AE will ensure comprehensive information on the following aspects: 1) accountability for the funds provided to the programme or sub-project; these funds may include the proceeds of AIDB loan or grant, or other resources from the AID; 2) transparency and disclosure of expenditure and specific information on components financed under the programme.

### 222.
**Programme Financial Statements:** The programme or sub-project financial statements prepared under BREFOL should:

- For all projects: i. Statement of Income (duties received from AIDB, counterpart funds and, where applicable, co-finances) and Expenditure (expenditures incurred both for the current year and accumulated over previous years); ii. Balance Sheet (assets and liabilities at the end of each financial year; balances); iii. Statement of Special Accounts(s); iv. Notes to the Financial Statements describing the accounting principles used and a detailed analysis of the main accounts.

- For revenue-generating sub-projects under BREFOL (in addition to the requirements above): i) a balance sheet showing assets, liabilities, and net worth at the end of each financial year; ii) other assets and liabilities of the project at the end of each financial year; iii) a profit and loss account (or an operating
