## Financial Reporting and Documentation Requirements

### As an annex to the financial statements prepared under BREFOL, the following would be included as an annex to the financial statements of all projects under the programme: i) a reconciliation between the project accounts and the disbursement account; ii) a cash flow statement showing the cash flows during each financial year.

#### Financial reporting:
BREFOL participating countries are required to provide regular and comprehensive accounting and financial information on the programme and sub-projects under the programme. The format of the financial statements for the grant and loan programmes and financial systems and financial communication reports may vary depending on the type of sub-project, lending instrument and country. The ADB may not require a standard format but will ensure that reports submitted are in accordance with the requirements.

#### Retention and filing of documents:
Each country participating in the BREFOL programme is required to maintain and preserve programme/sub-project documents and supporting documents and to make them available for audit purposes at any time.

#### Documentation of compliance:
The ADB's Country Conditions require each country participating in the programme to retain all documents relating to the loan proceeds so that they can be audited by external auditors or the ADB's representative. Documentation should be retained in a manner that is readily accessible and retrievable.

### Audit: The general conditions of the loan and grant agreements require that all programme and sub-project accounts be audited annually in accordance with relevant generally accepted standards. According to the terms of reference for the audit, the auditor is required to examine the financial statements or projects, or the auditor, is required, among other things, to examine relevant documents, review internal controls, assess the reliability of the information provided by the borrower, evaluate the efficiency of the project, form an opinion on the quality of the financial statements, and confirm that the funds have been used in accordance with the terms of the loan or grant agreement. Wherever the accounts of the project or programme audited by qualified and independent auditors who meet ADB requirements, in countries where there is no such requirement for the Auditor General's Office (AGO) under the law, the AGO may be invited to review the audit report prepared by the auditor; provided that the matter is discussed during programme/approval and loan negotiations, and the audit report is submitted to the AGO at least 30 days before the submission of the audited BREFOL-financed project/programme. The auditor's financial statements, auditor's report and management letter should be retained by the borrower for a period of five years from the end of the financial year to which they relate. Failure to comply with this requirement will result in the immediate suspension of disbursements under the Disbursement Account.

## Procurement and Disbursement Arrangements

### Procurement Arrangements:
The procurement of goods (including services other than consultancy services), works, and the provision of consultancy services for BREFOL-financed projects will be carried out in accordance with the Procurement Framework for Bank Group-financed Operations, 2015 Edition, and in compliance with the provisions of the GCF F&A (see Annex 10 for the detailed procurement procedures). The ADB may also require that the borrower adopt other procurement methods such as Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs), based on the relevant Standard Bidding Documents (SBDs) issued by the World Bank. It is important to note that the type of procurement method adopted must be the most appropriate. Indeed, following an analysis of the procurement systems of the five BREFOL participating countries, the procurement systems and procedures of Djibouti, Somalia and South...

## 224. Disbursement: The disbursement methods to be used in mobile project funding are:
(i) the direct payment method;
(ii) the special fund/revolving fund method; and
(iii) the reimbursement method.

Direct payments will be made by AIDB to contractors, suppliers, and sub-suppliers in accordance with AIDB procurement rules and procedures and national procurement laws. The special fund/revolving fund method is used for project funds that are disbursed directly to contractors and suppliers in accordance with AIDB procurement rules and procedures and national procurement laws. The reimbursement method will be used when eligible expenditures of all project funds have been incurred by the contractor, supplier or sub-supplier and submitted to AIDB together with a bank acceptable to the AIDB.

The opening of a special account with the central banks of the five participating countries is required for the disbursement of project funds. The opening of such an account will be subject to the approval of the central bank of each country concerned, which may require that the account be opened in conjunction with a bank acceptable to the AIDB.

The opening of the bank account will be a condition precedent to the first disbursement. The reimbursement method will be used when eligible expenditures of all project funds have been incurred by the contractor, supplier or sub-supplier and submitted to AIDB together with a bank acceptable to the AIDB.

### 225. Conditions Precedent to First Disbursement of Grants and Loan under BREFOL: The obligation of AIDB to make the first disbursement of the respective grants and loan shall be subject to the entry into force of the Project Agreement (PA) and Letter of Agreement (LA), and the submission by the Borrower with the provisions set out in the PA and LA, in a form and substance acceptable to AIDB, of all documents required under the PA and LA.

i. Submission of evidence of the establishment of the National Program Steering Committee (NPSC) for each country, or Regional Program Management Unit (RPMU) for IGAD countries, with qualifications and terms of reference acceptable to AIDB.
ii. Provision of Counterpart Contribution, as stipulated in the respective loan/grant agreements.

### 226. Underwriting: The Borrower Recipient undertakes:
- to carry out the Program in accordance with the Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPS), and the Bank's Safeguards Policies and the applicable national legislation in a manner and in substance satisfactory to the AIDB.

# Appendix 2: Technologies for African Agriculture Transformation (TAAT)

## 2.1 TAAT as an AIDB Flagship Program

### Introduction to TAAT

#### Overview of TAAT's Approach

- TAAT is a flagship program that offers a wide range of technology brokerage services to assist African countries.
- The services include the latest climate-smart technologies, their accompanying management practices, and market linkages for value addition.

#### Focus Areas

- TAAT focuses on improving productivity in 18 agricultural value chains within eight priority intervention areas: namely:
    - self-sufficiency in staple food crops
    - transforming African Savannas into breadbaskets
    - revitalizing tree plantations
    - expanding horticulture
    - increasing livestock production and productivity
- TAAT works alongside six enabler domains that address broader issues such as soil fertility management, water management, and capacity development.

#### Partnerships

- TAAT's partnership with the Consultative Group on International Agricultural Research (CGIAR) Centres and Advanced Agricultural Research Institutes, the world’s largest global agricultural innovation network.
- CGIAR Centres work in concert with the National Agricultural Research and Extension Systems of African countries, associations of Agripreneurs.

#### Climate-Smart Agriculture

- TAAT spearheads the adoption of innovative soil and water conservation techniques such as Zai, Half-moons, and Contour Lines Technique. These soil and water conservation practices are being demonstrated in areas such as the Sahel region and dry areas in East Africa.
- TAAT promotes the use of cost-effective and easily deployable irrigation and water management technologies.

#### Drought-Tolerant Maize Varieties

- The MAIZE-Maize Project, led by the African Agricultural Technology Foundation (AATF), has developed drought-tolerant maize varieties that were cultivated on 5.2 million hectares on 841,000 hectares.
- As a result of this project, 379,600 metric tons of maize with a value of USD 107 million was produced.

#### Market Linkages and Strategic Partnerships

- TAAT facilitates linkages between farmers and agro-processors, grain off-takers, and millers to produce value-added products such as maize flour and stockfeed.


---
**Page 127**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 119

## TAAT’s Impact on Wheat Production

### Wheat Activities: In Ethiopia, following the recent successful and nationwide deployment of heat-tolerant wheat varieties, the wheat area was rapidly expanded from less than 5,000 ha in 2018/19 to 187,246 ha in 2020/21 and 653,500 ha in 2021/22. The average yields of wheat increased on average by about 20% between 2018–2021. Ethiopia now produces over one million metric tonnes in 2022, and the country achieved self-sufficiency in wheat production. Ethiopia says it is a net exporter of wheat for the first time.

### Strategic Partnerships: TAAT’s success in Ethiopia was largely due to strategic partnerships with key stakeholders, including the Ethiopian government, CGIAR, and private sector actors. The program also received significant funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other donors.

## Sustainable Livestock Production

### Comprehensive Livestock Technologies: TAAT's involvement in sustainable livestock production includes feed formulation, feed mixers, slaughter equipment, rotational grazing, eco-friendly production equipment. The program also emphasizes research on high-performance animal breeds with enhanced heat tolerance and disease resistance. It promotes the use of locally available feed resources for livestock feed production.

### Sustainable Horticulture and Vegetable Production

#### Climate-Resilient Varieties and Precision Farming: TAAT through its compact led by WorldVeg focuses on introducing heat-tolerant, high-yield, and disease-resistant varieties in horticulture and vegetable production. The program also includes the development of early warning systems for sustainable horticulture and vegetable production.

#### Sustainable Staple Crops Production

### Climate-Resilient Varieties: TAAT focuses on sustainable intensification of adapted cassava, maize, rice, and soy seed varieties. The program advocates for the use of improved short-cycle varieties in staple crops to enhance heat tolerance and yield stability under climate change conditions. It emphasizes the production of early generation seeds (EGS) and the construction of Semi-Automatic Hydroponics Technology (SAHT) centers for rapid multiplication of seed and demo trials.

### Innovation and Smart Agriculture

#### Climate Forecasting: TAAT leverages remote sensing technologies for informed decision-making, including the Risk Sharing Facility (RSF), crop insurance schemes are introduced to attract private sector participation in staple crops production. The program also includes the development and deployment of monitoring systems leveraging remote sensing technologies for informed decision-making.

### Summary

In summary, TAAT’s multifaceted approach encompasses innovative farming techniques, adoption of climate-resilient varieties, strategic partnerships, and capacity building through training and technology transfer. This comprehensive strategy is instrumental in deploying heat-tolerant crop varieties and technologies, ensuring sustainable productivity, improving income for farmers, and addressing challenges related to climate change in the agricultural sector. The program’s impact across various sectors and geographical domains showcase its vital role in advancing sustainable practices across the African continent.

Monitoring and Evaluation

The Monitoring and Evaluation of Agri-Science Uptake in Research and Extension (MPRO) is a well-thought-out monitoring and evaluation system providing a new cutting-edge data collection architecture that uses analytical tools and latest methods for tracking progress and assessing performance results based on the M&E framework. The system consists of a powerful, user-friendly, web-based, and mobile powerf
orce data collection interface, the tool uses innovative analytical tools to showcase performance results in real-time. This innovative approach is expected to significantly improve the effectiveness of the project and its impact. The MPRO solution is positively impacting smallholder farmer behaviours that lead to transformative change. This innovation will be shared with other projects and countries through a digital platform, providing a scalable solution, offering valuable insights through an intuitive, interactive dashboard. The project's M&E architecture has been designed to support the implementation of the project’s M&E budget, this new digital tool MPRO will be applied to this project and used to inform management decisions on how best to allocate resources for maximum impact. The M&E system involves ensuring data is correctly and timely gathered on adaptation related activities in each participating country. The MPRO Platform has been designed to encourage reliable reuse and release of the M&E data, which will be shared with other projects and countries through a digital platform, providing a scalable solution, offering valuable insights through an intuitive, interactive dashboard. The M&E system involves ensuring data is correctly and timely gathered on adaptation related activities in each participating country. The MPRO Platform has been designed to encourage reliable reuse and release of the M&E data, which will be shared with other projects and countries through a digital platform, providing a scalable solution, offering valuable insights through an intuitive, interactive dashboard.

2.2 Affirmative Financial Action for Women in Africa (AFAWA)

Affirmative Financial Action for Women in Africa (AFAWA) is a pan-African initiative to bridge the USD 42 billion financing gap facing women in all countries in Africa, with the aim of strengthening enabling environments and increasing access to financial services. AFAWA is implemented by the African Development Bank (concessionary rate). AFAWA adopts a holistic approach through three pillars: AFAWA finance leverages the AfDB’s financial resources to provide concessional loans to women entrepreneurs at competitive interest rates in the M&E area. In this regard, AFAWA will be fully integrated with the AfDB’s existing lending and investment landscape in Africa and to create incentives for lending to women in business; (ii) USD 350 million risk-sharing facility to support financial institutions in providing credit to women entrepreneurs in Africa; (iii) USD 1 billion technical assistance fund to support capacity building of financial institutions, governments, and other stakeholders. This mechanism will build upon the existing network of commercial banks and microfinance institutions to create structural change and lasting impact for women, and (i) rating system to evaluate financial institutions’ performance in providing credit to women entrepreneurs in Africa.

Top institutions will be rewarded with preferential financing terms from AfDB. AFAWA technical assistance fund will support capacity building of financial institutions, governments, and other stakeholders by strengthening their product portfolios for women and strengthens the capacity of women entrepreneurs through training and mentoring. The technical assistance fund will also provide a range of advisory services to financial institutions to ensure successful implementation of their product portfolios for women in Africa. AFAWA is expected to increase access to finance for women-owned businesses, improve productivity and growth. AFAWA enabling environment engages with African Governments and other key stakeholders to address the legal, policy and regulatory reforms and shine down the structural barriers impeding women in business.

Through the Bank’s commitment to fostering access to financial and technical support for Women-led Small and Medium Enterprises (WSMEs), AFAWA achieved significant missions in 2023. In year of resilience and recovery, AFAWA continued its efforts to provide WSMEs with access to finance across countries; AFAWA unlocked a cumulative lending of about USD 1.6 billion by leveraging on the Bank’s

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (3.0) PAGE 121

## Enhancing Visibility and Empowering Financial Institutions: Through 2023, AFAWI extended its main business lines to include financial inclusion and women’s empowerment in the African Finance Series, in partnership with the African Guarantee Fund (AGF) in Ghana (February), Angola (March), Mozambique (April), and Namibia (May). The series was supported by the European Union Central Banks for crucial actions and reforms, resulting cumulatively in the training of over 500 bankers and financial sector professionals. AFAWI also launched a new initiative to train over two days, emphasizing smart-gender investing and building strong value propositions for WMSMEs.

### Strengthening the ecosystem and Championing Reform Advocacy: AFAWI has played a pivotal role in policy reform, currently supporting 20 member countries in developing gender-responsive policies for WMSMEs. The Bank also supported the development of the Women’s Empowerment Principles (WEPs) in Africa, which was launched at the World Economic Forum in Davos.

### On special projects, the Bank through AFAWI signed an agreement in February 2023 with Femmes Alliance Solidaire under the African Union Empowering women in agriculture initiative to capacitate women farmers and increase their productivity. The project aims to train 150 women farmers to increase their cashflow production from 500 to 3,000 metric tonnes while meeting the quality standards required by international buyers. During a month of training, 200 women were trained in the region.

### In addition, Special Assistance grants with the Government of Mauritania to support its goals of supporting access to finance for WMSMEs in Mauritania, Mali, Guinea, and Senegal. The grant will be used to provide financial services to over 15,000 women entrepreneurs through the Zanzani Microfinance Bank (ZMB) and the National Guarantee for Growth (NGG) program.

### The Bank together with AGF went on a business development mission to Djibouti in March 2024 to explore opportunities for AFAWI through the financial sector and potentially at government level. With the support of the African Development Bank, the Bank is exploring the possibility that some colleagues from AGF are discussing with some of the financial institutions who have expressed initial interest in supporting women entrepreneurs.

### The Bank also supported the launch of the Women’s Empowerment Awards in 2023 and the African Bankers’ Award has integrated a new award category, namely the AFAWI Bank of the Year Award. This award recognizes banks that are committed to empowering WMSMEs through their WSMES as an integrated part of their SME strategy.

### Pathways towards Unlocking USD $5 billion and Bridging the Gender Financial Gap in Africa: Looking ahead, AFAWI will continue to focus on its core mandate of supporting women entrepreneurs. The approach will guide AFAWI’s efforts on fostering lasting partnerships and synergies while accelerating mainstreaming AFAWI into the bank’s operations across the sectors and the regions.

### In 2024, AFAWI intends to scale up its impact by focusing on three key areas: (i) scaling up investments towards WSMES; (ii) supporting women-led SMEs in Africa while ensuring a greater impact and inclusive growth for years to come.


---
**Page 130**
---

### The Africa Adaptation Acceleration Program (AAAP)

The AAAP is an initiative to scale up adaptation efforts and mobilise USD 25 billion to support climate-resilient development in Africa. Led by the African Development Bank (AfDB) and the Global Climate Adaptation Fund, it aims to help 100 million smallholder farmers of Africa adapt to the impacts of climate change. It comprises several pillars addressing critical areas such as agriculture and food security, water resources management, energy access, urban resilience, disaster risk reduction, climate adaptation and resilience, and innovative financing initiatives.

#### Agriculture and food security pillar

Agriculture is vital to Africa's economies, employing more than half the continent’s population. However, climate change poses significant challenges to the continent's agriculture, affecting food security and income, particularly for women and rural households. Climate change also increases the risk of crop failure and livestock losses, leading to decreases in yields and incomes. However, some 250 million people, including smallholder farmers and pastoralists, lack access to digital agricultural services. The Agriculture and Food Security Pillar aims to scale up climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices by providing access to affordable insurance and financial services for at least 30 million farmers in Africa by 2025, leading to improved food security for at least 180 million people.

#### Building Resilience for Food and Nutrition Security in the Horn of Africa

This project aims to build resilience against climate change impacts on food and nutrition security in the Horn of Africa. It will ensure the integration of climate-smart digital technologies for adaptation into this project. This includes several components: a national CSA strategy, a national CSA action plan, a national CSA investment framework, technical reports on agricultural insurance support, and national multi-stakeholder workshops to identify key digital solutions. The project will also provide training in digital tools for 10 million farmers and pastoralists and 15 million livestock through the use of gender-responsive service delivery models. It aims to create 300,000 jobs for youth and women, while cultivating 170,000 hectares using digital climate-smart solutions.

#### National Integrated Livestock Development Programme in Senegal Stage I (PIDNES I), €500,000

The objective of the programme is to contribute to the development of competitive, climate-resilient and job-generating livestock value chains. It will do this by supporting the implementation of a range of high-value-added activities. These include conducting an evidence-based assessment of PIDNES' climate vulnerability to inform adaptation strategies for the livestock sector in Senegal as it responds to climate change. In addition, AAAP has analysed the climate vulnerability of value chain actors to inform recommendations on how to build resilience into the project. The programme will also invest in digital tools for adaptation; facilitated multi-stakeholder dialogues and actions to increase community awareness about climate risks and opportunities; and training and capacity building for professional associations on the use of digital tools to build resilience in the livestock sector.

#### Programme to Build Resilience to Food and Nutrition Insecurity in the Sahel (P2R P3RS, €409,163)

The programme aims to contribute to the improvement of living conditions and food and nutrition security in the Sahel region. It will do this by supporting the development and implementation of climate information services, in particular by promoting public-private partnerships to facilitate the sustainable provision of these services; increasing access to climate information for smallholder farmers through opportunities for young people. In Niger, the project supported the organisation of a regional fair on best practices in climate change adaptation and resilience building, including digital solutions. In The Gambia, the project focused on upgrading the electronic farmer registry system and providing training to 100 extension officers and 250 smallholder farmers and agritech providers; and supporting the development and implementation of national frameworks for climate information services. In Burkina Faso, the programme supported the development and use of digital technologies to promote climate resilience and adaptation in the target countries and regional entities covered by the project.


---
**Page 131**
---

Programmes for Integrated Development and Adaptation to Climate Change in the Zambezi River Basin (PIDAZAMBEZI, EUR 147,937). In this African Development Bank investment, the AAAP has made significant progress towards its goal of developing a set of climate-resilient wheat value chain solutions suitable for climate adaptation and to highlight their applicability in different sectors. It has also deepened understanding of the challenges and opportunities associated with using digital tools for climate change adaptation. The AAAP has developed a range of innovative climate-resilient wheat solutions, opportunities, the AAAP has strengthened climate adaptation efforts through digital technologies. It has also developed a set of climate-resilient wheat value chain solutions suitable for climate adaptation in agriculture and digital solutions for climate adaptation. AAAP also facilitated training sessions on key topics such as climate change adaptation, climate-smart agricultural practices, sustainable land management, sustainable water management, and climate adaptation strategies, including regional climate insurance and weather forecasting.

Climate Resilient Wheat Value Chain Development Project (CREW: €160,000). AAAPs contribution to the CREW project is to introduce climate adaptation measures and integrate digital adaptation solutions throughout the wheat value chain. This includes assessing local climatic conditions and food security, developing a set of climate-resilient wheat value chain solutions suitable for climate adaptation, and using digital extension mechanisms. In addition, AAAP supports capacity building of extension agents and policymakers in Ethiopia to ensure that they are able to effectively implement climate-smart agricultural practices. The project aims to increase the resilience of Ethiopia's wheat production system, improve wheat production and productivity, and reduce food insecurity.

Mikindoni Njewaswazi Water Augmentation Programme (MNWAP: €248,952). The overall objective of MNWAP is to improve food security, poverty reduction and climate change resilience by supporting the development of a water augmentation system in the Mikindoni sub-county. This will lead to an increase in production and productivity in the target region. Increased production will lead to higher incomes for producers and improved livelihoods for households. The project aims to reduce poverty through increased agricultural productivity and growth in the agricultural sector, and significantly improve food and nutritional security and incomes by providing climate risk assessments to improve the project. The project will transform approximately 100 hectares of land into irrigated land.

Technical assistance: Stakeholder workshops were conducted in all regions of Africa to prioritize digital extension mechanisms for climate change adaptation. These workshops resulted in a set of training aimed to inform and strengthen the capacity of selected public and private actors, enabling them to effectively implement climate-smart agricultural practices. The project also aims to promote the adoption of CCAs in their respective regions. The training focused on the design and implementation of digital extension mechanisms needed to reach the last mile, thereby empowering farmers to improve food security and climate resilience. To date, more than 500 participants have been trained in South, East, West and Central Africa.

Secretariat's assessment of FP239

Proposal name: Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BREFOL)

Accredited entity: African Development Bank (AfDB)

Country(s): Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and South Sudan

Project/programme size: Large

### Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- The programme proposes innovative solutions to improve, at scale, the adaptive capacity of communities in the Horn of Africa through increased productivity and resilience of local agricultural and food systems and improved management of rangeland and agricultural landscapes.
- Focus on agriculture-based enterprises especially small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), and the deployment of innovative financial instruments such as microfinance and simple co-financing with selected private investors and intermediary financial institutions will help to stimulate investments for building climate resilience through transformation of local agricultural and food systems and management of rangeland ecosystems in the Horn of Africa region.
- Implementation through both national and regional institutions is commendable because it leverages on existing capacities, structures and governance mechanisms in the Horn of Africa while contributing to build the region's capacity at all levels.

#### Points of caution
- As three of the five countries are currently affected by conflict and social and institutional fragility, there are additional risks associated with the proposed implementation of the programme. Several mitigation measures, including separate disbursement rules per country and mobilization clauses, have been included in the design.


---
**Page 147**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Programme background

The Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BREFOIL) programme is a large-scale intervention aimed at reducing climate-induced food insecurity in the Horn of Africa region, which has been experiencing increasingly frequent and severe food-insecure conditions arising from various hazards in the region. The objective of the programme is to build resilience against these shocks by strengthening local food systems that are part of the Intergovernmental Authority on Development, the region’s economic bloc, namely Djibouti, Ethiopia, Eritrea, Kenya, Somalia and Sudan. The programme will support countries to prepare adequately for and manage food insecurity challenges caused by climate change in the Horn of Africa.

The Horn of Africa region is home to approximately 250 million people who are highly dependent on the local ecosystems for their survival, 64 per cent of whom live in areas prone to extreme weather conditions. The region has experienced severe droughts and floods over the recent past, and the need of emergency food assistance to meet basic needs continues to rise. Moreover, the region has experienced high inter-annual rainfall variability, along with a consistently increasing temperature trend that has led to reduced yields and increased risk to crop productivity. This has resulted in farmers experiencing declining crop yields and some areas becoming more vulnerable to desertification.

The programme will overcome these challenges by helping to transition the local food systems of the region from monoculture-based production systems to mixed species mix of varieties compared with current systems, and by supporting communities to manage and produce an array of crops that are better suited for climate change. The programme will also benefit indirect beneficiaries. Through capacity-building and the establishment of the new agroforestry systems, communities in the region will be able to diversify their livelihoods and become more resilient. They will also benefit from access to finance through a network of local financial institutions to help reinvest an endowment into the local food system.

The programme will deliver mitigation co-benefits in the form of reduced and avoided emissions that will be achieved through activities aimed at increasing energy access through improved energy efficiency, increased renewable energy generation, improved forest cover and land-use conditions.

The programme is requested USD 151 million of GCF Financing, structured in USD 97.90 million grant component and USD 53.10 million co-financing component, including co-financing, with USD 335.30 million with co-financing primarily in the form of grants and USD 248.60 million as loans.

In terms of environmental and social safeguards (ESS), this proposal has been categorized as environmental and social (E&S) risk category C. The review by the Secretariat confirms that there are no significant risks to the ESS, and therefore recommends that the programme be re-categorized as E&S risk category B.


---
**Page 148**
---

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Strengthening pastoral and agropastoral production systems' resilience to climate change (total cost: USD 687.70 million; GCF cost: USD 69.08 million)

- **Component 1.1:** Developing climate-resilient infrastructure, supporting the sustainable management of agricultural land, and improving access to climate information services.
- **Component 1.2:** Complementarity between the GCF and MDRB investments in an integrated approach (infrastructure development, technology transfer, and institutional strengthening) for transforming pastoral and agropastoral production systems in response to climate change.

## Component 2: Supporting low-carbon and resilient development of agribusinesses (total cost: USD 91.30 million; GCF cost: USD 64.56 million)

- **Component 2.1:** Development of agribusinesses through increased access to digital advisory services, markets, and supporting the development of inclusive MSMEs and cooperatives with access to climate financing.
- **Component 2.2:** Strengthening the capacity of agropastoral communities' adaptive response to climate change by providing significant financial support for women's groups, especially those with limited access such as youth and women. Investments in agribusinesses are also expected to contribute significantly to food security.

## Component 3: Strengthening of agropastoral communities' capacity to adapt to climate change (total cost: USD 26.66 million; GCF cost: USD 9.37 million)

- **Component 3.1:** Supporting the strengthening of agropastoral communities' adaptive response by developing climate-resilient infrastructure, strengthening operational capacities for climate regulatory framework reforms, and institutional strengthening.

## Component 4: Programme coordination and knowledge management (total cost: USD 28.95 million; GCF cost: USD 7.58 million)

- **Component 4.1:** Ensuring that monitoring and evaluation costs have been adequately provided for, and specific activities have been planned and integrated within the design of the programme to ensure that project implementation and monitoring and evaluation processes will be carried out.

## Gender and environment and social management framework aspects

- A costed environmental and social management framework (ESMF) was developed for each of the five countries. The ESMF includes a series of activities that will sufficiently ensure that ESS are put in place and observed throughout the programme's lifespan.

---

### III. Assessment against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The programme presents an innovative structure that will help unlock resilience-building opportunities and benefits for vulnerable poor communities in the Horn of Africa region. The programme will support 4.68 million direct beneficiaries and 16.37 million indirect beneficiaries, with a focus on promoting green investments in agriculture, water management, and climate-smart innovations and technologies that will help to transform their local food systems and livelihoods. This will enable these communities to increase the productivity of their land and capacity to be able to spread their food production and livelihood strategies in a manner that will allow for sustainable use of the agricultural lands on which they depend.

The programme will also deliver significant environmental benefits, with an estimated impact of 10.87 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO2eq) in 25 years, particularly from activities that will lead to the benefits of increasing community access to clean energy and enabling communities to attain enhanced ecosystem services from sustainable forest land and land management practices.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

BREFEG is an innovative and strategic approach to promoting and increasing the participation of the private sector in the development and implementation of climate change adaptation initiatives across the Horn of Africa region, building on lessons and outputs of outcomes of the programme.

The programme will adopt a strategic approach to enhancing the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities in the Horn of Africa region by focusing on promoting green investments that are inclusive and gender-sensitive. The focus is on empowering marginalised groups: women, girls and youth. This can help to ensure the long-term security and sustainability of livelihoods for these groups as they adapt to climate change impacts, including extreme weather events and climate shocks and impacts. The livelihood opportunities and diversification will further help to improve food security by increasing access to markets and improving natural resources in the target areas, thereby improving food and water security in the region.

The programme promotes an incentive-based mechanism to create an enabling environment for private sector investment in climate change adaptation initiatives through a combination of innovative tools such as credit lines, weather-based insurance mechanisms and investment guarantees. The financial instruments will be designed to ensure that they will generate an increase in the baseline of financing from private sector investors by up to USD 872 million over five years. This will help to scale-up and replicate climate change adaptation initiatives across the energy and agriculture, forest, and other land-use sectors in the Horn of Africa.

The paradigm shift potential is well embedded across all three components and is well designed to be influential over the long term.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The sustainable development potential is embedded in all components of the programme and places vulnerable communities at the centre of efforts to strengthen livelihoods


---
**Page 150**
---

# 3. Needs of the recipient

The programme targets one of the world’s most disaster-prone regions, where debilitating livelihoods arise from various hazards which are exacerbated by the high political fragility of the region.

The communities are facing severe challenges from climate change threatening their current food security and yet lack the financial resources and access to credit facilities to purchase farm inputs such as climate-adapted seed varieties, fertilizers and farm implements. These communities also face a range of other hazards including droughts, floods, hazards and the recurrent food crises, hunger and famine that characterise the region. The current food systems are not resilient or adaptive enough to cope with these multiple hazards and climate change. The proposed interventions, through agribusiness development and the credit lines and weather-based insurance mechanisms will help communities access a range of climate-smart options, build food systems and ecosystems which are resilient and diverse and foster longer-term food systems and landscapes, thereby building resilience and adaptive capacity.

The countries and beneficiaries are currently struggling to access finance to support the transition towards more sustainable livelihoods. Without adequate access to finance and investments, the local food systems will not be able to provide vulnerable communities with a range of climate-smart options that can help them bridge this important gap through working with key regional, national and local institutions in both the private and public sectors, and will help in fostering long-term solutions through financial innovation, building


---
**Page 151**
---

3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

28. Country ownership is well embedded in the programme, which is fully aligned with the climate change and broader development aspirations, strategies and policies of all five target countries. The GCF country strategy for each of these countries has been developed through a strong partnership and commitment of the governments of the five target countries to the programme activities and outputs. The GCF country strategy also places an important focus on ensuring better synergy with ongoing regional and national strategies and programmes in the five target countries.

29. The programme is aligned with the National Determined Contributions (NDCs), National Adaptation Plans (NAPs), National Adaptation Plan of Action (NAPA)s, and the GCF country strategy for each of these countries. The GCF country strategy has been developed with the strong participation and commitment of the governments of the five target countries to the programme activities and outputs.

30. The GEF, through its long-standing presence in the region and has a wealth of experience working with stakeholders and dealing with the development and environmental challenges in all five countries, will provide technical assistance (TA) to the GCF country strategy. This will facilitate meaningful engagement and involvement of all relevant stakeholders in the target countries in the successful implementation and scaling up of the programme activities and outputs.

31. The architecture of the programme consists of the governments of the five target countries, the European Union (EU), and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). This will bolster the integration of local contexts and ownership into the programme activities and outputs.

32. The programme is built on well-designed stakeholder consultations that were conducted throughout the programme development process, ensuring local knowledge, innovation and sustainability, and participation is fully embedded in the design of the programme.


3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

15. The programme will directly support poor and vulnerable communities in the Horn of Africa region to strengthen, diversify, and transform their livelihoods through agro-ecosystem systems. It will target 480 million direct beneficiaries and 15.55 million indirect beneficiaries and will contribute to increasing access to markets and greater access to climate-resilient techniques and climate resilient infrastructure.

16. The funding amount is well justified to provide the support needed to unlock the potential of agro-ecosystem systems in the Horn of Africa region, and to promote innovative approaches and lessons for transforming the agricultural and food systems of the entire Horn of Africa Region. The programme will also contribute to increasing access to markets and greater market linkages through the structuring and deployment of various innovative financial instruments and mechanisms including credit lines, weather-based insurance, and other green blended finance.


---
**Page 152**
---

and through collaboration with MOHES, intermediary financial institutions and other private sector actors to develop capacity building, technology adoption and market access tools for a wide variety of key products and value chains in the proposed enhanced agricultural and agribusiness systems. The programme thus fosters an innovative approach to help create long-term financial sustainability.

The programme also has an innovative exit strategy that will enable scaling up its impact through the creation of a new fund, which will be managed by the private sector for local communities, intermediary financial institutions and other private sector actors targeting local markets with innovative products and commodities from the local agricultural and food systems.

The programme has a proposed budget of USD 315.30 million, with GCF funding representing approximately 72 per cent (USD 226.89 million) of this total. The GCF financing represents an equity investment in the form of grants and loans at interest rates of 0 per cent or USD 184.39 million. The GCF financing includes a USD 95.70 million grant (27.1 per cent of the total funding) and a USD 130.60 million loan (38 per cent of total cost). ADB will provide support to the programme with a USD 125.41 million loan at an interest rate of 1.9 per cent over a 15-year grace period (12.8 per cent of the total funding) and support the programme with a USD 173.20 million grant (55.6 per cent of the total funding). The World Bank will provide support to the programme through a USD 49.20 million loan at an interest rate of 1.9 per cent over a 15-year grace period and a USD 80.70 million grant (25.9 per cent of the total funding).

Organization of the United Nations will provide a grant of USD 6.69 million (0.21 per cent of the total funding) to support the programme with technical assistance, capacity building and knowledge sharing.

The proposed programme involves multiple interventions in different countries; it was not possible to conduct a full economic and financial appraisal (FEA) of all the subprojects at this stage due to limited information on the cost estimates for some of these subprojects. An indicative subprojects, as outlined in annex 3 of the funding proposal. As per the ADBB policy guidelines, only those subprojects that are included in the indicative subproject list will be thoroughly scrutinized to assess their financial and economic viability. Each subproject must meet the prescribed economic net present value (NPV) and internal rate of return (IRR) thresholds as set out in annex 3.

The EPAs were conducted on the basis of guidelines provided by GCF on a "per indicative project" basis for each country of interest. The analysis shows that the proposed investments are financially feasible and economically viable. The EPAs suggest that the investment proposals will be financially feasible if they have an internal rate of return (IRR) higher than the chosen discount rates of 9% per cent and 11 per cent, respectively. Additionally, the results show that the financial feasibility and economic viability and sustainability of proposed interventions are relatively insensitive to changes in discount rates, increased costs, or decreased benefits over a period of one year, two years, three years, or five years. This in turn suggests that the financial and economic results obtained are robust.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**a)** Programme overview. BREFLO aims to help build climate resilience and food security in the Horn of Africa, a region highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change across all parts of Africa. The programme includes components to strengthen the resilience of pastoral and agropastoral production systems such as the development and rehabilitation of water and irrigation infrastructure; the promotion of sustainable land management practices; livestock health and market access infrastructure. It also aims to promote sustainable land management practices such as agroforestry, soil conservation, and improved pasture management. The programme includes upscaling of technologies to pastoralists and smallholder farmers in the Horn of Africa. The programme will also provide training for women and youth on climate-resilient agricultural entrepreneurship skills development in renewable energy technologies. It also aims to design innovative financing mechanisms that will support the implementation of the following components: (i) livestock feed; (ii) crop, aquaculture, and livestock value chains; (iii) woodlots for fuelwood; and (iv) the restoration of degraded lands. The programme will also provide training on climate-resilient agriculture. The programme includes reduced air pollution and improved air quality resulting in better health and well-being of the community. The programme will also contribute to the improvement of water resources through the rehabilitation of irrigation infrastructure, and soil conservation practices through improved forest cover and land-use changes in the target areas.


---
**Page 154**
---

### Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments

The AD has classified its programme as Category A-2 in accordance with an integrated safeguards system classification. This is equivalent to GCF's Category B classification given that the activities to be supported will involve development and rehabilitation of water and market access infrastructure, which are considered high-risk activities. The project also includes a component related to marketing, which will target smallholder beneficiaries. The activities foreseen could have potential environmental or social impacts, but these risks are considered individually or cumulatively, are few, generally site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through mitigation measures. These measures would be implemented at the operational level of the AD's accreditation level. All has submitted an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) for each of the target country (Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia, and South Sudan).

#### Compliance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards

The paragraph below describes the ESS that will be applied to this project.

##### ESS1 (Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts)

Each participating country has prepared an environmental and social management framework (ESMF). The ESMF identifies key risks and manage individual activities (subprojects) of the programme in each country. While the general approach for assessing, managing, monitoring, and reporting on these risks is described, more detailed analysis of the E&S impacts will be carried out at the operational level as part of the project design or implementation phase of these assessments. Participating financial institutions will also be required to have operational environmental and social management systems (ESMS) where standards will include those set by GCF.

##### ESS2 (Labour and Working Conditions)

The ESMF has identified that the target countries are in compliance with ILO conventions and will comply with the core principles of these fundamental conventions. The ESMF also identifies risks in relation to occupational health and safety and basic worker's rights, non-discrimination, and decent work for women and men, including those engaged in unpaid labour. The frameworks require access of programme and subproject workers to grievance mechanisms and other forms of redress. In addition, the participating entities ensure that labour issues in the primary supply chain are adequately assessed and addressed.

##### ESS3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention)

The programme is generally geared towards efficient use of resources, particularly water. Hence, the infrastructure projects will be designed to minimize water consumption. The irrigation systems will be designed to achieve this. Some of the activities are also designed to complement the objectives of the project by promoting the cultivation of drought-tolerant crops to maximize scarce water resources. Nevertheless, the programme's activities may generate some environmental and social impacts (E&S). These include the possibility for soil erosion, noise, air and water pollution impacts due to construction activities from earthmoving, spillage and generation of waste. There is also the potential for land degradation and loss of biodiversity in the form of temporary structures/reserves through evaporation. The potential for silting of water reservoirs is another environmental impact that could be generated by these activities. As regards processing and livestock/dairy facilities, there is potential for air and water pollution impacts due to emissions from livestock handling operations and waste from laboratories and facilities. The ESMFs prescribe the development of waste management plans where these issues are addressed in site-specific environmental and social impact assessments of the activities.


---
**Page 155**
---

### ES4 (Community Health, Safety and Security)

The following hazards are potentially relevant based on the current situation of the programme countries. These include construction site and route accident hazards, infrastructure collapse hazard for dams and water impoundments, and other natural hazards such as dust/sandstorms, locust infestations, floods, landslides, earthquakes, tsunamis, and volcanic eruptions. There is a potential for cultural conflicts, increased incidence of gender-based violence and/or sexual exploitation and abuse (SE&A), spread of vector-borne diseases, zoonotic transmissible diseases and water and vector-borne diseases. There is also the potential for wild animal encounters and other wildlife-related risks.

The agricultural activities could lead to pesticide poisoning and food safety hazard from processed or stored food products. If the programme area has a history of conflict with a significant history or active cases of conflicts, there is potential for the exacerbation of conflict as a result of the programme’s activities. Security arrangements may also have adverse impacts on the environment and biodiversity in the programme area. To mitigate these types of conflicts, the programme will organize public consultations under the guidance of local authorities and other relevant stakeholders. Based on findings from the detailed E&SS assessment studies, conflict sensitivity analysis will also be undertaken to determine whether there are any potential risks associated with the presence of ground, and that appropriate management measures will be developed.

### ES5 (Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement)

The ESHP has also assessed land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. The countries involved in the programme (Djibouti and Kenya) have also indicated that subjects with significant displacements will not be relocated as a result of the project activities. However, it is possible for the relocation to be small scale and other interventions requiring significant area of lands can be sited in government land or private land where no compensation is required. In such cases, expropriation by expropriation would be rare. However, these lands may still be occupied by some families/tribes as they have been traditionally accessed by groups of people who are not necessarily the owners of the land. The programme will conduct comprehensive environmental and social impact assessments, Resettlement Action Plans which will detail the resettlement process and compensation arrangements. A standard document will be prepared should this be standardised in the triggered. The programme should also ensure that the ESHP is informed about any changes to the scope or impacts of land acquisition or use by their respective investments.

### ES6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Development)

The following hazards are potentially relevant related to loss or fragmentation and degradation of habitat, severance of animal migration routes and pathways (e.g. due to reduction in connectivity between habitats), destruction of natural vegetation (e.g., loss of water, soil or air), and loss of plant biodiversity as a result of introduction of invasive species. The programme will avoid the creation of new linear features that destroy natural habitat during earthworks and construction activities. The impacts and risks from the construction activities can be avoided by application of strict criteria among. The strategic environmental assessment (SEA) will include: (i) avoidance of sensitive areas for flora and fauna; (ii) establishment of buffer zones around conservation and ecologically sensitive areas to avoid degradation of these areas due to construction activities; (iii) restoration of cleared areas with native species; (iv) soliciting advice from biodiversity authorities on the need for any additional measures; (v) providing guidance to natural resources management practitioners on appropriate management measures; and (vi) supporting the development of a biodiversity action plan.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ES7 (Indigenous Peoples)

The scope of the programme and activities have potential to impact Indigenous Peoples who are found in the countries where the programme will be implemented and thus trigger application of


---
**Page 156**
---

The requirements of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy noting that regarding potential impacts related to Indigenous Peoples safeguards will need to be reinserted in the ESFsM for the participating countries. The timeline for provision of the revisions to the ESFsM can, exceptionally, be considered no later than effectiveness of the funded activities. The Secretariat has established a dedicated team within the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE, which is also encouraged to share its experience with other AEs in this regard.

### (h) ESSB (Cultural Heritage)

The potential impact on cultural heritage sites in the programme areas was identified as one of the risks under the programme. However, actions are being taken by the Secretariat to mitigate these risks through adequate financing. Further, to manage the risk in situations where there is discovery of physical cultural resources during project implementation, the AE will be required to follow the GCF Change Chance Find Procedure that outlines the actions required if culturally significant resources are discovered.

### 45. Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) safeguarding

The SEAH safeguarding approach proposed by the AE is considered inadequate by the Secretariat to comply with the GCF’s SEAH policy and the UN’s SEAH policy. Although the AE has noted that the programme may exacerbate the pre-existing levels of gender-based violence, the Secretariat believes that this risk can be mitigated through the country-level ESFsM, only the ESFs for Kenya identifies the potential SEAH risks, mitigation measures are not provided in the ESFsM. The Secretariat recommends that the AE adopt a consistent approach in assessing and addressing the potential SEAH risks that can be applied to all target countries. The Secretariat also suggests a condition that the AE provide further revised ESFsM with appropriate mitigation measures for the identified necessary SEAH risk screening, risk mitigation measures, the AE due diligence process for subprojects, and the programme-wide policy on sexual harassment through the grievance redress mechanisms (GRM) per country.

### 46. Implementation arrangements

Each country covered by the programme will have a Project Management Unit/Project Coordinating Unit which will be responsible for the day-to-day operations of the programme. The Project Management Unit/Project Coordinating Unit should be established as soon as possible after the signing of the ESFsM. The implementation of the ESFsM Consultants will also be engaged as needed, to provide technical reviews and advice on specific issues related to the implementation of the ESFsM. At country level E&S focal persons will be designated to ensure implementation, monitoring and reporting of the management of environmental and social risks and impacts in line with GCF’s safeguards.

### 47. Stakeholder Engagement and Information Disclosure

The programme has undergone consultations in each country often being introduced as a second phase or follow-up of a previous project. The ESFsM will be presented to the government for approval, followed by consultations conducted with government officials at the national, subnational and local levels. Plans for future communication and engagement activities are also included in the ESFsM. Stakeholder engagement plans will also be prepared during implementation. Stakeholder engagements will be very important given the fragile and conflict-affected situations of most of the programme areas.

### 48. Grievance redress mechanisms (GRM)

The ESFsM have generated GRMs to be implemented in each country, which are designed for the establishment of independent Grievance Redress Committees at central and region/distinct levels. The Grievance Redress Committees will have representatives from the project-affected persons, municipal governments, local communities, and other relevant stakeholders. The committees are designed to ensure that concerns are proactively identified, apprehensions from impacts of the interventions are adequately clarified, and that issues raised by complaints can be resolved in a timely manner.


---
**Page 157**
---

an efficient and timely manner. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### Gender policy

* The AIF has provided a programme-level gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore does not have an overall gender policy.
* The gender assessment was conducted through desk reviews and some stakeholder consultations. While the assessment covered all five countries in which the AIF is active, this did not cover all the participant countries. The stakeholder consultation for development of the gender assessment and gender action plan is limited in scope and in the range of categories of stakeholders involved.
* The gender assessment indicates that countries have favourable environments to promote women’s empowerment and gender equality. However, there are still significant gaps related equality between women and men in their constitutions, legal, institutional and political frameworks as well as having programmes and projects in operation. Many already have established national strategies for the promotion of gender equality and women's empowerment within the gender mainstreaming agenda. However, although many initiatives exist to address gender issues, they are often not integrated into other development policies and programmes or are in the lives of women and there remains a need for much more action in order to meet the commitments made by countries.
* The assessment also indicates that there is still a significant presence of widespread violence against women and girls, high levels of female unemployment and low levels of female entrepreneurship. These issues are particularly acute among rural women who are at subsistence level and are not very sustainable. It is also a reality that financial inclusion is not prioritized by financial institutions, particularly for women entrepreneurs, which is further exacerbating the gender gap in access to finance.
* Women have limited ownership or no access to land, productive resources, access to finance and markets. The lack of access to these resources is a major constraint on their role as farmers and contributes significantly to the low productivity of women’s agriculture. The information available for rural women is limited and, generally, particularly for climate-related information that is needed for making informed decisions or being part of decision-making processes. Literacy rates among women are also very low in all five countries and, consequently, their access to information and services is low. The sociocultural practices maintain women and girls in roles that inhibit their contribution to the household economy and their participation in agricultural development.
* The agriculture sector is affected by such realities where the productivity gaps of women farmers are significant compared with men’s farmers’ productivity levels.
* The challenges in accessing agriculture, climate-related services, inputs, advisory services and digital information are also a major constraint on women's ability to improve their productivity, nutrition and food security. In addition, it is well-known that much of the day-to-day work and engagement of women (be it in agriculture, entrepreneurship activities, women-led businesses or other income-generating activities) is not recognized for its economic impacts of climate change continue to negatively affect opportunities for women in terms of their resources and access to services. Women are also less likely than men to have their engagements in various farmers’ groups and farm-related activities.
* The gender assessment recognizes that women-headed households and women in polygamous marriages face additional constraints compared with those living in households headed by men. The constraints result in women lacking security, facing higher levels of poverty, higher levels of vulnerability and lower access to resources than their male counterparts. Women-headed households seem to have more economical use of resources at their disposal. Overall, the five countries are rated very low on gender-related indices.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The Secretariat considers the overall programme as medium risk.

Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk).

The AE, AFDB, demonstrated its capability by reference to its existing portfolio of projects and programmes in the Horn of Africa of comparable size or above. The AE is rated AAA stable by Moody's, Standard & Poor's and Fitch.


---
**Page 159**
---

## Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

### Co-finance: The Secretariat acknowledges the alignment of interests between the AE and GCF, with co-financing in the form of grants and loans from the AE (ratio 1:1.22). Co-financing does not exceed 40% of total financing. The risk that the co-financing commitment is not adequately mitigated by official approval from the ADB board in 2021-2022 and by conditions for GCF disbursements in the term sheet.

### Credit risk: The AE will channel GCF loans to the government ministries acting as EEs in Djibouti and Ethiopia, totaling USD 68 million or 40 per cent of total GCF financing. GCF is the sole lender in this transaction. The AE has a strong credit rating (A3/Standard & Poor’s AAA) within its funding window, and assumes the credit risk of intermediate lenders (local private financial institutions, insurance companies, etc.) that will be used to channel the GCF loan to the government ministries in two countries (e.g., Ethiopia Moody’s Caa1/Standard & Poor’s CCC). In addition, inflationary pressure on interest rates is a risk factor. The AE has a strong credit rating and can easily obtain the United States dollar and increase the cost of servicing a USD-denominated GCF loan. However, the highly concessional loans from GCF reduce the financial risk for the countries.

### Programme compliance: This programme addresses food production and security in several countries within the Horn of Africa region. Much of this region is challenged by low capacities to instill financial integrity into public institutions. The AE has not yet demonstrated that it can exceed the risks and has proposed a plan to monitor and mitigate such risks. With the imposition of three conditions, the compliance risk in the programme is measured as “medium”.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of the AE’s overall budget proposal is not material.

## Recommendations

It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

**a)** AFDB will be the AE of this programme. There are six EEs of this programme, as follows:

- The Government of Djibouti, acting through its Ministry of Commerce Economics and Finance, and Ministry of Agriculture, Water, Fisheries and Livestock;
- The Government of Ethiopia, acting through its Ministry of Finance and Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fishery Resources;
- The Government of Kenya, acting through its Ministry of Finance, and Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries, Cooperatives and Marketing;
- The Government of Somalia, acting through its Ministry of Finance and Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation;
- The Government of South Sudan, acting through its Ministry of Finance and Economic Planning and Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry; and
- The Intergovernmental Authority on Development.

**b)** Loan pricing will be equal for all borrowers. Both the GCF loan and AFDB loan are pari passu. All loans provided by the Facility to a country, from country to country, the accounts of the projects and subprojects under BREPOL shall, irrespective of the system adopted in the country concerned, provide information on: (i) accountability of funds provided to the project or subproject (such funds may come from GCF loan, AFDB loan, other sources such as counterpart funds); and (ii) disclosure of expenditure and specific information on components financed by the Facility.

**c)** All projects financed under the Facility will be monitored by the AFDB Portfolio Management team as per the relevant internal policies and procedures. The AFDB – as the accredited auditor – shall provide an audit report to GCF at least once a year, and reporting periodically to GCF under the terms to be agreed between AFDB and GCF. All projects financed under the Facility will be audited with the AFDB approval, monitoring and supervision standards and procedures.

**d)** The financial reporting should detail the various uses of the loan and grant proceeds. The auditor is required to review the financial statements prepared by the project each year in accordance with the relevant generally accepted standards. The auditor, in accordance with the standard audit practice, shall issue an opinion on whether or not the financial statements are presented fairly, in all material respects, in accordance with the applicable accounting framework and provide a report that includes an assessment of the internal control mechanisms to identify deficiencies and weaknesses that could affect the efficiency of the project. The auditor is required to ensure that the financial statements reflect how funds granted to the project have been used for their intended purpose. Each country must have the accounts audited by an independent auditor who has the necessary expertise to meet AFDB requirements. In countries where auditing is entrusted to the Auditor General’s Office under a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU), the MoU shall specify the scope and terms of reference for the audit for ADB-financed projects/programmes. The audited financial statements, the auditor's report and management letter must be received by AFDB no later than six months after the end of each fiscal year. Failure to meet this requirement will result in the immediate suspension of further replenishments to the Special Account.

### Results monitoring and reporting

As a cross-cutting initiative, the programme aims at generating results across GCF mitigation projects in Ethiopia. The expected direct beneficiaries are estimated to reach 4.68 million direct beneficiaries and 15.55 million indirect beneficiaries, corresponding to 20% of the total population of Ethiopia. At least 50% per cent of the beneficiaries across both categories are expected to be women. The expected mitigation results will be achieved through the implementation of various projects.

The Secretariat has worked with the AE over seven rounds of reviews to refine the programme theory of change and significant improvement has been noted. The theory of change diagram is now more aligned with the GCF’s integrated results management framework.

The logical framework has been developed in accordance with the GCF integrated results management framework and follows the recommended template. A significant paradigm shift persists from the previous version, which was designed to align with the project outcome level to facilitate performance management. However, in the latest round of review, the Secretariat has worked closely with the AE to ensure alignment under Outcomes 1 and 2, and providing clarity on means of verification for indicators for Outcome 3. The Secretariat has also defined indicators for assessing the co-benefits linked under the programme’s theory of change.

Suggestions have also been provided to the AE in the latest round of review to augment H1 and H2 indicators with additional information on intermediate results and H3 (Beneficiary Estimation). It has been recommended that the AE should provide further details on identification of beneficiaries as direct/indirect and arrangements for avoiding double-counting.

### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the AE on 8 November 2017 and it became effective on 7 November 2019. The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all necessary approvals from the Government of Ethiopia for the implementation of the programme.

The proposed programme will be implemented in the following countries in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against civil or criminal proceedings in these countries until their status is further assessed. The countries are as follows:

- Republic of Djibouti: The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Republic of Djibouti on 26 October 2017. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of the Republic of Djibouti on 3 November 2017.
- Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia: The Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia on 26 October 2017. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia on 3 November 2017.


---

---

# Republic of Kenya

The Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Republic of Kenya on 30 September 2015. The latest communication was sent by the Government of the Republic of Kenya to the Secretariat on 5 October 2018.

# Federal Republic of Somalia

The Secretariat provided a draft agreement and the Government of the Federal Republic of Somalia had a meeting on 8 October 2018. The Secretariat has not yet dispatched a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Federal Republic of Somalia hence, negotiations have not yet started.

# Republic of South Sudan

The Secretariat has not yet dispatched a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of these Republics. The Secretariat and the Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations in the countries, or if undertaken, such activities would be subject to restrictions on powers and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the countries, or has been provided with an alternative mechanism for redress.

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 180 days after Board approval, certifying that all necessary approvals have been obtained from the Government required by it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme;

(b) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, confirming that the Fund has received a certificate or legal opinion from the Accredited Entity providing a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.
