Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP239

Proposal name: Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BREFOL)

Accredited entity: African Development Bank (AfDB)

Country(ies): Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and South Sudan

Project/programme size: Large


I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
1. This is the second submission of the funding proposal for Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BREFOL) under the standard proposal approval process.
2. The funding proposal was first submitted to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) on 3 May 2024 and informed by a set of written questions and answers (Q&A) from the ITAP, as well as an addendum to the funding proposal package presented to the ITAP on 15 December 2021.
3. The total budget remains at about $195 million, with a total of USD 151 million (USD 96.7 million or 48% of the total) in co-financing (co-finance) loan requested from GEF. It also remains a cross-cutting programme, expected to contribute to both mitigation and adaptation results areas, with an emphasis on adaptation.
4. The AfDB for the programme is the African Development Bank (AfDB).
5. The executing entities (EEs) of the programme comprise:
   - The governments of the five countries (Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and South Sudan), which are the principal implementing agencies responsible for food and agriculture, respectively; and
   - The Intergovernmental Authority on Development (IGAD), the regional organization for the greater Horn of Africa.
6. The proposed programme has four components, as listed in Table 1 below, with their respective indicative costs and share of total financing.


The funding proposal was first submitted to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) on 15 December 2021. This submission was not endorsed by the ITAP but has now been revised and resubmitted, guided by a set of recommendations from the ITAP. The revised proposal is accompanied by an addendum to the funding proposals not endorsed by the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP). Prior to the submission of the funding proposal, the AfDB had already approved the project concept note (PCN) for Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa.

The funding proposal was submitted on 3 May 2024 consists of the funding proposal (clean and track-change versions from Secretariat discussions), the questions and answers (Q&A) from the previous submission, a draft Secretariat assessment report, and an addendum to the funding proposal. The addendum includes revised assumptions for the package in terms of the total budget, the total financing profile, and the total greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reduction. On 20 May 2024, the ITAP held its 17th and 18th meeting with AfDB officials for sufficient information on the AfDB entity and the ITAP on 23 May 2024.

The five countries that make up the Greater Horn of Africa are Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and Uganda.

Component 1 receives 56 per cent of the total budget (38 per cent of the GCF contribution and 46 per cent of co-financing). It contains the programme's single largest output (Output 1.2.1) which is expected to be completed by June 2027, accounting for 38 per cent of the total budget (19 per cent of the GCF contribution and 53 per cent of the co-financing). Output 1.1 will be funded with a USD 70 million grant and USD 28 million loan from the African Development Bank (AfDB) to support the construction, rehabilitation and management of livestock infrastructure such as water points, feedlots, and livestock infrastructure. Component 1 also houses outputs 1.2 and 1.3 towards improved land management practices and climate-smart innovations, operating with GCF grants (USD 16 million and 24 respectively).

Component 2 supports agribusinesses and other enterprises in accessing markets and climate-resilient technologies, as well as the development of youth employment and youth on entrepreneurship, renewable energy technologies/solutions, and the extension of credit lines to smallholder farmers. This component is expected to be completed by a total of 43 per cent of the GCF contribution and 15 per cent of co-financing.

The extension of credit, however, is limited to two of the beneficiary countries: BREFOL will provide concessional loans through the African Development Bank's finance institutions in Djibouti and Ethiopia. Furthermore, BREFOL will promote existing climate insurance mechanisms for livestock enterprises in Ethiopia. The remaining GCF resources are to complement AfDB’s investments to support the establishment of a BREFOL

*The AfDB is a funding window for AfRIF. It is the concessional arm of the AfDB that provides grants and concessional loans to low-income African countries.*

# Impact potential

## Scale: Medium

This cross-cutting programme is to contribute to five GCF results areas: two mitigation result areas (MRAa) and three adaptation result areas (MRAs). These are:

- **MRA1 - Energy generation and access**: More reliable people and communities;
- **MRA2 - Health, well-being, food and water security**: And MRA4 - Ecosystems and ecosystems services.

### Assumptions about the theory of change illustration

The three components contribute in different ways to the programme’s outcomes and co-benefits. These are:

- **Outcome 1:** Increased adoption of climate-resilient solutions;
- **Outcome 2:** Enhanced carbon sequestration and reduced greenhouse gas emissions from land use changes;
- **Outcome 3:** Strengthened adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities;
- **Co-benefit 1:** Improved livelihood opportunities; and
- **Co-benefit 2:** Ecosystem services improved.

### The theory of change illustration

The way in which the programme’s activities, outputs, outcomes, and co-benefits contribute towards the overarching goal and results areas was a major issue in the first submission. The logic flow and the application of the theory of change remains problematic. The ITAP is concerned with this design insufficiency and suggests improving the logic and clarity through a carefully designed theory of change illustration that includes all activities, outputs, outcomes, and monitoring (see condition (i) in the concluding section).

### The six proposed interventions

The following are the six proposed interventions:

- **Intervention 1:** Energy generation and access (MRA1 - Energy generation and access). These are off-grid solar photovoltaics and the installation of biogas units. The funding


---
**Page 166**
---

proposal for mitigation impact potential of 54,830 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂-eq) by the end of the seven years of implementation and 226,183 t CO₂-eq by the end of the 25 years lifespan. The mitigation impact potential of the off-grid solar photovoltaics was quantified using a clean development mechanism methodology for small-scale electrification, and the project is expected to generate 40 GWh/year of electricity.

The project also claims a mitigation impact potential from MRA (Forestry and land use), specifically from activities for the rehabilitation of rangelands and for agroforestry. The mitigation impact potential of these activities was quantified using the methodology for ACTs developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations. The anticipated mitigation impact potential is 1,025 t CO₂-eq/year (see Annex 3) or over 78,946 t CO₂-eq over the lifespan of the project.

The TEAP assesses the overall estimation of 1,290,182 tCO₂-eq by the end of the implementation period. The methodology used for this part of the project is sound, clear and well justified. However, there is still a lack of clarity about how the climate impact of the project will be verified in line with the requirements of the international climate change verification (MKV) system of the participating countries (see condition (i) in the concluding section).

On adaptation input potential: The funding proposal indicates an expected adaptation outcome with 4.7 million direct beneficiaries. Indirect beneficiaries are assessed as 15.5 million. The funding proposal does not provide a clear explanation for this figure, and the calculations (see Annex 3) indicate exactly 50 male/female. In order to reach at least 50 per cent women beneficiaries, it is recommended that the project team provides further discussion under section 1.3 (para. 34–36) and condition (ii) in the concluding section.

Annex 23 (Estimation of Beneficiaries) includes different ways to estimate the number of beneficiaries. The funding proposal should review the beneficiary estimation methodologies and explore the necessary qualitative delineation of the target population for each activity, especially for the agroforestry programme. Even the use of more qualitative indicators can be explored as a way of drawing attention to the need to focus on specific groups or sub-populations rather than specific outputs and broader outcomes (see condition (i) in the concluding section).

All BREFOMs are expected to contribute to AR1 [Enhancement of the most vulnerable] by increasing access to climate information, early warning systems and vulnerability of the region. It should be noted, however, that specific methodologies for reaching the most vulnerable (which could include a combination of community financing for concessions credit may be effective in targeting those able to successfully repay small-scale financial support, but it may also harm the most vulnerable from accessing the concessional climate finance facilitated by this project].

23. Indirect beneficiary estimates are by definition imprecise and rough estimates of hectares covered or potential population reached. Acceptance of a conservative area where a given indirect beneficiary calculation would not great impact; however, relates to activity 3.1.2 “Integrate and disseminate climate information and early warning into the existing agricultural extension services” (see Annex 3). The project is expected to reach all people in a given geographic area with climate-related early warnings. The present methodology for estimating indirect beneficiaries is based on an assumption-informed agro-advisory services (activity 3.1.1) but in relation to ENS, only the number of beneficiaries.

The TEAP notes that the beneficiary estimation (or project/programme-specific indicators listed in section ES of the funding proposal) should be limited to those businesses that actually acquire loans and resources, but only how many are eligible (including women and youth).

1.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium

23\. In the GCF context, paradigm shift potential is defined as the degree to which the proposed activity can catalyze change beyond a line-of-sight or programme investment. To this extent, the proposed project has the potential to create a new paradigm of water and irrigation infrastructure that is high quality and communities are interested in using and maintaining such infrastructure; then these investments should provide benefits relating to water and food security for generations.

24\. In addition, if the project leads to more efficient use of water resources, better irrigation and livestock infrastructure is of high quality and communities are interested in using and maintaining such infrastructure, then these investments should provide benefits relating to water and food security for generations. If this happens, the livelihoods of smallholder farmers will be enhanced.

25\. Extension services and training for improved land management and climate-smart agriculture practices can help increase yields, reduce post-harvest losses, improve soil fertility, use a better mix of crops, and protect livestock from disease. If successful, this could lead to greatly enhance livelihoods and adaptive capacity, which should be leading qualities that improve the lives of communities.

26\. Entrepreneurship training and the extension of credit – as connected with climate-smart business models – can help smallholder farmers increase their income, strengthen their community and its ability to cope with climate change and also make mitigation action more compatible with sustainable livelihoods.

27\. Replicability is another area that presents great opportunities for GCF to help overcome specific barriers to financing. More financing is needed in the region. However, the potential of the programme to be replicated on a large scale remains uncertain with regard to gender; this may depend on the level of success of BREFIL in taking lessons and insights forward.

28\. The reportedly highly successful pan-African Affirmative Finance Action for Women in Africa (AFiAFA) is another example that shows how GCF can help overcome specific barriers to financing. The AFiAFA’s commitment to fostering access to finance and technical support for Women-led Small and Medium Enterprises.

29\. The funding proposal is very vague on ways to strengthen regulatory and policy frameworks, which are critical for climate change resilience. It seems there will be policy dialogues in some of the countries, but with different topical orientations. Policy and regulatory frameworks are critical for climate change resilience. The GCF has identified a number of areas where it can provide support, such as ‘one-off investment’, but this is not the path or “paradigm shift potential” opted for by BREFIL.

30\. The funding proposal correctly notes that conducive regulatory frameworks are critical for climate change resilience and that the GCF has already provided support to the five country governments do work on the strengthening of institutional capacities for climate change adaptation. This is a very important area, as it will help create an environment for robust entrepreneurship. Such developments beyond the programme would be important for making

1.3 Sustainable development potential

The theory of change figure (funding proposal, page 32, figure B.2.2) indicates co-benefits of improved livelihood opportunities and "ecosystem services improved". These are fully consistent with the SDGs.

Also, the project contributes to achieving the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Significant contributions should go towards SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 9 (Industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities) and SDG 13 (Climate action). The project also contributes towards stabilizing of climate as well as the restoration of degraded rangelands and forests. This is fully consistent with the SDGs.

The wider (co-)benefits of the proposed programme may be substantial, very much depending on the level of success in reaching the most vulnerable people and communities and enhancing their livelihoods.

The economic and social co-benefits may be the greatest, not least by way of enhancing women's access to credit and enabling further income-generating. If the focus on women is successful, this will have a positive impact on the wider community as well. The programme (co-)benefits could contribute to women's economic independence, which in turn may have beneficial effects for their children.

However, the literature of Africa faces serious gender inequalities, which may compromise the success of the programme as these hamper the freedom and well-being of women and girls. Areas of concern include: limited access to land and other productive resources by women and girls include the limited ownership of, access to, and control over productive resources such as livestock, tools, seeds, water, fuelwood, food, credit, information, land, wood, fodder, grass for construction and the otherwise reduced mobility outside the home. Other factors relate to gender discrimination in education, which may result in lower levels of employment and reduced representation in political, legal and policymaking institutions. These inequalities are compounded by a lack of access to financial services and microfinance practices. As further pointed out in Annex A this affects the way women participate in livelihood diversification activities, and will thus have a bearing on the success of the proposed project.

The programme is designed with gender equality as one of its objectives; however it is not sufficiently integrated into the funding proposal's mainstream activities. Moreover, the rationale for the focus on women is not clearly explained or justified within the proposal, while none of the other activities have this focus, nor indeed the prevailing gender discrimination could motivate any other activity (also) to have a more specific gender focus and gender-responsive approach.

The ITAP notes that consultations with beneficiaries and stakeholders – communities, women's groups, local authorities and non-governmental organizations – will continue in the implementation phase (see page 185 of the funding proposal). This provides the AR with suitable opportunities for developing an updated gender action plan (GAP) as part of the project's monitoring and evaluation framework (see following section).


---
**Page 169**
---

## Environmental benefits, as indicated in paragraph 48 of the funding proposal, are anticipated through the promotion of resilient agroecological production systems by promoting the adoption of sustainable land management best practices and agroforestry, leading to improved ecosystem services and increased soil organic carbon content. Furthermore, BRELOIL will contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture through reduced tillage, lighting, and processing, alongside the potential generation of biogas energy from over 3,890 cubic meters per year.

### One factor which could limit environmental benefits, or even do harm, is the risk of overextraction of groundwater. In all five countries, stakeholder consultations came up with the idea of “groundwater as a resource” (see paragraphs 109–113 of the funding proposal). With the shift from “costly” direct to “free” solar photovoltaic energy generation, there is an increased risk that groundwater will be overextracted. This risk is heightened where institutional capacities and regulatory frameworks are weak, and groundwater monitoring may be absent.

### Needs of the recipient: Scale: Medium to high

* The Horn of Africa is one of the world’s most disaster-prone drought and food insecurity regions.
* Conflict and political instability, coupled with drought and flooding, continue to affect millions of citizens, including refugees, internally displaced persons (IDPs), and returnees. This has a direct impact on access to resources needed for the income generation that is essential for accessing food and adapting to climate change adaptation. Millions of children suffer from acute malnutrition. There is no question about the need to address these critical issues in order to protect vulnerable populations.

* The proposed programme also sets out to support the most vulnerable people and communities and address gender aspects. These are critical intentions that need even further emphasis and critical attention, as highlighted above.
* The funding proposal includes a section on governance risks (two paras. 211–213 of the funding proposal). It is critical for the successful implementation of the programme that the level and risk of conflict and lack of capacity be continuously monitored and addressed in project design and implementation. This will require the development of appropriate tools to be brought into project management and methodologies for programme delivery. Annex 24 (Fragility analysis) provides a useful starting point, as does the assessment of the sociopolitical situation of the beneficiary countries, and would be worth updating. Such an update should also include a review of the governance risks and how they will inform the methodologies for implementing the BRELOIL activities (see condition [iii] in the concluding section).

* Updated country-level strategies for managing fragility concerns in an adaptive manner during project implementation will be key for the implementation of activities 3.2.1 (Support delivery of climate-smart agriculture services) and 4.1.2 (Technical and institutional capacity building), as well as other sectoral activities.

* There is also a clear need for climate-smart agriculture in the region. The BICW to the DfID-funded Climate Change Adaptation Programme (CCAP) has identified the need for the much-needed financing needs in the climate-sensitive agricultural sector. This facility will enable the development of a business case for the CCAP, which will help identify and derive the potential investment activities in the climate-smart agriculture sector. Given the prevailing market interest rates, ranging from 12 per cent to 13 per cent in Djibouti, and 12 per

* See paragraph 73 of the funding proposal, and activity 2.2.3.


---
**Page 170**
---

1.5 Country ownership

The five participating countries—Djibouti, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia and South Sudan—are all provided no objection letters and will contribute in kind to financing. The initial concept of a joint trust fund (JTF) for climate change adaptation (CC Adaptation Trust Fund) covered seven of the eight IGAD countries. Uganda excluded itself, and Sudan was unable to participate due to its ongoing conflict situation. Hence, five IGAD member states are involved in the presently proposed programme.

Additional consultations with the participating countries after the submission of the funding proposal have confirmed that they will continue supporting the JTF concept. It appears from ADP’s responses to the ITAP’s questions, that several countries were not able to identify a single institution to be responsible for the implementation of the project. Hence, the BDCW and the related Direct Insurance Credit Window are limited to Djibouti and Ethiopia.

At the regional level, this whole endeavour is a follow-on from IGAD’s Drought Resilience Strategy (DRS) and the IGAD Climate Prediction and Early Warning System (I-CPEWS). The DRS has been integrated in the programme's design and the implementation plan. In some participating countries, the I-CPEWS is being implemented through the National Meteorological Services, to be strengthened with the addition of specialized staff. The proposed programme aligns with IGAD’s Drought Disaster Risk Reduction (DRR) Initiative Strategy and the Rapid Response Mechanism (RRM). Also, the African Union’s Adaptation to Climate Change Strategy (2010–2030) played a pivotal role in narrowing down the BREFD subinventions, consistent with the IGAD’s DRS.

At the national level, the proposed programme aligns with the respective countries’ national strategies and action plans on climate change. All of them have submitted at least a first (draft) national adaptation plan (NAP).

The participating countries' capacity to implement funded programmes is essential. The ministries responsible for agriculture, water resources, environment and other development environments and have important capacity needs. Nevertheless, they are also the most suitable entities to take on a project of this nature, and their involvement should enable them to develop their capacity in climate change adaptation and contribute towards national economic sustainability.

The BREFP hence commends the approach of working through regional and national entities, and trusts that the AD has incorporated necessary and sufficient means to support and develop the implementation capacity of the executing and implementing entities. The Ministry of Agriculture, Water Resources and Environment (MoAWE) in Ethiopia, for example, proposed (para 185) suggests that “the large number of people mobilized during the consultations confirms that countries and IGAD are highly committed to the programme and have high expectations of its ability to bring improvements.”

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The TAP finds that the proposed initiative BREFUL is well aligned with the updated Strategic Plan and will contribute to addressing climate change impacts on vulnerable groups.

The TAP also recognizes that the AECs and easter countries are committed to addressing the important goal of enhancing climate resilience among agricultural and other communities in these countries. The TAP further notes that this initiative is one of the most impactful initiatives in the most climate affected and vulnerable parts of the world.

A general finding (from both the first and second submissions), however, is that the way that the GCF Board has structured its funding proposals is not consistent with some of the most important endeavours proposed. The TAP hence recommends a set of measures, formulated in the conditions below, to be fulfilled prior to the first disbursement of GCF proceeds.

Hence, the ITAP recommends that the Board approves this funding proposal with the following conditions to be fulfilled prior to the first disbursement of GCF proceeds:

### Conditions precedent to the first disbursement of GCF proceeds:
(a) The Accredited Entity shall provide to the GCF, in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, an updated logical framework that is consistent with the theory of change; and
(b) a detailed monitoring, evaluation and learning plan (MEL Plan) that:

- Clearly outlines milestones towards achieving the Funded Activity outcomes and outputs;
- Sets out the definition of indicators and data gathering methods for progress in mitigation and adaptation to climate change of specific systems programs;
- Includes the methods for analyzing and interpreting the data;
- Includes the description per Host Country of the process of integrating the monitoring, evaluation and learning plan into the national system of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) for adaptation and MRV system for mitigation; and
- Sets out a strategy for knowledge management and exchange.

(c) The Accredited Entity shall deliver to the Fund, in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, an approved gender action plan that explains how to achieve the stated goal of 50 per cent female and 50 per cent male split among direct and indirect beneficiaries of the funded activity. This should include information on access to land or other assets, land titles or livestock, information and means of communication, as well as the ability to autonomously manage resources.

(d) The Accredited Entity shall update, in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, the information and analysis contained in Annex 24 to the Funding Proposal,

## Proposal name:
Building Climate Resilience for Food and Livelihoods in the Horn of Africa (BREFOL)

African Development Bank (AfDB)

## Impact potential
Thank you for your insightful comments and recommendations. Yes, we agree to prepare and submit to the GCF Secretariat prior to first disbursement:

(a) an updated logical framework that is consistent with the theory of change; and  
(b) a detailed monitoring, evaluation, and learning plan (MEL Plan) that:
(i) Clearly outlines milestones towards achieving the Funded Activity outcomes and final targets, disaggregated by sex;
(ii) Sets out the proposed data collection and data gathering methods for progress in mitigation of, and adaptation to, climate change of specific systems proposed in each Host Country;
(iii) Includes a description of how to collect, analyze, and interpret the data;  
(iv) Includes the description per Host Country of the process to integrate the monitoring and evaluation information to the reporting system of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) MiLE system for adaptation and MRV system for mitigation; and
(v) Sets out the steps and responsibilities for knowledge management and evidence building.

## Paradigm shift potential
These IAP comments are well noted. As suggested and described in the funding proposal, BREFOL leverages lessons and insights from existing African Development Bank (AfDB) initiatives such as the Africa Climate Technology Finance Action for Women in Africa (AFAWA). The AfDB is committed to fostering access to climate finance support for Women-led Small and Medium Enterprises.

Furthermore, BREFOL aligns with commitments made by other related initiatives by five country governments on the strengthening of institutional capacities for climate resilience and the creation of an enabling business environment for climate-smart entrepreneurship. We also believe that BREFOL will contribute significantly towards the goal to make BREFOL investments into tools, training and infrastructure more effective and sustainable.

## Sustainable development potential


---
**Page 176**
---

As rightly noted by ITAP consultations with beneficiaries and stakeholders including communities, women’s groups, youth associations, civil society organizations (CSOs) and non-governmental organizations (NGOs), we agree that this process will provide suitable opportunities for developing an updated gender action plan that is more inclusive of the needs of all target groups.

To address the possible risk of overpumping and overextraction of groundwater, site specific geophysical assessments will be performed to ascertain that the selected sites/acquirer can support the proposed activities. The boreholes will not be located on the same location but will be scattered in the target communities based on demand for water and the availability of suitable locations. This will ensure equitable interventions within the community. In addition, the solar-powered submersible pumps which will be installed at the boreholes will only be pumping water for a short period of time during the day into storage tanks. The borehole pumps will not be working for the rest of the day and at night, which will allow groundwater recharge.

Needs of the recipient

Thank you for your comments and for highlighting the unique challenges in the region and the need to develop BREFOL Direct Credit Window (DCCW).

The ADB’s usual practice applicable to BREFOL will be to employ multifaceted approach that combines due diligence, risk assessments, capacity building, stakeholder engagement, and policy reforms. The DCCW will also support the implementation of activities 3.2.1 (Support delivery of climate regulatory framework and mainstreaming of climate change into agricultural sector policies and strategies) and 4.1.2 (Technical assistance to implement the ADB Strategy for Addressing Fragility and Building Resilience in Africa (2022-2026)).

Country ownership

Thank you for your communication. The ADB (AE) will continue to provide necessary support to develop the implementation capacity of the executing and implementing entities at regional and national level.

Efficiency and effectiveness

These comments are as well noted. As recommended for the organization and rolling out of the innovative financing lines, BREFOL will leverage on the lessons learned on success of ADB’s ongoing AFWA programme.

The AE take note of these recommendations and will ensure clear linkage between the proposed reforms and treasury services, regulatory reforms, as well as blended finance and potential guarantees, and ensure cross-programme coherence and solid contributions to the enabling environment for continued beneficiary financial prowess and economic independence.

# BUILDING CLIMATE RESILIENCE FOR FOOD AND LIVELIHOODS IN THE HORN OF AFRICA (BREFOL)

## GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN

### DJIBOUTI

#### I. Introduction

The BREFOL Project in Djibouti is classified as Category 2 in the Bank's Gender Marker System (GEN II), which means that the project contributes significantly through several of its outcomes and objectives to achieving gender equality.

The following activities were discussed with all the technicians and experts of the project, including the Coordinator, the Monitoring and Evaluation Officer and the Gender Expert for the Ministry of Agriculture team; the Project Program Manager and the Djibouti Country Project Team (DJPCT) team. The discussions focused on how to make these activities fully integrated or integrable within previously planned activities, including:

(i) the strengthening of the agricultural extension service and agro-technical infrastructure (within the budget line for technical manuals to be developed by the same firm),

(ii) consultations with gender-sensitive beneficiaries (with 50% women participation),

(iii) the inclusion of gender-based violence prevention in the prevention of risks related to the transmission of HIV/AIDS, STIs but also the risks of gender-based violence such as sexual exploitation, harassment and abuse of power (SEAH) within the project,

(iv) the deployment in the field of the innovative GALA (Gender Action Learning System) methodology for the training of women leaders at EAD and Odom at regional level,

(v) the recruitment of a National Gender Expert.

The reworked table of gender activities in virtual session was shared with all participants with also a specific gender questionnaire to collect the country information required during the assessment mission. The table is based on the inputs provided by the Ministry of Agriculture and other national structures (including the Ministry of Women and Family) as well as the Djibouti Country Project Team (DJPCT) team (from the Ethiopian party) and the project team were organized to finalize the project gender activity plan.

#### II. Gender analysis

Regarding the Africa Gender Equality Index (AGI) developed annually by the AfDB and UNICEA, Djibouti is one of the three countries on the continent not to have benefited from the lack of time series data in the index. This explains why Djibouti has a very low score compared with its performance vis-à-vis the continental average and other countries from this index. This presupposes a very weak consideration of gender issues in national development statistical indices, as a sign of the lack of sufficient disaggregated data.

According to the Mo Ibrahim Index of Governance in Africa (HAG) 2020, at the gender level, Djibouti occupies the third place out of 54 in Africa with a Gender score of 31.8/100 and an


---
**Page 180**
---

# Djibouti women have limited socio-economic opportunities compared to men.

Although Djibouti is experiencing high economic growth (8-9%), unemployment is massive and affects 42% of the population aged between 15 and 64 years old, with a female unemployment rate of 30.7%, and 65.6% unemployment rate in the absence of significant private sector development, 41% of the women are engaged in informal economic activities (informal economy) as a self-employed or unpaid employee. However, the administration recruited twice as many men from 2009 to 2017 so that the unemployment rate for women increased from 36.8% to 63.4% between 2002 and 2017. Accumulating inequalities rooted in traditions and customs limiting their socio-economic role, women experience a low level of education and limited access to financial services. From 2005 to 2015, the rate of women working in the informal sector even increased significantly (from 34.7% to 74.7%).

## Women's empowerment in Djibouti is paradoxically weak and underdeveloped

In 2014 came a recognized dynamism of Djiboutian women, only 16% of new companies created were created by women (Oulofra Abdo Ismail, Director of GOPIC, 2018). While was almost non-existent in the past, women's entrepreneurship is now on the rise and today represents a force for development. The Republic of Djibouti has set itself a target of 35% of businesses created by women by 2021 by counting on support for the formalization of existing micro-enterprises, the creation of new enterprises, and the elimination of obstacles and precarizations (ibid.). According to Aicha Hamadou Harmand, it is an economic lever for the entire population. However, women entrepreneurs face many constraints such as the marginalization of women in the labor market, the overwhelming majority of whom work in the informal sector. Women work every day to feed their families and are very present in the submission of requests for loans from banks or microfinance institutions. They also suffer from the lack of funding and infrastructure. Several issues surrounding the start-up or expansion of women's businesses remain unresolved: lack of access to credit, financial services and barriers faced by women entrepreneurs as well as ways and means to overcome them.

## Financial inclusion for farmers is very limited, particularly for women farmers

Government efforts to combat the exclusion of people from the banking system with the implementation of microfinance institutions have not been successful. The number of beneficiaries of these institutions is a significant increase in the number of credit beneficiaries and their volume. The number of women beneficiaries has increased by 10% between 2014 and 2015 (source: MFPPI, National Gender Policy 2011-2021; Republic of Djibouti, SCAPE 2015-2019, p.173), and the volume of credits granted by FDP (from FDJ $57 million to FDJ $500 million, ibid.). However, women still represent only a small percentage of beneficiaries in these institutions. The majority of women and girls continue to face many constraints that prevent them from benefiting from Djibouti's growth potential by remaining at the stage of "income-generating" activities, which are not very sustainable, with unchanged access to other means of production.


Source: https://ags-onlinedata.fr/themes/GENADoc-Outline-overview/, Retrieved 09/22/21.


---
**Page 181**
---

This stagnation is due to cultural factors. In rural areas, the girl prepares herself from an early age to take on household chores and take charge of the maintenance of the home. Mothers thus view the reproductive role of the woman and will sometimes prefer to prepare the little girl for the function of future mother and housewife. These factors constrain access to land, livestock or other productive resources. In addition, in rural areas, women are often denied equal rights with men (Article 101), in practice women have little access to land and are often even deprived of their inheritance in favour of the men in their family (AID, 2020).

Unpaid work and burden of caregiving. Like many African countries, the weight of socio-cultural norms confines the majority of unpaid work on women. Djiboutian society is patriarchal and traditional. The division of roles between men and women is based on the responsibility of men and women to bring up their children; the traditional perception that these responsibilities are exclusively those of women has been reinforced by religious dogma. Men leave this burden on women's shoulders. Indeed, it is rare for men to stay at home to care for children (UNICEF, 2010). Mothers thus value the reproductive role of the woman and will sometimes prefer to prepare their little girl for that function from an early age.

This is especially observed in rural areas where the girl will be prepared from an early age to take on household chores and take charge of the maintenance of the home. The Family Code Act has put an end to a large number of forms of discrimination, the persistence of patriarchal attitudes and deep-rooted and rooted stereotypes concerning the roles and responsibilities of men and women in society. However, these changes are not yet fully reflected within Djibouti's social structures.

Fruiting for women constrains little to their employability. Employment in Djibouti is general and very low. The gap between urban and rural areas is also a major stagnation factor at risk of perpetuating women’s poverty. With a literacy rate that is struggling to increase (from 58% in 2013 to 64% in 2017), the majority of women are excluded from the job market, especially rural women whose literacy rate is 14.2%. This result is well below the 40 points of the average for women aged between 15 and 24 years old with a primary level, with a gender parity index that has even declined (from 0.89 in 2008 to 0.86 in 2017), which jeopardizes the achievement of parity at the average (0.82), secondary (0.83), technical (0.75) and tertiary levels (0.4). This decline is also due to a decrease in girls' enrollment rate, with a drop from 69% in 1998 to 58% in 2017 at the primary level. This decline in Djibouti is the result of a slow evolution in girls’ enrolment (net enrolment ratio for girls aged between 6 and 14 years old) and a decrease in gross enrollment rate (GER) from 68.8% in 2011 to 78.9% in 2017. The construction of gender-sensitive infrastructure and school canisters has had tangible effects on the retention of rural girls (GER from 21.5% in 2013 to 40.6% in 2017), but this is not enough to counteract the high dropouts. The main reason for girls dropping out and dropping out of school is related to marriage or the need to go to work for the family.


---
**Page 182**
---

In the agricultural sector, smallholder farms run by women face a productivity gap of 10-30% compared to their male counterparts. In a context of resource scarcity, a third of women own land farms and women herders own a quarter of the livestock (small enclaves). The effects of climate change are more pronounced in Djibouti, where it is already difficult to ensure food production and livelihood. Agriculture in Djibouti is limited to very small areas, due to the lack of water for irrigation, and is almost exclusively for self-consumption.

Epidhaut also faces challenges in accelerating nutrition and food security. From 2010 to 2013, stunting rates among children aged under five years were between 45% and 60%. Among children aged 0-5 years, given the current health system, 8.9% of the Djiboutian population over 55 years old are estimated to be living with HIV/AIDS. The prevalence of women (extreme childhood) of childbearing age remains high. Inadequate nutritional practices are one cause of micronutrient deficiencies and malnutrition in children under five years, and power within the household is a barrier to seeking care. The universal coverage of health services remains a challenge, penalizing poor and rural women in particular. Despite increased access to health services for pregnant women, maternal mortality rates remain high compared to urban areas. The maternal mortality rate is very high at 381 deaths per 100,000 live births (PSD), figure almost five times higher than the regional average (78/100,000) (World Bank, 2015). Gender-based violence remains a major problem in Djibouti. The prevalence of HIV among those aged 30-49 years is 6% and greater health vulnerability. According to UNAIDS, the prevalence of HIV among women aged between 15 and 49 years old (UNAIDS) is 27% for girls). more acute malnutrition (19% vs. 16%), and greater health vulnerability. According to UNAIDS, the prevalence of HIV among those aged 30-49 years is 6% and greater health vulnerability. According to UNAIDS, the prevalence of HIV among women aged between 15 and 49 years old (UNAIDS) is 27% for girls). more acute malnutrition (19% vs. 16%), and greater health vulnerability.

According to a study by another bank project in the Djibouti and Al-Sahel area (SNISCO, ESA), 80% of the population (men and women) knows that HIV/AIDS virus exists but does not necessarily know the transmission channels, prevention mechanisms, or even their own HIV status. The knowledge level is higher among men than women (65% vs. 49%). Among those aged between 15 and 49 years old, 80% of people know about AIDS but only 37% of them know that using condoms was a risk of transmission.

The Covid-19 situation is exacerbating gaps in access to health to the detriment of the elderly and women. Djibouti has the highest Covid-19 prevalence rate in East Africa, recording 1,300 cases of Covid-19 per million inhabitants as of April 28, 2020, according to the African Centre for Disease Control (ACDC). The feasibility study of this project mentioned the absence of a hospital structure in Defensatoine, and the request for the installation of a dispensary was made by more than half of the population.

13% of female-headed households do not have access to water compared to 10% of their male counterparts and 8% of female-headed households use traditional wells (to fetch water), and...


---
**Page 183**
---

The use of improved toilets for which the significant inequalities between rural and urban households are based. Only 10% of rural households have access to improved toilets compared to 85% for urban households. Depending on the gender of female heads of household, there is a four-point difference, to the detriment of those headed by women.

Moreover, it should be emphasized that Djibouti has the specificity of the existence of a very rudimentary rural population (less than 10% of the total population). In addition, in 2014, more than 98% of nomadic households belonged to the two poorest quintiles of the population, and nomads accounted for the largest percentage of food insecure households. Since 2010, massive displacement has occurred among pastoralists due to droughts and conflicts (in particular in the region of Ali Sabieh). These populations, who have lost all their livestock, are entirely assisted by aid programs. According to the latest data from the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries, 32% are severely food insecure and 32% moderately food insecure (AGVSAN, 2014; WFP).

Climate change leads nomads to a frantic search for pastures for their livestock and a concentration of livestock on watered parts of the country, resulting in overconsumption of pastures. This situation is exacerbated by the fact that pastoralists are increasingly forced into sedentary populations to sell or see the death of part of their livestock, animal capital constituting their main source of income.

The digital divide hits rural women hardest. In terms of access to information and communication technologies (ICTs), significant inequalities prevail to the detriment of female-headed households and between rural and urban households. Inequalities emerge to the detriment of women in all regions of Djibouti, but especially in the region of Ali Sabieh with a man at the head, mobile phones (67% vs 79%), and as an least one means of information and communication technologies (80% vs 79%). Despite the 2014 "Zero Women Without Telephones" campaign, women are still less likely to have access to ICTs than men. Inequalities related to gender and/or place of residence. Only 5% of rural households headed by women have access to at least one means of information and communication technology (ICT) compared to a man head (compared to 80%) and 22% to at least one medium NICT (compared to 80% of urban households headed by women).

The provision of inclusive financial services is expanding in Africa to help women smallholder farmers overcome some of the constraints mentioned, with insurance, for example, to risk agricultural financing for women farmers or yield insurance providing agricultural loans to reassure banks and encourage them to support agriculture. However, Africa is the continent most vulnerable to climate change and is already prone to a wide variety of natural disasters and women in particular do not have collateral for loans. The African Development Bank (and several other organizations) has therefore launched the "African Women's Agency" initiative. This requires investments in digital infrastructure, policy and regulation, digital products (SMS providing agricultural advice), innovation and capacity building with a gender-centric approach.

### III. Gender Analysis within the context of REEFLOT, Djibouti

The objective of the Programme is to contribute to the improvement of living conditions, including for women and youth, food and nutrition security, increased resilience, and peace and security in the Horn of Africa.

The watersheds selected in the project area are located in the regions of:
- Ali Sabieh: Assamo & Ali-Addé & Holt-Holl & Oboley & Daasbiyo, 513 km²


---
**Page 184**
---

### IV. Actions taken by the Government

At the level of Djibouti’s legal, institutional and political framework, significant institutional progress in favor of gender equality can be noted on the part of the Djiboutian government with the inclusion of women's rights in general laws, the ratification of conventions, the ratification of international protocols on the promotion of gender equality, as well as the updating of a national gender strategy. However, the implementation of these measures is still insufficient and the empowerment of women and girls is manifested in the prohibition of early marriage, forced marriage, rape, and violence against moral and sexual harassment, although pockets of conservatism encourage by religious leaders.

However, national, political and budgetary instruments to implement these provisions remain insufficient (i.e. although recognized as a crime: violence against women, including female genital mutilation (FGM), is significant). This is important to note given that in any project, the influx of external male labour represents a major impact with the increase in GIV and OIV.

### Footnote
* These data are taken from EDEEC 2015/2016 Djibouti Research on Employment: The Informal Sector and Consumption and the Djiboutian Household Survey, of the DEM 2017, Household Survey, and were collected as part of the project.

**Page 185**

exploitative sexual relations (women and girls, especially migrants, finding themselves forced into prostitution).

With regard to the economic opportunities of Djiboutian women, at the initiative of the **14th Lady** of the Republic of Djibouti, UNFID and ODIPIC (Djiboutian Office of Industrial and Commercial Property), a plan to promote women's entrepreneurship has been launched.

Many initiatives were then born, such as the Centre for Leadership and Entrepreneurship (CLE), inaugurated in 2019, set up in partnership with the Ministry of Women and Family, which offers training courses on business management, marketing, and communication while building business plans but also seeking funding. This incubation center also offers young women (men and women) the opportunity to test their projects through a period of experimentation and experimentation of start-ups. Also, the Club des Jeunes Entrepreneurs Djiboutiens (CJED) offers a training program in entrepreneurship for young people between 15 and 24 years old who wish to undertake, provides assistance in the form of support to strengthen the entrepreneurial skills of learners, promotes the coordination of institutions in charge of women's economic empowerment, participates in the organization of events and activities related to entrepreneurship and competition organized by the institutions in charge of promoting entrepreneurship. Finally, the Women Like Djibouti project supported by ODIPIC, supports women working in the informal sector to realize their actions.

The "Djibouti Vision 2035" focuses on renewable energies for the modernization of rural areas and the Djiboutian State has undertaken a vast energy infrastructure program with a view to improving access to electricity. The project aims at creating jobs, promoting women's economic empowerment through microfinance projects and other initiatives that create jobs or promote technical training have so far benefited women little. This project, which aims to target women and youth, has the potential to be a game changer.

The vision of Djibouti 2035 "aims for a situation where '35% of women are economically independent'"

### VI. BREFOL Djibouti Gender Action Plan

The Bank's Gender Strategy focuses on improving access to finance and technical assistance, strengthening technical skills, building gender-sensitive infrastructure, and key external partnerships with the Government of Djibouti. The project will ensure that women’s specific needs can demand that gender-specific challenges be taken into account in any Bank-financed project.

In order to address some of the gender gap issues raised in the gender analysis of this project, and in line with the ongoing efforts of the Government of Djibouti to improve gender equality and equity, the following gender interventions will be implemented:

- promotion of financial inclusion and microfinance instruments for rural women,
- which includes access to credit,
- support for the structuring of women's agro-pastoral cooperatives and the collective acquisition of land titles or other forms of tenure security,
- extension and access to ICTs and renewable energy programmes targeting rural women,
- gender-sensitive agricultural infrastructure,
- extension services, including training in new and energy technologies,
- inclusion of gender analysis in all project studies with sex-disaggregated data collection and targeted recommendations,
- specific awareness-raising against gender-based violence.

* A gender responsive infrastructure will systematically include consultations with the most vulnerable (assuming disadvantaged voices are taken into account in design, planning and management), UN Women.

- Training 100 women in digital literacy and functional literacy (12) by UNFED.
- Extension of the network of field assessors for climate vulnerability (50% points of the localities [50% women]) to also deliver digital and functional literacy courses and establish the list of people in need of CNI and certificates of agricultural occupation.
- Strengthening the participation of women on the Committee set up by the Ministry of the Interior with the establishment of a network of field assessors of climate vulnerability equipped (50% women/men) and the representation of women on the Committee set up by the Ministry of the Interior (planned for phase 2) by strengthening the participation of women and youth (50%).
- Equipment for home irrigation ("home water-pumps") and renewable energy solutions (solar panels, etc.) to reduce the time spent by women in their domestic burden.
- Strategy for the inclusion of vulnerable populations (migrants, nomads) among the beneficiaries of the project with measures to combat their food insecurity.
- Specific support for the development of entrepreneurship, for example with the establishment of an incubator in national priority agricultural value chains with comprehensive entrepreneurial and technical training in these sectors.

# ETHIOPIA

## Context

Ethiopia is a low-income country with a population of 112,078,739 of which 50% are women according to the World Bank Data for the country. It is interesting to note that the country has made some tremendous progress towards actions to gender equality. According to the global Gender Gap Index of 2020 by the World Economic Forum, Ethiopia ranks 82nd in the 2020 ranking with a score of 0.593. The country also ranked 14th among African countries and achieved 16th position globally in political empowerment with about almost half (47.6%) of its ministers being women and 38.8% of parliament seats occupied by women[1]. It has also operationalized various policies, strategies, and action plans to promote gender equality such as mainstreaming gender within the key national development plans such as the Growth and Transformation Plan I and II (GTP), gender budgeting, increased political participation by women in local government structures, representation of women on boards and councils but though lower at federal level; registration of considerable improvements in reproductive health services for women. The country has also made progress with 40% women owning land and 25% owning through joint land certification for women in male headed households. Despite these remarkable results women are still marginalized in the fields of health services, education, economic empowerment, and political participation due to their full potential. For example, Ethiopian women are worse off in agricultural productivity, business revenues and hourly wages than men due to differential access to productive resources like credit, inputs, capital, etc.

Specific Gender Gaps have resulted in challenges and barriers to women's economic empowerment and achievement of gender equality in Ethiopia. These include: Limited access to basic services like education Gender Parity Index (GPI) is low in secondary and tertiary levels. Females are less likely than males to be enrolled in primary school, drop out before grade 8 and continue with secondary schooling compared to their male counterparts and more likely to perform poorly than boys on grade 10 examination. Apparently, there are fewer females enrolled in higher institutions (25% of female students) as compared to males (34%) at undergraduate (18percent) levels. Mean years of schooling for females is just 1.6, less than half of the already low mean years of schooling for males, at 3.7 years.

Low BMI among women in the reproductive age group: Around 23.2 percent of women in the reproductive age group (15-49) are underweight and 80% of them have a BMI below 16 which make anode diseases. The highest proportion of overweight of women is observed in Afar (19.91 percent), followed by Tigray (34 percent), Gambella (31.8%) and Somali (31.2 percent). On the other hand, the highest prevalence of obesity among non-pregnant women of reproductive age group (15-49) stands at 29.4 percent in Addis Ababa, while about one-in-five women in Dire Dawa and Harar are overweight or obese[2].

# Barriers to economic participation – limited access to productive resources

Women are worse off in agricultural productivity, business revenues, and hourly wages than men due largely to differences in access to land, credit, and other inputs. Women’s low access to formal credit is partly linked to their limited ownership/control of physical assets that serve as collateral. Women experience lower returns on investments due to structural issues that persist even when they have the same level of education and wealth as men.

The Government has prioritized increasing women's access to agricultural extension services. The Women’s Empowerment in Agriculture运动 (WEA) is a key component of this effort, with beneficiaries from which women may benefit. The Growth and Transformation Plan II (GTP II) also has the target of increasing the percentage of rural women farmers who are benefiting from extension services to 40 percent by 2015. In 2013, about 68 percent of rural female-headed households were reached through the agricultural extension services. Though women make up more than 40 percent of the agricultural labor force and head approximately 25 percent of all farms in Ethiopia, they are still underrepresented among beneficiaries of these services, especially men.

On average male-headed households own 2.2 hectares of land compared to 1.7 hectares for female-

## Underrepresentation of women in High skill jobs and in managerial positions:
Close to 80 percent of women are unskilled (as opposed to 25 percent for males). The proportion of females who work as production line workers or department heads is less than 4 percent, whereas that for males is about 13 percent. Women make up only 20 percent of science and technology graduates in Ethiopia, compared with 80 percent of men. Only 30 percent of information and communication technologies (ICT) technicians. Women’s share of the most highly qualified professions, senior positions and management is estimated at 30percent.

over economic shocks, prolonged periods of existence in (small) living quarters, and reduced access to water facilities because of lack of and supply networks that are often crucial exacerbate cases of intimate partner violence in the country. Lessons learned from Phase I (DRSLP) and DRSLP II/III[7] of the project, data collected through key informant interviews, consultations with gender focal points in the relevant Ministry of Agriculture, and focus group discussions with community members indicate that women for this project in selected project regions indicate that pastoral and agro-pastoral women in Ethiopia are doubly marginalized; by virtue of their gender and through their migratory lifestyles making access to services and resources difficult. In addition, they are also doubly burdened as they continue to provide most of the manual labour in agro pastoral communities. For example, while men migrate for long periods of time to earn a living, women remain behind to take care of families during prolonged periods of drought and with very limited resources to support their families. Due to their socio-cultural gender norms, they are forced to walk for long distances in search of water and firewood. This is an additional burden on them as it takes away from the time available to engage in small income earning businesses (IGAD, 2016).

Gender disaggregated information on roles and access to assets and productive inputs by men and women farmers during drought periods are also very difficult. However, consultations and research indicate that pastoral women face considerable barriers in accessing land and credit. Where land is owned by female heads of households, the parcels of land are much smaller than those jointly owned by male heads of households. In addition to this, women have limited access at community level, patrilineal system of land ownership and inheritance based on customary laws and practices override the established laws, institutions and legal frameworks on land ownership such that preferences for men in terms of land allocation and inheritance are common. Women also have no access to credit in formal microfinance institutions and rely on traditional saving schemes to operate small businesses that are not economically viable. They are also often excluded from vital information and decision-making processes at community level, which is critical to climate change resilient agricultural practices due to their low literacy levels and limited participation in decision making in community level institutions. This in turn raises their vulnerabilities to shocks related to climate change compared to men (IGAD 2015; UN Women, 2014).

The major beneficiaries of the proposed project will be women-headed pastoralist communities with 30% or 50% being women-based on government commitment and number of households reached, and their local counterparts in 30 districts across 4 administrative regions in Ethiopia. The primary beneficiaries of the proposed project interventions are expected to number about 2,267,736 people (about 441,198 households). These beneficiaries will be selected individuals, households


---
**Page 203**
---

(male headed and female headed households), and communities residing in agro pastoral areas in four Lokrums regions. They include poor pastoralists (cows), cattle, sheep, and their owners) and agro pastoralists on targeted rangelands; those out option of pastoralist earlier investment in taking up alternative forms of livelihood; and the populations in the selected woredas and kebeles.

Specifically, this project targets to build climate resilience for food and livelihoods among agropastoral communities and mainstream gender along the project components, thereby reducing the gender gap in access to resources and services. The project will be implemented over a period of 3 years (2019-2022). The project outcomes is increase income from agro pastoral value chains with increased per capita income among women beneficiaries and creation of jobs for women and youth. Additionally, the project targets to improve access to water resources, land tenure security, and climate resilience through improved and digital financial services tailored to meet the needs of the two marginalized groups, biofortified foods to women (in male headed and female headed households) and men within their households, participation in decision making processes at all levels, and increased income from agro pastoral value chains for women led and youth led cooperatives. Furthermore, the project will also contribute towards gender equality by increasing women's access to resources and their representation in the leadership and membership of grassroots institutions and organizations.

Therefore, the current project aims to mainstream gender in all components as well as address specific issues through targeted activities as outlined in the GAP. The project seeks to empower women both within the project areas and in program implementation and aims to achieve 30% or more of women representation at leadership positions in all project components. There will be a role for women in management and training activities in line with GoE regulations. The GAP activities are supposed to address gender gaps and barriers that contribute to negative affect women's ability to do the gender gap and achieve economic empowerment for women and youth. A gender expert within the PUI will ensure that the project is implemented in a way that addresses the gender gap and achieves economic empowerment for women and youth.

Identification of specific gender-based risks and mitigation measures to address identified gender gaps, inequalities, or discriminatory practices in the regions targeted for program implementation in Ethiopia.
1) Project activities that are dominated by men; for example as design of infrastructure like boreholes, roads and rehabilitation of such structures can be found to limit the participation of women in these activities. This will be minimized through training of women in public speaking and making sure that at least 30% of representation in these forums are women with again equal representation in leadership positions.
2) that there is a lack of female representation in decision-making bodies; this can be represented in all forums across the project components (water committees, sustainable management of agro pastoral lands, climate smart villages with demonstration sites, access to digital technology on climate resilience and so forth). This will be addressed by ensuring that women's participation with norms will involve transforming the mind-sets of both men and women and their traditional and customary leaders especially through gender transformative activities like community


---
**Page 204**
---

### 3) Markets for agro pastoral value chains especially livestock can have the tendency to be dominated by men. This could limit women’s power of women groups in respect to prices of livestock and other agro pastoral value chain products. These challenges can be addressed through capacity building in public speaking for women, having digital platforms packaged and simplified to meet the market needs of women especially previously provided with training on how to use these platforms and also by involving the project region.

### 4) Other challenges related to this project could arise due to time poverty constraints based on women’s heavy workload. Alternative technologies like renewable energy – solar and biogaseter systems will be made available to women to reduce their time poverty constraints imposed on them by their productive and reproductive roles. Access to biogaseter systems will also help in reducing the amount of firewood used for cooking of homesteads and villages so that women spend less time travelling to fetch water. For water, adequacy during dry the season and accessibility through shorter distance travelled should be facilitated. This can be achieved by providing boreholes and participation in reseeding programs and in land rehabilitation programmes.

### 5) Women have low literacy levels and therefore could face challenges in understanding livestock value chains and other agro pastoral value chains. The project should support simplified and packaged crop production technologies and extension service training manuals and modules targeting low literacy levels of women to enhance food security and income generation from the livestock and other agro pastoral value chains. These materials should be developed and pre-tested in the local languages to meet the needs of women. Training should be conducted near villages and homesteads where women are comfortable to avoid the fear of traveling for long distances. This will also help in reducing the time poverty constraints which limit women’s mobility to faraway places without permission from the male heads of households and due to their heavy workload. Training periods of time and dates of training should be decided upon based on consultations between women and education extension service providers.

### 6) Based on intra household control of valuable value chains by men and control of income from these value chains, it is advisable to invest in women only value chains. In the agro pastoral communities, it is advisable to invest in women only value chains targeting women to make sure that women have the opportunity to earn income from value chains which are not based on gender norms or patriarchal practices. Such value chains include poultry and rabbit rearing and other activities termed as small stock value chains. Studies have shown that when a certain value chain becomes profitable, there is the possibility of control by men. Therefore, the project implementers at the community level


---
**Page 205**
---

should put in place safeguards to ensure that markets, and market prices and products from women only value chains are not taken over by men. Such safeguard could include women organizations forming for entity, sharing and joint keeping and marketing their products as a group and thereafter sharing the income. Processing plants for produce from women only value chains are more secure from male dominance if they are operated as women's cooperatives or associations as economic entities.

7) Women are not homogeneous, and care must be taken not to assume that the interests, needs and concerns of women are the same. Such an assumption could result in biases in identifying target groups for project activities. For example, the project activities are women from male headed households and female headed households like younger women. Women from female headed households like widows and single mothers should be involved in all project activities targeting women using a quota system of representation to make sure that all gender and age groups of women are represented throughout the project activities through representation at all planned activities across the project components.

8) Harmful traditional and customary practices can result in low participation of women in the project. Community mobilization to create awareness against such practices should be encouraged through gender transformative action plans for example through community wide meetings, drama, radio, television, posters, leaflets, etc. Use of capacity building materials simplified and packaged to target different local communities groups on behaviour change and use of gender sensitive and development behaviour change communication strategies to promote women and girls empowerment.

9) There could be gender biased implementation of project components by field staff if they are not trained on gender mainstreaming into the project cycle activities. The gender expert in the PSC or gender expert and coordinator should build the capacity of field staff and gender focal points at the local level so that gender is mainstreamed into the project activities to achieve empowerment for women and girls in the gender gaps identified in agro pastoral communities.
